# Daniel

# ANNOTATIONS ON DANIEL

## THE ARGUMENT

The book of Daniel is historical, to the seventh chapter; and prophetical in the rest. In the history of things done, we have singular monuments of the rare piety of Daniel, and his three companions of the same nation, about the same age, and in the same condition; who all in their youth were carried captives to Babylon, in the first captivity by Nebuchadnezzar, (not counting upon the captivity of Manasseh,) which was in the fourth year of Jehoiakim King of Judah, in the beginning of it: where at the first Daniel and his fellows, being instructed and trained up in learning for three years together, without intermission, were afterwards for their skill and dexterity advanced to honorable employments in that empire. God so took them into his particular care and charge, that their human instructions and learning became useful to them; being made subordinate, and subserving to divine gifts and graces, which he further bestowed on them. For they had the Spirit of sanctification, zeal for God's glory, firm faith, and invincible, though tried by fiery afflictions, and cruel decrees, inflicted on Daniel's three companions in their youth, and upon Daniel himself in his old age. Moreover, Daniel had the gift of prophecy. The dream which the King had forgotten, and which none of his wisemen could tell him, Daniel recalls to his memory, and gives the true interpretation thereof: whereupon being promoted to high dignity, he prefers his faithful companions; Providence so ordering affairs, that the Church in her sufferings and captivity in Babylon, should not want noble patrons for her comfort, countenance, and maintenance. He was famous for prayer and sanctity; as appears, Ezek. 14.14, 20. And, in the eleventh year of Jehoiachin's captivity, so singular for wisdom, that by a common proverb he was set a pattern thereof in Babylon; where Ezekiel also then prophesied: who taunts the King of Tyrus, (who thought himself so wise above others) with this mock, saying, Ezek. 28.3. *Behold, thou art wiser then Daniel.*

In the prophetical part of this book, Daniel's wisdom does clearly shine forth, in showing when the mystery of man's redemption should be wrought; and ceremonies, and legal sacrifices should cease, and be abolished, to give way to the service of God in spirit and truth; namely, when the Messiah was slain. Now to work sure confidence in the expectation of this singular good, Daniel has incomparable visions concerning the four great monarchies of the world, until the coming of Christ's kingdom; which was to be erected upon the ruins of the former, and to rule afterwards to the world's end: By which visions the Prophet shows, that the Jewish Church must be in a suffering condition; especially under the feet of the image, the divided kingdom of the Greeks, into north and south, Syria and Egypt, under the Seleucidæ and Lagidæ, successors to Alexander the Great; who exercised many outrages against the Jews: but above all, Antiochus Epiphanes was the most cruel, subtle, and pestilent persecutor, that ever the Jewish Church had. He hated piety, and was void of humanity, being maliciously set against Judah's religion; chiefly aiming to root out the worship of the true God, and to blot every impression and motion of piety out of men's hearts, against those former enemies, and this last, but worst of all. Daniel comforts, and quickens the languishing saints, by foretelling the singular history of things to be done in the Church, even till the coming of Christ the Messiah: and not only by predictions, and premonitions, would he make those heavy afflictions, which were to come, more tolerable: but also he tells the time precisely when the Messiah, by his death, should purchase for his Church spiritual deliverance, & and set up his spiritual, and everlasting kingdom, by causing and meriting salvation and eternal felicity to all both Jews & Gentiles which were of the election of grace. Foretelling likewise the final & fatal judgment of God to be executed by the Romans upon the Jewish nation, for denying the Holy One of Israel, and betraying the Lord of life to be killed by the Roman power. In the reading whereof, our Savior requires special care of understanding, Mat. 24.15. Wherefore Daniel's book seems to some, to contain the history of six hundred years, beginning with Babel's first year under Nebuchadnezzar; and reaching thence to Jerusalem's fall; the Romans thus; seventy years Babel's captivity lasted: thence seven seventies to the death of the Messiah: thence forty to the destruction of Jerusalem by the abomination of desolation, or abominable army of Roman infidels, working (by the just judgment of God) the desolation of the Jews, city, and country. These three sums; first, seventy in Babylon: secondly, seven seventies, that is, four hundred and ninety till Christ his death: Lastly, forty thence, to Jerusalem's destruction, make up six hundred years, the whole time and limits of Daniel's book. Yet others making Daniel's seventies, Chap. 9. to begin, not at Cyrus his proclamation for the Jews' return, when Jeremiah's seventy ended, 2 Chron. 36.21, 22. Ezra, Chap. 1. but at Artaxerxes his commission to Nehemiah, for to restore, and to build Jerusalem, (the walls and city) Dan. 9.25. and counting eighty-five years to intercur between those two dates, viz. the seven last of Cyrus; seven of Cambyses; seven months of Artaxerxes, or Smerdis Magus, thirty of Darius Hystaspis: one and twenty of Xerxes; and twenty of Artaxerxes, (for in his twentieth year Nehemiah had his Commission) they do so increase the number to be six hundred eighty-five years. This uncertainty, and others hereunto belonging, by reason of their opinions about the beginning of Daniel’s seventies, do arise from the uncertainty of Human Histories, upon which the reckonings of these times do depend. Whereof see more on Dan. 9.24,25,26,27. Daniel lived at the end of the Babylonish Captivity; yet returned not with the rest; but stayed behind: not so much by reason of his old age, as for his employments in the King’s affairs, and useful there to procure the good of his Nation, and to promote their Cause.

## CHAPTER 1

**Vers. 1.** *{In}* The beginning and end of these seventy years’ Captivity, is from Nebuchadnezzar’s first, unto the first of Darius: and Daniel was in Babylon the whole time, as is plain in this Chapter, ver. 1. & 21. Jeremy in the first of Nebuchadnezzar tells, that *Nations shall serve the King of Babylon seventy years*, Jer. 25.11. So the account must be from Babel’s first. See 2 Chron. 36.21.

*{third}* Heb. *three.* But the Cardinal number when it is governed of a Noun, here, has the signification of the Ordinal number.

*{year of the reign of Jehoiakim King of Judah}* In the end of this third year of Jehoiakim, Nebuchadnezzar began to reign: and in the fourth year of Jehoiakim, he overthrew Pharaoh’s Army at Carchemish by the river Euphrates, Jer. 46.2. And presently began his expedition in Babylon, and came up to Jerusalem in the same fourth year of Jehoiakim, which was still in the first of Nebuchadnezzar. See Jer. 25.1. 2 King. 24.1. Now lest the desolation of Jerusalem, spoken of, Jer. 25.11. should make the unacquainted stagger, we must know, that the land paid, or kept and enjoyed her Sabbaths, Lev. 26.34. and rested 70 years, 2 Chron. 36.21. from the first Captivity, to the end of Babel’s reign. For in most exact truth, the desolation and rest was in part from the first battering of Jerusalem, and carriage of any from the Country. This third year of Jehoiakim was about 3400 from the Creation. Of this Jehoiakim’s death, we read, That *he was bound in chains to be carried to Babylon*, 2 Chron. 36.6. But dying on the way, he was buried as Jeremy prophesied, Jer. 21.19. & 36.30. though the common phrase of *sleeping with his Fathers* be afforded him, 2 King. 24.6.

*{Nebuchadnezzar}* 2 King. 24.1. 2 Chr. 36.6. the son of Nabopolasser. See *Annotations* on Ezek. 26.7.

*{King of Babylon}* See *Annotations* on Gen. 10.10. The Assyrians, Chaldeans, Persians, reigning as Monarchs successively, had in possession, and were styled Kings of this famous city. See Neh. 13.6.

*{and besieged it}* In the beginning of the fourth year of Jehoiakim; as was said.

**V. 2.** *{And the Lord gave, etc.}* So Deut. 32.27. Jer. 37.17. God, the Author of Nebuchadnezzar’s victory, *sold them into the hand, etc.* Judg. 3.8. & 4.2. Of Jehoiakim’s restitution, and subjection a while; and his rebellion after; and final overthrow last of all, see 2 King. 24.1,2. Jer. 25.1. 2 Chron. 36.5,6.

*{with part}* For part remained till the time of Jechoniah; and the last part, till the time of the last subversion of all, in the eleventh year of Zedekiah; which Nebuchadnezzar had most just cause of wrath against the Kingdom of Judah, for their double or treble rebellion against him. The Vessels of the Temple were of sundry sorts, and very many in number, Ezra 1.9,10,11. & 8.25,26,27. and yet part of them had been cut in pieces, at Jehoiachin’s captivity, 2 King. 24.13.

*{Shinar}* Read Gen. 10.10. & 11.2. which was a plain by Babylon; and is here taken for Babylon, where was the temple of their great god.

*{The house of his god}* As monuments of his triumphant victory; and of his thanksgiving to his god therefore. See 1 Sam. 31.9,10. This his god was the idol Bel; the chiefest Idol in account with the Assyrians, Persians, and Babylonians. Most think they understood by it the Sun. And hence was that Idolatry to the Sun, in Manasseh’s time, who had been in Babylon; and mentioned in the reign of Josiah, 2 King. 23.11. And hence also it is, that to the Fire, as a spark of the Sun, they gave Divine worship. Bel likely signified to them, what Baal did to the Jews, a Lord: from Bel and Nebo, their Idols mentioned, Esay 46.1. some of the Babylonian Kings would assume part of their names; as Belshazzar, Nebuchadnezzar: and the like name the Prince of the Eunuchs, by the commandment of the King, gave to Daniel, vers. 7. Chap. 5.12.

*{treasure house of his god}* Idolaters’ costs upon their gods. See Diodorus Siculus book 3. chap. 4. Belshazzar, in the sacrilegious profaning of them, had that handwriting, etc. Chap. 5.2, etc.

**V. 3.** *{Spake unto Ashpenaz}* In that third year of Jehoiakim, ver. 1. or beginning of the fourth. For Nebuchadnezzar’s first year comprehended part of the third year, and part of the fourth of Jehoiakim, Jer. 25.1. And in that year, and not in the eighth year of Jehoiakim; or in the seventh or eighth year of Nebuchadnezzar, as some would, (compare 2 King. 24.12. with Jer. 52.28.) was Daniel carried Captive, and this his commandment given to Ashpenaz.

*{Eunuchs}* Or, *Courtiers*; whereof many were Eunuchs: some not, Jer. 52.25. See Gen. 39.1. Esth. 4.4. & 6.2. & 7.9. Acts 8.27.

*{and of the King’s seed}* Heb. *of the seed of the Kingdom.* Esay foretold this, Chap. 39.7. not yet that Daniel and his Companions were Eunuchs in its strictest sense; as the mind of some is: nor yet certainly, though perhaps probably, of the seed Royal. The King’s purpose was, to keep them as hostages; and that he might show himself victorious, and also by their good entreaty, and learning his religion, they might favor rather him, than the Jews; and so also be able to serve him as pages of honor; and so from time to time to be advanced to offices in the Court, or Governors in their land. Moreover, by this means the Jews might be better kept in subjection, fearing otherwise to procure hurt to these Noblemen.

**V. 4.** *{But well favoured}* Not such as those, Lev. 21.17. Such rather as Absalom was, 2 Sam. 14.25. The King required three things; that they should be of noble birth, witty and capable of learning; and also of a strong and comely Nature and beauty; For *gratior est pulchro veniens è corpore virtus;* when there is *εἶδος ἄξιον τυραννίδος*. And all this; that they might do him the better service; and this he doing for his own commodities, makes it the less to serve for the praises of his liberality: yet in this he is worthy of praise, that he esteemed learning; and knew that it was a necessary mean to govern by.

*{and skilful in all wisdom}* In the principles of it: and according to the capacity of their years. And much towardness by many signs may appear in young and tender years; as in Athanasius, and others.

*{to stand, etc.}* As 2 Chron. 10.6. 1 King. 10.9.

*{in the King’s palace}* Ver. 19. before the king.

*{teach the learning, and the tongue of the Chaldeans}* That they might forget their own religion and country fashions, to serve him the better to his purpose: yet it is not to be thought that Daniel did learn any knowledge that was not godly: unless it were so far forth as whereby to be able to convince and condemn it. As Solomon would know *madness and folly*, Eccl. 1.17. as the Fathers would know the errors of philosophers, and tenets of Heretics, to be more able to confute them. In all points, Daniel refused the abuse of things, and superstition; insomuch that he would not eat the meat which the King appointed him; But was content to learn the knowledge of Natural, Moral, and Rational things, and Sciences; and the Chaldean tongue, which differed from the Hebrew, and was fittest to be spoken in the Chaldean Court, and in the King’s presence. Thus, *Moses was learned in all the wisdom of the Egyptians*, Act. 7.22.

**V. 5.** *{daily provision of the King’s meat}* Haply that they might be drawn to eat meats forbidden in the law.

*{the wine which he drank}* Heb. *the wine of his drink.* Such as he had at his own Table: Such was the King’s Humanity, and royal regard of his conquered Captives.

*{three years}* To the intent that in this time, though but short, they might learn both the manner of the Chaldeans, and also their learning and language.

*{before the King}* As well to serve at the Table, as in other offices of greater employment. Less care have we to fit ourselves to stand before the King of Heaven, to serve him in our several callings and offices.

**V. 6.** *{Now among these}* There were more besides, vers. 10.

*{of Judah}* Most likely of that Tribe.

*{Daniel}* So speaking of himself in the third person; and so often do the Prophets; as Esay 1.1. Jer. 1.1. Ezek. 1.3. yet otherwhiles, in his own person; as Chap. 8.1. and 9.2. for that no doubt can be made, but that he, and no other, is the penman of this book. See Chap. 10.12.

**V. 7.** *{Prince of the Eunuchs}* So ver. 8,9,10,11,18. This man is not named: and has Melzar under him, ver. 11. and seems not to be Ashpenaz, who is called the master of the Eunuchs, vers. 3. nor Arioch, who is called the Captain of the King’s guard, Chap. 2.14,24,25.

*{Gave names}* By the commandment of the king, Dan. 5.12. as Pharaoh did, Gen. 41.45. and Pharaoh Necho, 2 King. 23.34. and this Nebuchadnezzar, 2 King. 24.17. This was haply to show his absolute power, and right of dominion over them: as Adam gave names, Gen. 2.19,20. and to honor haply his idols of Chaldea, or the children by the idols’ names, Chap. 4.8. the principal whereof were Bel and Nebo, Esay 46.1. and to cancel likely in them the memory of their birth, country, and religion, and the knowledge of the true God, whose name they bore, *El*, and *Jah*: for the Jews usually gave their children names which might put them in mind of some part or point of Religion; or upon some extraordinary occasion changed the name; as Esau was called Edom, and Gideon Jerubbaal. God himself in favor changed the names of Abram, Sarai, and Jacob; and our Savior did so to Simon, and to the sons of Zebedee, Mar. 3.16,17.

*{Belteshazzar}* After the name of his God, Chap. 4.8. signifying, Bel keeps treasure, or keeping Bel’s treasures: but Chap. 10.1. the name is altered by displacing only a letter, and so making the name to signify, He blasts out a fire upon the enemy; even the Chaldean. It differs somewhat from the name of King Belshazzar, Chap. 5.1.

**V. 8.** *{Daniel}* Being followed herein by his three companions, as appears.

*{Purposed}* Such was his constancy, in piety, and resolution against sin, Heb. 12.4. Act. 11.23.

*{defile himself with the portion of the King’s meat}* The Babylonians did eat many things forbidden the Jews, Lev. 11. Deut. 14. and meats allowed, as wine here, yet became impure to the Jews in certain cases. And that case spoken of, 1 Cor. 8.1,4. (whereof see more, Act. 15.20. Rom. 14.1, etc. 1 Cor. 10.25,27,28,29.) seems to be in practice amongst the Babylonians, Dan. 5.4. Daniel brings this in, not so much to show his sobriety, temperance, and abstinence, from meats; as to show how God, from the beginning, assisted him with his Spirit, and at length called him to be a prophet.

*{he requested}* In his own and his companions’ names, vers. 10. He did it, presently without delay, and humbly, and modestly, for providing for the safety of his conscience, and theirs.

**V. 9.** *{Favour}* Heb. *gave Daniel into favour.* The effect of Solomon’s prayer, 1 King. 8.50. God made the Prince of the Eunuchs to show him favor and mercy, by granting him what he required, in that manner as is after set down: Otherwise he might have taken occasion hereby of displeasure, and anger at Daniel, as despising this the king’s bounty, and condemning his meats as unclean. See Gen. 39.4,21. Psal. 106.46. Prov. 16.7.

**V. 10.** *{I fear my Lord the King}* He supposed that they did this for their religion, which was contrary to the Babylonians, yet herein he would bear with them, if they could free him of his fear.

*{worse}* Heb. *sadder,* Gen. 49.9. Prov. 17.22.

*{your sort}* Or, *term,* or, *continuance,* vers. 5,6. or of your age and condition.

*{endanger my head}* My life, through his just wrath, upon my disobedience, and unfaithfulness.

**V. 11.** *{Melzar}* Or, *the steward.* It seems, it may be some name of office, as governor of the pages. Daniel discerning a willingness of connivance in the Prince of the Eunuchs, so he might be saved harmless in his fear: now he addresses himself to this under-officer. Thus wisely and diligently he uses all lawful means, and constantly wades through all difficulties, for the keeping of his conscience clear, and undefiled.

**V. 12.** *{Prove thy servants}* He moves for them also; and that earnestly and humbly, with all complements of civility.

*{ten days}* Meaning that within this space he might have the trial: and this he spoke, being moved by the Spirit of God, in confidence of his blessing upon them.

*{pulse}* Heb. *of pulse;* Not that it was a thing abominable to eat dainty meats, and to drink wine, as before, and after, they did, Dan. 3. So it were according to the rules of their Levitical Law. But if they should have been thereby won to the king, and have refused their own Religion, breaking the Levitical laws for meats, Lev. 11. Deut. 14. that meat & drink then had been accursed. Pulse and water, here mentioned; that the officer might see their confidence the more, and have the clearer trial, and better proof and experience of their saying, and the thing itself.

*{to eat}* Heb. *that we may eat,* etc.

**V. 13.** *{and as thou seest}* The motion fair, and not without the officer’s profit, and grounded upon a comfortable assurance of God’s blessing.

**V. 15.** *{Their countenances appeared fairer}* Not lean; but the more fresh and fair. Their bare feeding, and that also of Moses, when he fled from the Court of Egypt, declares that we must live in such sobriety, as God does call us unto; seeing he will make it more profitable unto us, than all dainties; for his blessing only suffices, Exod. 23.25. Deut. 8.3. Mat. 4.4.

**V. 16.** *{thus Melzar}* It suits with his profit: He took the king’s daily provision to himself, and his own family.

**V. 17.** *{in all learning and wisdom}* Meaning in liberal sciences, and natural knowledge; and not in the magical arts, which are forbidden, Deut. 18.10,11.

*{Daniel had understanding}* Or, *He made Daniel understand*; God did it. He the Author of learning and wisdom, Jam. 1.5,17. John 3.27.

*{in all visions and dreams}* Not in vain, nor false visions, Jer. 23.16. & 14.14. Ezek. 12.24. & 13.7,16. Zech. 13.4. Nor in common and ordinary idle dreams; whereof, Eccl. 5.3,7. or lying dreams, Jer. 23.25,27. But in such visions and dreams, as were extraordinarily sent of God, foretelling things to come; as those to Pharaoh, Gen. 41. and to Nebuchadnezzar, Dan. 2. Or such, whereby he revealed his will to his Prophets, Numb. 12.6. And this gift of understanding was not in him, by way of habit, constantly abiding; as appears, Chap. 2. nor was the like in Elizæus, as appears, 2 King. 3.15. & 4.27. But by way of infusion; upon some special occasions, and for some special uses, as God pleased. However, by this it appears, that Daniel was a Prophet, and none of the other three; for by dreams and visions, God appeared to his Prophets, as we said, 2 Chron. 26.5. Dan. 10.1. And herein his Book excels; declaring the coming up of idolatry, chastisement, and fall of the Babylonians, Medes and Persians, Greeks, whole, and parted; and how Christ defends the holy Jews, until the redemption. Dreams also seem here to have reference to those, Chap. 2.1. and Chap. 4. vers. 5.

**V. 18.** *{the end of the days}* Of the three years, above mentioned, vers. 5. in the year of the world, 3406. or 3407. or thereabout.

**V. 19.** *{communed with them}* And others too, as appears in the words following. And by comparing the speeches and answers of all together, he found none like Daniel, and his three companions.

*{stood they before the King}* Vers. 4. to serve him in his palace.

**V. 20.** *{wisdom and understanding}* Heb. *wisdome of understanding.*

*{ten times better}* A certain number for an uncertain; so Gen. 31.7,41. Num. 14.22. Job 19.3. A very great difference and degree of excellency.

*{Magicians and Astrologers}* These names, at the first, were, and might be taken in good part; as the word *Tyrannus*, *Sophistor*, and the English word, Knave, is found in Chaucer, for a male child: though afterwards, through abuse of men and arts, the names grew infamous.

**V. 21.** *{And Daniel continued}* In esteem and dignity, in the highest offices and employments, under the kings of Babylon. This verse comes in here, as it were by way of a parenthesis.

*{even unto the first year of King Cyrus}* And after, Chap. 10.1. Of Cyrus, see Esay 44.28. & 45.1,4,13. & Dan. 5.30,31. & 6.28. and 10.1. That is, he was esteemed in Babylon, as a Prophet, in dignity and honor, as long as the Empire of Babylon stood, and afterward also: and yet Belshazzar, in his last year, seems ignorant of him, Chap. 5.13. with this first of Cyrus, the seventy years of captivity ended: Thus, Nebuchadnezzar, forty-five, almost, reigning with his Father, and alone: compare Jerem. 25.1. with 2 King. 25.27. Evilmerodach, two years, Neriglissorus, four years, Laborosarchodus, nine months; Belshazzar, 17 years; Darius Medus, one year. See Jer. 27.7.

## CHAPTER 2

**Vers. 1.** *{And in the second year of the reign of Nebuchadnezzar}* Heb. *and in the second yeare in the reign of Nebuchadnezzar.* And this seems to suit better with the truth of the sacred History. For this Dream and Exposition, was not in the second year of Nebuchadnezzar’s reign, and Daniel’s captivity. The things set down towards the end of the former Chapter, make against that; neither does anything force us to understand them by way of anticipation. And much less was it in the second year of his Monarchy; which for this cause, some would have to begin many years after, upon his conquest over the Ammonites, Moabites, and Egyptians. And least of all was it, in the second year of his Successor, and son of the same name; as divers forged heathen Historians frame the catalogue of the Babylonish kings. But rather, this fell out in the second year, after the three years were expired, wherein Daniel and his companions had been trained up, as is expressed, Chap. 1. vers. 5,18. And this was the sixth year of Nebuchadnezzar’s reign, and Daniel’s captivity, and in the ninth of Jehoiakim. In the second year then after Daniel’s Coronation, as it were, with wisdom, Chap. 1.19,20. was this dream, by Daniel, recalled to the king’s memory, and expounded. And this was some six or seven years, before Ezekiel began to prophesy, Ezek. 2. ver. 2,3. As Joseph in the second year, after his skill in dreams, expounded Pharaoh’s, compare Gen. 40.23. with 41.1. God so disposing the times alike; the Magicians alike; and so guiding, and advancing Joseph and Daniel alike. See *Annotations* on Chap. 2.13.

*{Nebuchadnezzar dreamed}* And thus God has vouchsafed dreams, sometimes to heathen and idolaters, for the good benefit of his own servants; as to Pharaoh, Abimelech, and Laban, Gen. 12. and 20. and 31. This dream was about the year of the world, 3404.

*{dreams}* Not that he had many dreams, but that many matters were contained in this one dream; because in that one image which he saw, there were divers metals, which were types, and representations of divers monarchies, one succeeding another.

*{was troubled}* Vers. 3. See Psal. 77.3. Because it was so rare and strange a dream; even while his sleep was yet on him, was he troubled. See Gen. 40.6.

*{brake from him}* Or, *fled from him*, that he could sleep no more by reason of the disquietness of his spirit.

**V. 2.** *{Magicians}* Chap. 5.7. Gen. 41.8. Ezek. 21.21. More names, of several sorts follow, of men of curious arts, in themselves unlawful and devilish; and not easily, with certainty now distinguishable. See the many books of like artificers, Act. 19.19. Such were those in Egypt, Exod. 7.11. whereof Jannes and Jambres were chief, 2 Tim. 3.8. Such was Simon, Act. 8.9,10,11. Bar-jesus or Elymas, Act. 13.6,8. See 2 Chron. 33.6. such are forbidden, Deut. 18.10,11,12.

*{Chaldeans}* Or, *diviners.* This nation of all others was given to suchlike arts and studies: and all these astrologers, and sorcerers called themselves, by this name of honor, and from the name of this country; as though all wisdom and knowledge of the country depended upon them; and that all other countries were void of the same, vers. 4,5,10.

**V. 4.** *{In Syriack}* Or *the Babylonian tongue*. So Esay 36.11. yet now the Syriack and Chaldee tongue do much differ, and did in the days of our Savior, who used the Syriack tongue, as the Jews did in his time. This appears by many Syriack words in the New Testament, which yet are called Hebrew, because that was the ancient and mother language, and the Syriack but a corruption of it. See Joh. 5.2. & 19.13,17,20. Apoc. 9.11. & 16.16. Act. 21.40. & 22.2. Syria sometimes comprehended Babylon in it, Mela. 6.1. c. 11. Pliny 6.5. c. 12. Hitherto Daniel writ in the Hebrew tongue. Now he begins in the Syriack or Chaldee tongue; and so continues while the visions are general, and cause the Jews no danger; and this is to the eighth Chapter, using the Chaldee, which then was best known, and general over the East; But when the oppressors are named, and the Jews are plainly described the people whom God defends; then the eighth Chapter and all after, he writes again in Hebrew, and has a commandment to keep close the plain exposition, in Chap. 12.4. The additions to Daniel, which the Trent Council has made canonical, brings in a third language, as written originally in the Greek tongue.

*{O King}* Chap. 3.9. & 5.10. and 6.6. a speech of affection, and of honor natural, and civil, used also by the people of God, chap. 6.21. 1 King. 1.31. Neh. 2.3.

*{live for ever}* Forever, both in Hebrew and Chaldee, signifies often a long time only; as Exod. 21.6. Gen. 13.15. Psal. 89.1. See *Annotations* on Exod. 12.24. Here they express our wish at full, and desire his longest life; yet cannot mean his everlasting life in this world. Neither may we exclude them from thoughts of wishing him an everlasting happy life after this. So many heathens, specially of greatest fame for learning and wisdom, having writ so much of the immortality of the soul; and of an everlasting life in joy, or pains, after this life ended.

*{and we will shew}* It was more than they could do, Chap. 4.7. and 2.27. Gen. 41.8. But they promise this, to *gain the time*, etc. as the King says, vers. 8.

**V. 5.** *{the thing is gone from me}* The Chaldee signifies both word and thing; as likewise the Hebrew does. From me, not without God’s special providence; minding thereby the honor and advancement of Daniel, for the good of his Church and people in their captivity.

*{ye shall be}* This, though cruel and unjust in itself, as they truly plead, vers. 10,11. yet is a just reward of their arrogancy which vaunted of themselves, that they had the knowledge of all things, that they should be proved fools, and that to their perpetual shame and confusion.

*{cut in pieces}* Chap. 3.29. Chaldee made pieces.

*{and your houses shall be made a dunghill}* Ezra 6.11.

**V. 6.** *{Rewards}* Or, *fee,* Chap. 5.17. The word properly signifies a very great reward, such as prodigals who make no account of their wealth, use to give.

*{therefore shew me the dream}* So again, ver. 9. still pressing them to this point, and that tyrannically.

**V. 7.** *{Again}* Ver. 4.

**V. 8.** *{would gain the time}* Chaldee, *buy.* See, Ephes. 5.16. men in danger will give anything to gain time, for their own advantage, to delay, and protract it, in hope to find out some occasion to escape, in the meantime. So here, in hope that the king might yet rub up his memory, and better remember his dream, or turning his thoughts some other way, might as well forget his decree, as his dream; or at least, that in time his anger might assuage, and he mitigate, if not revoke, that rash judgment, and bloody decree.

**V. 9.** *{one decree for you}* That is, my sentence shall stand firm, and be brought to effect, Esth. 4.11. One Law, once passed, as Psalm 89.35. and never to be revoked; nor second to be made, 1 Sam. 26.8. had been the more tyrannical cruelty in the king; had not they bragged as much of their magical profession: though their profession in general deserved death by the Law of God, Lev. 20.27. Deut. 18.10,11.

*{for ye have prepared lying and corrupt words to speak before me}* I know that you, because you cannot tell me my dream, have prepared yourselves to give me a false interpretation, accommodated to the present time, and pleasing in the event.

*{and I shall know}* By your telling the dream that is past, I shall know your skill and ability to tell the interpretation thereof, though it be in foretelling things to come. And surely, if you can know the future, much more should you know that which is past.

**V. 11.** *{a rare thing}* Chald. *precious.* For precious things are rare.

*{whose dwelling is not with flesh}* That pure spirits, free from all mixture and infirmity of the flesh, have no conversation, nor ordinary communication with men, whose souls lodge in bodies, and are tied to senses: hence it comes to pass, that that divine light they receive, is very small, and also depends wholly on the good pleasure of God. Thus they acknowledge a Deity, and God’s omniscience; and that proper to him: as what man does not all this? Though they err in the point of more gods than one. And thus they make way to Daniel’s honor, and favor and interest in the true God.

**V. 12.** *{for this cause}* The more also for their last answer, ver. 10,11. whereby they charged the king with tyranny, requiring of them things never heard of, and surpassing the reach of all human nature.

*{very furious}* Prov. 27.4. and 21.24. and 19.12. and 16.14. and 20.2. This, as it is dangerous in the person of a king, so it much misbeseems him, Eccl. 7.9. Prov. 14.17. and 16.32. See Mat. 21.5. Prov. 20.28. Esay 16.5.

**V. 13.** *{And the decree went forth}* The king pronounced it, and he caused it to be proclaimed, *a decree written in blood,* Esay 10.1.

*{that the wise men should be slain}* Those that were there present: or else, all the wise men were to be slain. The king deeming and dooming them as impostors; and continuing in his rage, through his despair of knowing his dream, and the interpretation of it.

*{And they sought Daniel}* Absent belike at that time, or slighted as being novices; and the others old and experienced Magicians; or kept off, by reason of their envy toward them, Prov. 27.4. This envy threw Daniel afterwards into the den of lions. However, this shows that these things fell out after the three years of their training up, Chap. 1.5. and that Daniel and his fellows were not with the rest before the king, vers. 2. as not affecting to be of that number, nor meddling with such arts, nor coveting such rewards, and honors, vers. 12.

**V. 14.** *{answered with counsel}* Chald. *returned:* or, *restored counsel and wisdom*; to wit, why the decree for killing all the Sages should be stayed.

*{to Arioch}* Gen. 37.36. and 40.3. 2 King. 25.8. Haply being a stranger he had not attained to a free access to the king. But necessity drives him to it, ver. 16.

*{Captain of the King’s guard}* Or, *chief Marshal.* Chald. *chief of the executioners,* or *slaughter-men;* as Gen. 37.36.

**V. 15.** *{so hasty?}* Why may it not be deferred, if hope may be given of satisfying the king’s mind about his dream?

*{Then Arioch made}* Daniel so not hearing of it before. See *Annotations* on vers. 13. Arioch also haply made the motion of Daniel’s going to the king about it: which Daniel puts in execution in the next verse.

**V. 16.** *{give him time}* Not to study or meditate by discourse of reason, or by help of books, or rules of art, nor to deliberate; but to call upon God with fervor and perseverance: for in God he placed all his hope and help. Neither does the king deny this space of time to Daniel, upon the hope he had in his promise; though he would not use delay with the Magicians, who put him in despair, ver. 8.

**V. 17.** *{his house}* In Babylon, where it seems he remained during the times of the Babylonian monarchs, Chap. 1.21. And his house likely not far from the king’s palace, if this may stand with his ignorance of the king’s decree, ver. 15. However, his house was the fittest place for his purpose of prayer.

*{known to Hananiah}* For their help in prayer, and so he acknowledges, and owns them in the business, vers. 23. & 36.

**V. 18.** *{Desire mercies}* Prayer to God is the means of deliverance in greatest dangers.

*{of the God}* Chald. *from before God,* or, *the face of God;* as ver. 15. *from before the face of the King.*

*{that Daniel}* Or, *that they should not destroy Daniel,* etc. This was the immediate end which they aimed at.

**V. 19.** *{in a night vision}* Whether to him sleeping, or waking, it is uncertain. As also the particular manner how it was revealed: and whether the next night after, or some other short time; for the king’s hasty wrath would admit no long delay.

*{blessed the God of Heaven}* Did give thanks, and praise to him. God in another sense does bless us. And we in another sense do bless others, Gen. 24.60. Josh. 22.6.

**V. 20.** *{Blessed be the name of God for ever and ever}* Psal. 113.2. and 115.18.

*{for wisdom and might are his}* Job 12.13,16. Prov. 2.6. Jam. 1.5. 1 King. 3.9. See vers. 23. of this Chapter. Daniel did see this, in that God in wisdom did foresee, and in his might was able to effect this wondrous change of empires, as this vision did foreshow.

**V. 21.** *{and he changeth the times}* This seems to have special regard to the revolutions of states and empires, and to the other mutations of the affairs of the world; which was the subject of the king’s dream.

*{he removeth Kings}* Job 12.18. Gen. 14.

*{he giveth wisdom}* This has reference to the gift given to Daniel.

**V. 22.** *{he revealeth deep and secret things}* As now to Daniel; and so to Joseph, Gen. 37. & Chap. 41. Job 12.22.

*{and the light dwelleth with him}* He shows that man has neither wisdom nor knowledge, but very dark blindness and ignorance of himself: for it comes only of God, that man understands anything: He is Light, and dwells in Light, 1 Joh. 1.5. 1 Tim. 6.16. Psal. 139.12.

**V. 23.** *{I thank thee}* So Mat. 11.25.

*{O thou God of my Fathers}* To whom you made your promise, and who lived in your fear; whereby he excludes all other gods: and fastens his faith upon God’s free and gracious covenant, as the ground of this mercy, and his thanksgiving; not pretending to any worth of his own.

*{wisdom and might}* Valor and greatness of mind in actions; and wisdom in counsel, and in understanding of affairs, and skill to interpret the dream.

*{we desired of thee}* That is, he and his companions, vers. 17,18. see the benefit of conjoined prayers; and the modesty of God’s servants, who attribute the good things they receive from God, to other men’s prayers, as well as to their own.

*{made known to us}* Amos 3.7. Gen. 18.17. and 6.13. Psal. 25.14. Joh. 15.15.

**V. 24.** *{unto Arioch}* Not straight to the king; so full was he of modesty and humility; and so free from pride and arrogancy: and haply to stop him from present execution.

*{to destroy}* So 1 King. 2.25,29. 1 Sam. 22.17,18. Soldiers, and chief commanders used in such cases.

*{Destroy not the wise men}* See the mercy of God’s servants; Daniel sues to save the life of the wise men: not, that he favored their wicked profession; but in that he had a respect to equity and mercy: because the king proceeded according to his wicked affection and fury; and not considering at all, if their science were lawful or no.

**V. 25.** *{Then Arioch etc.}* Doubts nothing of Daniel’s truth, honesty and wisdom; but hastes to bring Daniel, and this welcome message to the king, the more to ingratiate himself into his favor: And herein ascribes to him more than in truth was, as courtiers use to do. For he sought not Daniel, unless it were to slay him, vers. 13. not found him; for Daniel offered himself unto him, vers. 24.

*{I have found}* Chald. *that I have found.* It seems then that Daniel was but yet a novice; as yet of no such name and fame, as afterwards. This was his first rising; this being but the second year after his three years of education ended.

*{Captives of Judah}* Chald. *children of the Captivity of Judah.*

**V. 26.** *{The King answered}* That is, *spoke*. For in the Chaldee, and Hebrew, and Greek also the word translated, *answered,* is very often used, when no question before is asked. So Job 3.2. Mat. 22.1. and 11.25. and 17.4. Luk. 14.3.

*{art thou able?}* Though he found him ten times better than the Astrologers, Chap. 1.20. yet, either that has reference to future times; or, in the throng of his mighty affairs, and sudden trouble of his spirit at this dream, he did not recollect himself to think upon Daniel: as neither Belshazzar did, Chap. 5.10,11.

**V. 27.** *{the secret}* Is a divine revelation; no natural dream. And therefore not within the power of man, to reveal, or interpret.

*{cannot the wise men}* No more than they could read the writing, Chap. 5.7,8. nor those Magicians in Egypt could do those miracles, Exod. 8.18,19. and Chap. 9. and 10. Daniel first shows the insufficiency of the wise men, before he shows God’s power.

**V. 28.** *{but there is a God in Heaven that revealeth}* He shows, that man by reason and art is not able to attain to God’s secrets; nor sorcerers by the help of their false gods: but the understanding only thereof must come of the true God. Whereby he smites the king with a certain fear and reverence of the only true God, of his wisdom, and power; to draw him from his false gods, that could not help his sorcerers in this their danger. And that he might be the more apt to receive the high mysteries that should be revealed to him. See Gen. 40.8. and 41.16.

*{maketh known}* Chald. *hath made known.*

*{in the latter days}* Or, *in the end of dayes;* as Gen. 49.1. Esay 2.2. Micah 4.1. Act. 2.17. that is, in time to come, after this, even to the Messiah; as is declared, vers. 29,45. See Jer. 23.20. Ezek. 38.8.

*{are these}* Of that same argument as your thoughts were before your dream, vers. 29,30.

**V. 29.** *{Thy thoughts}* Daniel from God tells him, not only his dream; but more also, even his waking thoughts before his dream.

*{came into}* Chald. *came up.* God sometime satisfies the curious desires of great men for the good of his Church.

**V. 30.** *{for any wisdom that I have}* He arrogates nothing to himself; but honors the king, in that for his sake God vouchsafes both the dream and interpretation; to insinuate the more into the king’s heart, a mind and affection to the true God. So before, vers. 29.

*{but for their sakes}* Meaning for the Jews’ sake also, and all the Church (which Nebuchadnezzar had then in his dominions, and power) was granted to Daniel, the revelation both of the dream, and the interpretation: or simply, for the sake that it might be made known to the king; for the word *their* is not in the original.

*{The thoughts of thy heart}* Vers. 29.

**V. 31.** *{sawest}* Chald. *wast seeing;* in a dream, vers. 1,2.

*{a great Image}* Hieroglyphically representing the various changes of kingly governments in the world, till the coming of Christ; which was from his time above six hundred years.

*{whose brightness}* See Chap. 4.36.

*{terrible}* Not so much to affright, as to astonish with admiration; as Luk. 7.16. & 1.65. Mat. 9.8. or as in the first verse of this Chapter.

**V. 33.** *{part of Iron, and part of clay}* Some parts of them of the one, and some parts of them of the other; and not the whole feet composed of a matter arising from a commixtion of iron and clay. See vers. 42.

**V. 34.** *{was cut out without hands}* See vers. 45. without art or industry; without engines and human helps. See Esay 45.9.

**V. 35.** *{like the chaff}* Such is the glory of all human kingdoms, of worldly men, and of all earthly felicity, Eccl. 1.2. Psal. 37.35,36. Job 20.6–8. Psal. 39.5. Such use Nebuchadnezzar should have made of this vision.

*{no place was found}* As Psal. 37.10,36. & 103.16.

*{became a great mountain}* Grew by degrees to a great mountain after it smote the image.

*{and filled the whole earth}* Exceeded all the other empires.

**V. 36.** *{This is the dream}* He appeals, as it were, to the king’s conscience for the truth hereof in all particulars. And therefore that he cannot doubt of the truth of the future interpretation.

*{we will tell}* Joins his companions with him in his modesty, and humility; and because of the help of their prayers: though the revelation was vouchsafed to him alone, vers. 19.

**V. 37.** *{Thou O King}* And his successors in the empire, Jer. 27.6,7. Ezek. 26.7. the Babylonian alone; not the Assyrian: much less must it be fetched so high, as from Nimrod, Gen. 10.9,10.

*{a King of Kings}* Ezra 7.12. as Deut. 10.17. and as Gen. 9.25. as Cant. 1.1. and as the Oracle was called *sanctum sanctorum,* the most holy place. Nebuchadnezzar had many kings feodary and tributary unto him, and under him: and captives with him in Babylon, 2 King. 25.28.

*{hath given thee}* And would do more: as this dream shows: for this was but in the fifth year of his reign. See Annotations on vers. 1. Chap. 4.25. & 5.21. Ezra 1.2. This donation of God should have prevented that pride of his spirit, Chap. 4.30. & Esay 14.12,13. which was like that of the Assyrian, Esay 10.8–16.

**V. 38.** *{And wheresoever}* In all your large empire, Chap. 4.12,21,22.

*{thou art this head of Gold}* He understands not only the person of Nebuchadnezzar, but the Chaldean Empire: called a *head,* because it was the first of these 4 monarchies. For the Assyrian Empire was past and extinct. And Nebuchadnezzar desired only to know what should be after him, vers. 29,30. And of gold, it is said to be in regard of its glory, riches, splendor, and magnificence in all greatness, Esay 13.19. & 14.11,12,13. Babylon is called *the golden City,* Esay 14.4. and monstrous was that Colossus of gold, made by the king, Chap. 3.1. It is likened to a lion, Chap. 7.4.

**V. 39.** *{arise another kingdom}* That of the Persians, and the Medes, begun by Cyrus the Persian, and Darius the Mede, Chap. 4.30,31. & 6.28. & 9.1,2. This is figured by the breast and arms of silver, vers. 32. and by the bear, chap. 7.5. and by the ram with two horns, Chap. 8.3. and that in regard of the conjunction of both these nations in one empire. See Esay 21.2. This though rich and splendid, yet not like that of the Babylonians, Dan. 7.5. and 8.3. & 11.2. and they worse touching ambition, cruelty, and all kind of vice; showing that the world should grow worse and worse, till it was restored by Christ.

*{Inferior to thee}* In peace, prosperity, and glory; not in length of duration, or extent of dominion, Paulus Orosius book 3. Chap. 8, etc.

*{third Kingdom of brass}* That which Alexander got by overcoming Darius Codomanus; represented by the belly and thighs of brass, vers. 32. And by the third beast, the leopard, Chap. 7.6. And also by the he-goat, Chap. 8.5. But with this difference, That in that representation of the he-goat, Chap. 8.21. The whole empire of Alexander the Great, and all his successors also in the Grecian Empire are understood, as the text in that chapter clears it. But here only, and Chap. 7.6. the empire in Alexander’s own person, and the power of Greeks with him, is understood: and all his successors in that Grecian Empire, are made the fourth kingdom here, represented by the legs of iron, and feet part of iron, and part of clay, vers. 33,40,41,42,43. and are also made the fourth beast, Chap. 7.7,8. See Chap. 11.3, etc. Alexander’s kingdom here is said to be of brass; Because he did nothing else but devour by force of arms, which were anciently made of brass tempered: alluding also herein to the state of this kingdom, that it was worse than the former; as brass is worse than silver. And it continued but for a very small time; only six years in Alexander’s person after the conquest of Darius.

*{rule over all the earth}* Over a great part thereof, so that he shall obtain the greatest empire of the world; called universal, by popular speech, Dan. 8.5. Luk. 2.1. So did Alexander the Great with the whole power of Greek states, which made him their king for the Persian War. Of the largeness of his empire, we may read Plin. lib. 4. cap. 10. Justin. lib. 12. Q. Curtius, l. 10.

**V. 40.** *{The fourth Kingdom shall be strong as Iron}* Signified by the fourth beast, Chap. 7.7,8. So understood of the Grecians jointly. By the two legs, and feet, part of iron, and part of clay, vers. 33. is understood by some, the Roman Empire; and that before Christ’s second coming, divided into the West and East Empire; the Latin and Greek Empire, the German and Constantinopolitan; whose arms are the spread eagle with two heads. Some apply it to the state of the Roman Empire before Christ’s birth. Both meet with such difficulties and impossibilities herein, as make them various, inconsistent, and unsound in their assertions: and the truth is, That the taking in the Romans here, is an error hurtful to Daniel’s book, and to all Christianity, and other stories, being inconsistent with them; and has no color of truth from Daniel. But the contrary; as appears, vers. 34,35. and vers. 44,45. See the *Annotations;* and Chap. 11. Some here will go so far as to have Antichrist included. But all that can be true therein is, that what things are spoken here, and Chap. 7.7,8. and 11.21,28,30,31,32,36–38. may be by way of analogy or type referred to Antichrist. And possibly in regard of a like revolution of things happening, St. Paul to the Thessalonians, and St. John in the Revelation, may allude hereunto. As that of Gog and Magog, Revel. 20.8. does to that in Ezek. 38. & 39. Chapters. We conceive, that in the first and proper sense, the successors of Alexander the Great are here understood, the Seleucidæ, and Lagidæ, reigning in Syria, and Egypt; who more sharply oppressed the Church than all others; insomuch, that they have gone about to do violence to the conscience, Ch. 7.7,8. & 8.9,10,24. & 11.4,5,21. And this kingdom is called of iron, a base metal, but very hard; a metal for war. And indeed this kingdom was cruel, and tyrannical, and bloody, specially to God’s people, the Jews. See Chap. 11. and the books of Maccabees. The condition of it is further described to be very variable; sometime powerful, and otherwhile very weak; a quality very agreeable to these two kingdoms of the Seleucidæ, and Lagidæ, Alexander’s successors, in regard of their originals, acts, and events.

*{And bruise}* Whom? even the Jews’ nation: but the wise Daniel hides that, which would cause them to be more hated of the heathen. Confer with these words, Chap. 7.7,8,23. and 8.24. and 11.22, etc.

**V. 41.** *{the feet and toes}* Insists more largely in this, and the two verses following, upon that in the 33 verse; as the thing most concerning the Jews, and their calamities by these oppressors: that so they might be foretold of them, and strengthened against them.

*{the Kingdom shall be divided}* Into the Seleucidæ that held Syria, and the Lagidæ that reigned in Egypt; called the kings of the North, and of the South, Chap. 11.5,6. and they had continual wars and discords among themselves.

**V. 42.** *{the toes}* The provinces and principalities annexed to these two kingdoms.

*{part of Iron}* He has regard to the variety of the state of the two kingdoms, and their annexed provinces and principalities, often raised up, and powerful; and often abased, and depressed one by the other; and afterwards by the Maccabeans. See Ezek. 38.21, etc. and in the end by the Romans.

*{partly strong}* In relation to Judah, in dealing against them. So unto Antiochus Epiphanes; or somewhat farther, ver. 40.

*{broken}* Or, *brittle.*

**V. 43.** *{with the seed of men}* Those kings of Syria and Egypt shall contract affinity, by giving one another their daughters in marriage: for Ptolomy Philadelphus gave his daughter Bernice to Antiochus Theos. Antiochus the Great, gave his daughter Cleopatra to Ptolomy, Epiphanes; etc. Chap. 11.6,17. 1 Mac. 10.51. and 11.9. whereby notwithstanding they cannot be joined in heart, and make a firm and constant union.

*{not cleave one to another}* Chald. *this with this.* Marriages made for bad, or by-ends, seldom or never thrive.

**V. 44.** *{and in the days of these Kings}* Chald. *their dayes;* or, *after the dayes of those Kings and kingdomes.* So Job 27.15. those that remain of him shall be buried in death; that is, after death: In the days of those last kings; when the kingdoms of the Seleucidæ, and the Ptolomies are fallen: or about the end of those kingdoms of Syria and Egypt. He interprets that, vers. 34,35.

*{a Kingdom}* Spiritual and heavenly, of the Messiah manifested in the flesh, Chap. 7.13,14,27. John the Baptist looked to this: and our Lord also, saying, *The Kingdome of heaven is come:* and so did St. Paul, 1 Tim. 1.17.

*{which shall never be destroyed}* Chap. 4.3,34. & 6.26. & 7.14,27. Mic. 4.7. Luk. 1.33. Esay 9.7. Psal. 145.13. His purpose is to show, that all the kingdoms of the world are transitory; and that the kingdom of Christ shall only remain forever.

*{kingdom}* Chald. *kingdome thereof.*

*{shall not be left}* As the former kingdoms, and monarchies were the Chaldean to the Persian; and the Persian to the Grecian: But shall ever remain and abide in Christ: so militant here, *as the gates of hell shall not prevail against it;* and after forever triumphant in heaven.

*{break in pieces}* For that the Son of God, Sovereign King of the world, is he that at all times has destroyed all empires, and powers contrary to his, Psal. 2.9. Isa. 60.12, etc. Zech. 11.8. 2 Cor. 10.5,6. who having made an end of subverting these four monarchies, in their several times, with the ruin of these last kingdoms of Syria and Egypt, in the beginning and rising of the Roman Monarchy, has established his universal kingdom, though amongst his greatest enemies. The empire of the Romans then cannot be understood and comprehended in these kingdoms. It being not broken to pieces, so that no place was found for it, by this stone, when it came, but rather began then, or most flourishing, and still remaining, in some degree, to this day.

**V. 45.** *{Stone}* Psal. 118.22,23. Mat. 21.42. 1 Pet. 2.4, etc. Esay 28.16. Act. 4.11. Zech. 3.9.

*{was cut out of the mountain}* Meaning Christ, who was born indeed of the Virgin Mary, but sent of God from the Mount of heaven. See Esay 51.1,2.

*{and it shall stand for ever}* This kingdom of Christ, at the beginning should be small, and without beauty to man’s judgment, baser than all those former metals; and should be set up and carried on by poor and base means, in man’s eyes, by the foolishness of preaching; but yet should at length grow (by the only power of God, without force and human art) to a great mountain, ver. 35. and fill the whole earth, Esay 49.23. & 60.12. And this kingdom which is not only referred to the Person of Christ, but also in its degree, to the whole body of his Church, and to every member thereof, shall be eternal: for the Spirit that is in them is life eternal, Rom. 8.10. See 1 Cor. 12.12. Ephes. 1.23.

*{without hands}* Or, *which was not in hand.* Things wrought without means do effect wonders: so does Christ, brought into the world, by the work and power of God, set up his kingdom. For the stone is the power of Christ, or Christ that chief cornerstone, weak and base in men’s eyes; but *a stone of stumbling, and rock of offense*, able to break in pieces all opposites, vers. 34,35. Isai. 8.14,15. And Christ did not, by any human art and authority, enter upon his kingdom, and the administration thereof, but by the sole will of the Father, and operation of the Spirit.

*{the iron}* That image, vers. 31,32,33. Wise Daniel again tells, first how the last shall be destroyed; and not how Nebuchadnezzar’s house first should fall. So vers. 34,35. So he deals in Chap. 11.11,12. Mat. 1.11. to distinguish him from Nebuchadnezzar’s false gods.

*{hereafter}* Chald. *after this:* vers. 28.

*{interpretation thereof sure}* Not feigned, to flatter the king; as this king discovered the Magicians would have done, ver. 9.

**V. 46.** *{worshipped Daniel}* The power that the opinion of a Deity, has in, and over the greatest monarch. He being ignorant of the true God, and his worship, and deeply struck with an admiration at Daniel, and apprehension of some divinity in him, Chap. 4.8,9. & 5.11,14. he was ready, as idolaters use to be, Act. 14.11. & 28.6. to yield himself, and to command others to offer a divine worship unto Daniel; a worship due indeed, and proper only to God, though he did not take Daniel to be that true God. But no doubt, Daniel withstood it, and turned the worship unto God; as may be gathered by Daniel’s piety, Chap. 1. vers. 8. and the king’s answer to Daniel, in the next words. So the Angel refused to be adored of John, bade him *worship God*, Rev. 22.9. See Act. 10.25,26. & 14.13,14,15. Yet some Papists here, are willing enough to justify both Nebuchadnezzar’s act, in giving this worship to Daniel, and Daniel’s act in receiving it (for, say they, here is not a word of his refusing it.) And all, thereby to make the better way for the justification of themselves, in the idolatrous worship they give to Saints and Images.

*{commanded}* Chald. *said.* If a king’s word be a command, then much more is God’s.

*{offer}* As Act. 14.11. More than Alexander the Great did to Jaddua the High Priest: Josephus of the Jewish Antiq. 6.11.

**V. 47.** *{The King answered unto Daniel}* Admonishing the king, most likely, of his error, and telling him of the true God, as formerly, vers. 27,28,30,45.

*{God is a God of gods}* A God, above the gods of his Magicians; the only true God. This confession was but a sudden motion. And so it was also in Pharaoh, Exod. 9.27,28. But his heart was not thoroughly touched; as appeared soon afterward.

*{a revealer of secrets}* Esay 41.22,23. Psal. 139.2.

**V. 48.** *{made Daniel a great man}* So Joseph advanced, Gen. 41. This was about two years before the captivity of Jeconiah: an encouragement for the faithful, to submit to Nebuchadnezzar, and to go willingly to Babylon; their own nobles being so advanced there. Thus was Mordecai advanced, after in the time of the Persian Monarchy, for the safety and deliverance of the Jews, Esth. 8.2.

*{great gifts}* Not that the Prophet was desirous of gifts, or honor, Chap. 5.17. but because, by this means, he might relieve his poor brethren, which were grievously oppressed, in this their captivity: and also he received them, lest he should offend this cruel king, which willingly gave them: and would be as good as his promise, vers. 6.

*{chief of the governors}* Chap. 4.9.

**V. 49.** *{Daniel requested of the King}* He did not this for their private profit, but that the whole Church, which was then there in affliction, might have some release and ease, by this benefit. And hence we see, that men which have the favor of kings, should prefer good men, whom they know to be such, as Daniel knew these to be, to such places, as they cannot attend to execute themselves.

*{over the affairs of the Province}* His chambers, and his comings in, and dispatches: and also his affairs abroad in the provinces.

*{Daniel sat in the gate of the King}* Meaning, that he was a judge; judges did use to sit in the gates for judgment, Deut. 21.19. Amos 5.15. that he had the whole authority; so that none could be admitted to the king’s presence, but by him, or him thereby. For the Chaldean and Persian kings were stately and curious, in admitting of access, Esth. 4.11. He stirred not from the king, being, as it were, the master of his palace, and the chief minister, and officer of his empire; of greatest power and familiarity with the king.

## CHAPTER 3

**Vers. 1.** *{Made an Image}* This was done, after that, in the former chapter; as appears, Chap. 2.49. and the twelfth verse of this chapter: and likely, about two years after that dream, in the former chapter, about the seventh year of Nebuchadnezzar’s reign, and a little before Jechoniah’s captivity; seeing only the captived with Daniel, are accused here, vers. 8,12. Anything in Apocryphal prayer of Azariah, is not of credit to discountenance this. Though it puts the Papists upon a time, long after. The occasion of making this image, is not like to be with any relation, any manner of way, to that image which he saw in his dream, in the former chapter, though some are of this opinion. But rather, it may be, upon the like occasion, to that in the sixth Chap. vers. 1, etc. upon the envy the Chaldeans had against the Jews, for their preferment in the chief offices and honors of the kingdom, to seek an advantage thereby to ruin them, though likewise *Nebuchadnezzar* might the rather be moved, to yield to such a motion made, as seeking and feeding, therein, his own ambition, and vainglory; under pretence of religion and holiness, in making, that is, in causing to be made such an image to his idol Bel. Pagan stories have many examples of the like colosses, dedicated to idols. This image, likely, was the image of a man: yet we can hardly conceive, with some, that he made this to be dedicated to himself, to be worshiped thereby, as a God; though the pride of his heart, and the vanity of his mind, was very great, and he much fallen from that, Chap. 2.47. and not much come short of the king of Tyre, Ezek. 28.2. And if we consider what kinds of gods the heathen had, & worshiped as such, even such as had been infamously vicious men; as for instance, their Jupiter, or chiefest god; and what numbers they had of them; Hesiod says, 30000. Tertullian in *Apologetico*, speaks of 30. whom they called all Jupiters. We may the less marvel, if some famous monarchs, among the heathen, such as this Nebuchadnezzar, and Alexander the Great, might affect such a deity, if not in their lifetime, yet an *ἀποθέωσιν*, after their death; and to have such like honor done to them, as were to those conceived deities. See Esay 14.12,13.

*{of gold}* It is likely it was gold without, and hollow within. A great expense upon this idol, which might have been bestowed upon better uses. But this has still been the custom of idolaters, to spare no gold, to think nothing too costly and dear for their idols, Hos. 2.8. & 8.4. Exod. 32.2,3,4,31. 1 King. 12.28. Deut. 7.25. & 20.17. Judg. 8.26,27. Psalm 115.4. Esay 46.6. Jer. 10.9.

*{six cubits}* Diameter, that is, through the traverse of it, overcross the body, as it is likely. Also besides, there being no proportion of six cubits of breadth, and sixty of height, it is fit to be supposed, that in, and under the number of sixty, is comprehended some great pedestal, upon which the statue stood. Of cubits, see *Annotations* on Ezek. 40.5.

*{Dura}* *Deera;* Ptolomy in his sixth book of Geography. Set it up in an open champaign place, that it might be the better seen and worshiped of all.

**V. 2.** *{gather together the Princes}* He calls all sorts, vers. 4. But the great men, and officers, only, are here named, as best serving, to set forth the honor of this dedication; and by their example, to draw on others to the same idolatrous worship. *Magnates*, are magnets: *as in water face answers to face*, Prov. 27.19. So, for the most part, the mean correspond with the manners of the mighty. The several kinds of officers, here named, are not now, by us, very certainly distinguishable: as neither the several kinds of instruments, vers. 5,7,10,15. nor the several sorts of attire, Esay, 3.18–24. His earnestness in superstition, should teach godly princes, to be forward in setting up true religion, and promoting it.

*{to the dedication}* Showing, that the idol is not known for an idol, so long as it is with the workman: but when the ceremonies and rites recited and used, and the consent of the people had, and invocation made, then of a block, or image, they think they have made a god: or an excellent means, or manner for the worship of their God. And in this point of their dedication, they might likely take some hint, and general notion, from the people of God; among whom, by God’s command, in other cases, dedications were practiced, with sacrifices and music, and sundry ceremonies to that end used, divers days: as Numb. 7. 1 King. 8. Ezra 3. & 6. Neh. 12. See Deut. 20.5. Psal. 30. in the inscription.

*{the King had set up}* This was sufficient with the wicked, to approve their religion, if the king’s authority were alleged for the establishment thereof; not considering in the mean season, what God’s words did permit.

**V. 3.** *{Then the Princes}* Some of these words following, are rendered as well as our English words, and offices will bear: and the like is done in the instruments of music, in the 5,7,10,15 verse.

*{stood}* Wondering at such an image; and with their minds intent to hear, and obey the king’s will, upon the sound of the music, to worship the image.

**V. 4.** *{aloud}* Chald. *with might.* Many heralds, likely, for so great a multitude; and several heralds, for several languages.

*{it is commanded}* Chald. *they command.*

**V. 5.** *{ye fall down and worship}* The music sounding, and the people adoring and praying. Here the falling down would soon discover who stood up, and see who refused, if any such should be. All this the Devil likes to be done to images and idols, which God forbids, Exod. 20.5. either absolutely, or relatively; mediately, or ultimately, to be done to them. And yet the Devil tempted our Savior to have done himself, Mat. 4.9. But this worship, when it is not civil, but religious, the Saints and Angels of God, do only yield it unto God; as Gen. 22.5. & 24.26,52. Exod. 34.8. 2 Chron. 20.18. & 29.29. Apoc. 4.10. Psal. 95.6. And they refused to have it yielded to themselves, Apoc. 19.10. & 22.9. Act. 10.25,26. Mat. 4.10.

**V. 6.** *{the same hour}* The hot and cruel zeal of idolaters, in and for their idolatrous worship, vers. 13. Here is no time of respite, no delay, ver. 15.

**V. 7.** *{Therefore at that time}* There can be nothing so wickedly commanded, but multitudes will be ready to obey it. Such is the power of a king’s command; and the dread of punishment: and so little are multitudes to be followed in matters of religion. All the people, nations and languages, that were there gathered, (for all individuals could not be) did fall down, and worship this golden image.

**V. 8.** *{wherefore at that time}* They greedily took this occasion, which was the thing they sought, to accuse the Jews: Or, as the Chaldee has it, *They did eat accusations*. They did it instantly; having their eyes upon their persons, more than upon the image, knowing before, that these men detested their Chaldean gods. Therefore as soon as ever the heat of the adoration, and dedication was over, they delay no time, but presently accuse them.

**V. 9.** *{O King, live for ever}* Chap. 2.4.

**V. 10.** *{Thou, O King}* Vers. 4,5,6.

**V. 12.** *{certain Jews}* See *Annotations* on vers. 1.

*{Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego}* It seemed that they named not Daniel, because he was great in the king’s favor; or upon some just occasion, of sickness, or public affairs, was absent; if he did not purposely absent himself; thinking, that if these three had been destroyed, they might have had the better occasion to accuse Daniel after. And this, declares, that the policy, in erecting this image, was invented by the malicious flatterers; which sought nothing, but the destruction of the Jews, whom they accused of rebellion and ingratitude.

*{have not regarded thee}* Chald. *have set no regard upon thee.*

*{whom thou hast set over}* The greater (as they imply) is their rebellion, and ingratitude.

*{thy gods}* Multitude of gods, with the heathen, through the ignorance of the one true God. Yet the wisest of the heathen, constantly avowed, one only true God.

*{nor worship the golden image}* They not so mad, as to think the images to be gods. The king himself, distinguishes between his gods, and the images of them, vers. 14.

**V. 13.** *{in his rage}* Much more should we be zealous, for the true worship of the true God.

*{commanded to bring}* It seems they were there, in obedience to the first part of that command, ver. 2. which clearly included them; though they would not worship; ready there, to show, and avow their detestation of that idolatry; and to answer anything in that case, might be laid against them. Or else, surely they were not far off: for this dedication, likely, lasted certain days.

**V. 14.** *{true}* Or, *of purpose;* as Exod. 21.13. He wonders, and scorns they should take upon them, to be wiser in the true religion, than he: Yet for all his rage, he condemns them not upon hearsay; but brings them to answer for themselves, Act. 25.16.

**V. 15.** *{Now if ye be ready}* Signifying, that he would willingly have them to be a good example of obedience to others; and that he would receive them to grace, if they would now, this day, or the next day of the dedication, at the sound of the music; (though rather his heat, and the rigor of the decree, vers. 6. promise no such favor, to put it off to another day) obey his decree. The imperfect speech, argues his heat. Some of the names of the musical instruments, seem to be derived from the Greek tongue; as if they had first received them from the Grecians; as *Cythera*, *Sambuca*, *Psalterium*, *Symphonia*.

*{but if ye worship not}* Though they were dear to him, for their own worth and wisdom, and services they did him, in the affairs of the province of Babylon; as also for Daniel’s sake: yet all that should not assuage his wrath, nor cool his zeal, for his idolatrous service.

*{ye shall be cast}* This is the weapon persecutors use, against God’s Church and children.

*{who is that God that shall deliver you?}* A proud speech against God, as Exod. 5.2. Esay 36.20. 2 King. 18.30. He forgets himself, and that confession of his, Chap. 2.47. See vers. 28.

**V. 16.** *{not careful}* The matter is such, as needs no time of deliberation, nor answer at all; at least in words, but rather in deeds of constancy and courage on our part, 2 Macc. 7.2. And God, for his part, will answer for himself, he is able enough to clear his power, against your proud boast and threat. They should have done injury to God, if they should have doubted in his holy cause: and therefore they say, they fear not the danger, or death threatened; they are resolved to die for God’s cause; and when men have God on their side, they are not troubled to answer before great men. See Matth. 10.19. Mar. 13.11. Luk. 12.11. Acts 4.19. Rom. 8.37.

**V. 17.** *{is able}* They ground on two points; first, on the power and providence of God over them, against his proud brag, vers. 15. And secondly, on their cause, which was God’s glory, and the testifying of his true religion, with their blood; and so make open confession, that they will not so much as outwardly consent to idolatry.

*{and he will}* Psal. 27.1. & 118.6. Micah 7.7. This was their trust, either upon special instinct, or upon the many examples of God’s like dealing with many of his servants, in like cases, whereof the Scripture is full.

**V. 18.** *{But if not}* That is, *will not*. They do not say, *He cannot*, Matth. 8.2. God might see it best for them to suffer. And however, though God’s judgments may be sometimes secret, they are always just. And therefore they are resolved, without all care, or cunctation, notwithstanding any danger, not to obey his idolatrous will.

*{we will not serve thy Gods}* Thus the servants of God, will run through any danger, rather than sin against God, Heb. 12.4. Singular faith, and fortitude, piety and constancy.

**V. 19.** *{full}* Chald. *filled.*

*{form of his visage changed}* Passions of the mind, soon appear in the face, Gen. 4.5. & 31.2,5. Dan. 5.9. Joel 2.6. Ezek. 27.35. Esay 13.8. Neh. 2.2. Neither the majesty of his place and person, nor the presence of all his peers, nor the celebrity of that solemnity, could any way moderate his furious passion.

*{seven times}* Chald. *one seven times.* It may be exactly so much: Or, a certain number put for an uncertain, to signify very much more than afore; as Esay 4.1. Prov. 24.16. Psal. 12.6. The apocryphal addition, at the end of Azariah’s prayer, relates, that the flame streamed forth, above the furnace, 49 cubits. Indeed, this would the sooner have dispatched them, and shortened their pain; but the mad king (*ira furor brevis est*) heeded not that: And the wise God, made it serve, to make the miracle the more marvelous. Idolaters are cruel, when they are crossed: and the more the tyrants rage, and the more witty they show themselves, in inventing strange and cruel punishments, the more is God glorified by his servants, to whom he gives patience and constancy, to abide the cruelty of their punishment, and to slight them and it; knowing, that either he delivers them from death, or else, for this life gives them a better.

*{wont}* It seems, this was a usual kind of capital punishment, among the Chaldeans, Jer. 29.22.

**V. 20.** *{most mighty}* Chald. *mighty of strength.*

*{in his army}* Or, *guard,* then attending him. See *Annotations* on Chap. 2.14.

**V. 21.** *{In their coats}* Chald. *mantles.* Some translate them *breeches*; but these were not much in use with the Jews. Those they had on them, of what kind, and how good or precious soever, for haste, vers. 22. Whereas, others, likely, in case of like punishment, were cast in naked. And God’s providence made use of this, to the increase of the miracle, vers. 27.

*{hats}* Chald. *turbant.* The word is nearest the Greek word, *πέτασον*.

**V. 22.** *{the flame}* Chald. *spark.* Catched hold of them, and burnt them.

*{slew those men}* The fire wanted not its power of burning; but obeyed its Creator, in slaying these, and not hurting the other. It is not good to be instruments of great men’s cruelties; for such sometimes fare worse, than either principal authors, or sufferers. We cannot say, that these men were the persuaders of Nebuchadnezzar, to this burning rage and cruelty.

**V. 23.** *{fell down bound}* But straight were loosed, and walked in the midst of the fire, vers. 25. The bonds burnt; and no smell of fire on them, or their clothes, vers. 27.

**V. 24.** *{astonished}* As he had cause: and it touched him most, who had cast them in.

*{rose up}* From his chair of majesty, and throne of installation or inauguration; to go to the furnace.

*{in haste}* As soon as the miracle was discerned; which was presently. And therefore not the next day after. Thus God can make a sudden change in the greatest monarch. So Chap. 5. vers. 6.

*{Counsellors}* Or, *Governours:* Not unlikely their accusers.

*{three men}* And no more: and those bound too.

**V. 25.** *{Lo, I see}* He was hard by the furnace to see the execution: or now come to it, to see the miracle. And saw now four, and them loose, and walking, and not touched with the fire.

*{walking}* The furnace was great. That long prayer of Azariah; and long song of them all three in the midst of the fire and furnace, are apocryphal additions, made canonical by the Trent Synod of the Roman Church.

*{they have no hurt}* Chald. *there is no hurt in them,* Psal. 29.7. and 66.12. Esay 43.2. Here the fire said, *It is enough,* Prov. 30.15. To ascribe this to their virginity, or fasting, is without warrant. The Holy Ghost ascribes it to their faith, Heb. 11.34.

*{and the form of the fourth is like the Son of God}* Or, *like a son of the gods*; or *like some young God*; most bright, beautiful, and divine. Such were the heathenish opinions. But the doubtful words in the heathen king’s speech, have been well taken of the ancients, as they best might mean: And some understand him hereby to mean an Angel; and so he calls him expressly *an Angel of God,* ver. 28. And the Angels do bear a form and likeness of God; and are called in Scripture the *Sons of God,* Job 1.6. & 2.1. & 38.7. and are God’s ministers and messengers, as the word does signify, sent by him to do his will, and deliver his servants; as Act. 12.7–12. And all this might Nebuchadnezzar hear or learn of Daniel. But whatever Nebuchadnezzar did, we yet may understand this fourth to be, as the words do literally bear, the very true Son of God, our Savior Christ; who is with his servants in their extremities.

**V. 26.** *{mouth}* Chald. *door.* So near as he might with safety; the fire being now not so raging.

*{the servants of the most high God}* Thus, God convinces the conscience, and does sometimes miraculously force great men to honor him, and his servants.

*{come forth}* He speaks now, no doubt, submissively; who before spoke so proudly, ver. 15. This change was made by the right hand of the most high. And here is their obedience unto God; that they would not for any fear depart out of this furnace, till the time appointed; as Noah remained in the ark, till the Lord called him forth.

**V. 27.** *{Nor was an hair—nor the smell of fire}* The least hurt cannot come to those whom God will preserve, Isa. 43.2. Mat. 10.30. Act. 27.34. 1 Sam. 14.45.

**V. 28.** *{Blessed be the God of Shadrach}* He was moved by the greatness of the miracle to praise God; but his heart was not yet thoroughly touched. We see that miracles are not sufficient to convert men to God, Deut. 29.3,4. but that doctrine, and the saving grace and power of God, must be adjoined, without which there can be no faith, and true conversion. Yet thus for a while again his heart was brought to repent and relent.

*{who hath sent his Angel}* Nebuchadnezzar no Sadducee, Act. 23.8. And likely told thus much now by the three confessors or martyrs, upon questions and demands by him made unto them.

*{that trusted in him}* Faith *quenches the violence of fire*, Heb. 11.34.

*{and have changed the King’s word}* That word, vers. 15. doing contrary to it, and thereby have now changed the king’s mind, in all this business.

*{and yielded their bodies}* Mat. 10.39. Joh. 12.25. Luk. 14.26.

*{except their own God}* Mat. 4.10. Exod. 20.3. Likely, hereupon other Jews were not questioned for non-adoration, after this miracle shown, if the dedication did hold more days after this. Though most of them were too prone to idolatry, Jer. 16.13.

**V. 29.** *{I make a Decree}* Chald. *a Decree is made by me.* Spoken now; registered after. A better decree than his other decrees, chap. 2.9,13. & 3.10. And much more is it lawful, and the duty of Christian kings, to make decrees for God and his worship, and against sin and idolatry.

*{speak any thing amiss}* If this heathen king, moved by God’s Spirit, would not suffer reproachful speeches against God unpunished, but made a law, and set a penalty for such transgressors; much more ought all they in authority, that profess religion, to take order that such impiety reign not; lest according as their knowledge and charge is greater, so they suffer double punishment.

*{any thing amiss}* Chald. *error.* And yet for all this, wicked Jehoiakim at this time would not cease to deal amiss in Judah; until he was buried as an ass, and had his carcass made as dung, being cast away unburied, Jer. 22.26. & 36.30.

*{cut in pieces}* Chald. *made pieces,* Chap. 2.5. See *Annotations* upon it.

*{No other god that can}* Nor Bel himself, nor Jupiter Belus.

**V. 30.** *{promoted}* Chald. *made to prosper.* He had done so before, Chap. 2.49. Now he promoted them more, made them to prosper; or at least restored them to their honors: and forbade all any more to wrong them.

## CHAPTER 4

**Vers. 1.** *{Nebuchadnezzar the King}* This chapter contains Nebuchadnezzar’s proclamation, or letter, and the historical narration of the occasion of it. And all in such a style, as may seem to be penned by Nebuchadnezzar himself, or at least in his name; and inserted by Daniel in his book, as is the mournful song or writing of Hezekiah, by Esay, Chap. 38. and the edict of Cyrus, and the letters to Artaxerxes, and from Artaxerxes; and likewise to Darius, and from Darius are by Ezra, in his book, 1,4,5,6. Chapters; as also the copy of the letter of that other Artaxerxes to Ezra, Chap. 7. But such as would have this to be a parable rather than a true history, are no ways to be endured.

*{In all the earth}* Meaning as far as his dominion extended.

*{peace be multiplied}* Heathens sending out proclamations to their people, begin with wishing happiness to them. So Dan. 25.6.25. Therefore they have need to see, that nothing be commanded but for their happiness. The like form of salutation is used by the Apostle, 1 Pet. 1.2. & 2 Pet. 1.2. and by Jude, vers. 2.

**V. 2.** *{To shew the signs}* That God which can make the mouths of children to speak to his praise, Psal. 8.2. can make heathens trumpeters of his glory.

**V. 3.** *{an everlasting Kingdom}* Chald. *a Kingdome of eternity.* Lasting beyond all time, Chap. 2.44. See the *Annotations* there.

*{from generation to generation}* Chald. *with generation and generation;* or, *with one generation as well as with another*: or, *to all generations*.

**V. 4.** *{I Nebuchadnezzar}* He had not conquered Egypt, until after the 27 of Jechoniah, or his own 34.
