Ezek. 30.1. wherefore this vision should be about his 36 year: (and so about 28 or 29 years after that history in the former chapter; and 30 or 31 years after the dream of the image, Chap. 2. and falls out to be in the year of the world 3433. or thereabouts) And this proclamation was about a year or two before his death, his reign continuing 45 years. For his madness begun 12 months after this vision, ver. 29. And that continued 7 years. At the end whereof he was restored to his wits, and to his kingdom; and then made this proclamation, and so reigned one or two years after.

*{was at rest in mine house}* From all enemies, and fear of such as he had subdued; so that there was no trouble that might cause me to dream. And therefore it came only of God, who can trouble men in the height of their prosperity, Psal. 30.6.

*{in my palace}* In Babylon, ver. 29,30. Chap. 1.4.

**V. 5.** *{a dream}* This was another dream besides that which he saw of the four empires; for Daniel both declared what that dream was; and what it meant: And here he only expounds the dream.

*{Visions}* More than one were in this one dream.

*{of my heads}* In the head is the faculty of imagination: There are the animal and natural spirits, which carry in the forms and similitudes of things to the common sense and fantasy; and raise up thoughts and cogitations.

*{troubled me}* Chap. 2.1. Job 7.13,14. Both the dream, and his after thoughts upon it disquieted him.

**V. 6.** *{all the wise men}* The greatest princes have sometimes need of others to declare unto them, what themselves know not.

**V. 7.** *{Magicians}* Chap. 22,27.

*{they did not make known}* It was beyond their power, 1 Cor. 2.11. Gen. 40.8. & 41.16,39. 1 Cor. 1.19. In that he sent abroad to others, whose ignorance in times past he had experienced, and left Daniel which was ever ready at hand; either he did it to try them, and their skill of interpretation, whereof they vaunted, though falsely, when the dream should be told them, Chap. 2.4,7. or else it declares the nature of the ungodly, which never seek to the servants of God, but for very necessity; and then they spare no flatterings.

**V. 8.** *{at the last}* This word *Achoren,* in the Trent Bible is translated *Colleague;* so signifying, that Daniel was as a fellow companion to Nebuchadnezzar. But differing so, through a mistake of the word, from all other translations.

*{Belteshazzar}* See the Note of Chap. 1.7.

*{of my God}* This no doubt was a grief to Daniel, not only to have his name changed, but to be called by the name of a vile idol; which thing Nebuchadnezzar did, to make him to forget the religion of God. And this place argues, that the king forsook not his idolatry.

*{and in whom is the Spirit of the holy Gods}* He a heathen still, and so speaks, holding a multitude of gods; and some of them holy and good, others evil and bad, which they worshiped, that they might not hurt them; and that Daniel was inspired, taught, and guided by the Spirit of the holy Gods, and had it in him, and thereby divined: not much unlike that damsel, Acts 16.16,19. And this further appears, by the title he gives him, ver. 9.

**V. 9.** *{Master of the Magicians}* Chap. 2.48. & 5.11. which shows that heathenish conceit which Nebuchadnezzar still had of Daniel, which also was a grief to the Prophet, to be numbered among the sorcerers, and men whose practices were wicked, and contrary to God’s Word.

*{Spirit of the holy Gods}* He speaks as a heathen still; in a heathenish sense. The Scripture uses also the name of Gods; but understand thereby, either false gods, Psal. 96.5,6. or else the Angels in heaven, Psal. 97.7. Heb. 1.6. or magistrates on earth, Psal. 82.1,6. See 1 Cor. 8.5,6. Psal. 86.8.

*{tell me the visions}* That is, what the visions mean, as it follows in the text, the latter word expounding the former: for in the verse next before, Nebuchadnezzar tells the dream to Daniel.

**V. 10.** *{a tree}* Man often in Scripture is likened to a tree, and several kinds of men to several kinds of trees, Mat. 3.10. & 12.33. Jude 12. Psal. 1.3. & 49.20. 2 King. 14.9. Ezek. 17.24. & 31.3. Here by the tree is signified further the dignity of a king, whom God ordains to be a defence for all kind of men, and whose state is profitable for mankind.

*{in the midst of the earth}* Indeed Babylon was much about the midst of the earth.

**V. 12.** *{the beasts of the field}* Ezek. 31.6. applied to the Assyrian. The right and true use of monarchy and magistracy is hereby hieroglyphically and excellently deciphered.

**V. 13.** *{a watcher}* Meaning the Angel of God, which neither eats nor sleeps; but as one full of eyes, as in that vision, Ezek. Chap. 1.18. & 10.12. is ever watchful and ready to see, and do God’s will; as the Echo still readily answers the voice and is not infected with man’s corruption, but is ever holy: And in that, that he commands to cut down the tree, he knew that it should be cut down, though not by man, yet by God, ver. 31.

*{an holy one}* Chap. 8.13. How heathenish Nebuchadnezzar should come to this apprehension and knowledge of this person we know not; unless he might gather it out of those words in the vision, vers. 17.

*{cried alone}* Chap. 3.4.

**V. 14.** *{Hew down the tree}* See the change of human felicity. And when God calls great men to account for their sins, he makes thorough work with them, leaving neither fruit, nor leaf, nor branch, Ezek. 17.9. Job 18.16. Esay 9.14. Thus God drowned Pharaoh, who had caused the male children of Israel to be drowned. God’s thunderbolt breaks and burns the strongest oaks and highest buildings.

*{from under it}* That is, from abiding there.

**V. 15.** *{the stump of his roots}* When God deals heavily with great sinners, yet there is some mercy; he leaves a stump in the ground.

*{Let it be wet with the dew of heaven}* That is, him that is signified by this stump. Lying without, abroad, night and day, Cant. 5.2. Babylon is a hot country, and the winter there, but little cold.

**V. 16.** *{Let his heart be changed}* His heart; not his manly shape and figure. Hereby he means, that Nebuchadnezzar should not only, for a time, lose his kingdom, but be like a beast; should think himself to be, not a man, but a beast; and so live, and lie, and feed as a beast, vers. 25,32,38. God can make a man’s heart, as stupid as a beast’s: So wise a man, a madman, vers. 34,36. He, who, in his heart, would have been a God, now, in his own heart, to be a beast.

*{Let seven times pass over him}* In that miserable estate and condition. See vers. 23,25,32. These times were years: as in another case, the interpretation is made in the text itself, Chap. 11.13. And these years, such as our years: for such are the years, in Scripture account; as may distinctly be gathered out of the history of the Flood, Gen. 7,8,9. Chapters. We may not then conceit, here, other kinds of years, as some do; nor conceive a number certain, to be put here for an uncertain. He then that destroyed Solomon’s Temple; that seven years’ work of so many thousands, should be, exactly, so many years beside himself. The remarkable, memorable judgment of God, upon so mighty an enemy, and destroyer of his people, city, and sanctuary.

**V. 17.** *{this matter}* God has decreed this judgment, and the whole army of heaven, have, as it were, subscribed unto it: like as also, they desire the execution of his decree, against all them that lift up themselves against God.

*{the watchers}* That is, of God, vers. 24. God the Father, Son, and Holy Ghost; environed with his Angels; described in Scripture, as those that sit in counsel with God, 1 King. 22.19,20. Job 1.6.

*{and the demand by the word of the Holy ones}* Or, *according to the word of the Holy ones, is this Petition.* Intimating, that in the consultation, as it were, about the punishing of the pride of Nebuchadnezzar, the holy Angels petitioned for this very thing; as well as the Holy ones, and Saints on earth, petitioned against him.

*{the living may know}* Even by Nebuchadnezzar’s publishing and proclaiming of it, vers. 2,35,37. God brings down the kingdoms of men, to make his own known, Luk. 1.52. 1 Sam. 2.6,7,8. Psal. 75. vers. 6,7.

**V. 18.** *{declare}* And that speedily: His guilty conscience, and this kind of dream, made him suspect the worst; and he would fain be out of pain.

*{the Spirit of the holy Gods}* He speaks still as a heathen man. See vers. 9. And he speaks now upon former experience had.

**V. 19.** *{was astonied}* Both in regard of the sad interpretation; and also, because it lay on his part, to expound it to the king, who had so much honored him; and whose peace was profitable, both to Daniel, and the people of the Jews; for which they were commanded to pray, Jer. 29.7. So the Prophets used, on the one part, to denounce God’s judgments, for the zeal they bore to his glory; and on the other part, to have compassion upon man; and also to consider, that they should be subject to God’s judgments, if they did not regard them with pity.

*{for one hour}* A long time; it being in the king’s presence. In which time, no doubt, as he first prayed to God, for the truth in interpretation of the dream; so understanding it, he was after troubled at the sad meaning of it, both for the king’s sake, and also how he might best come off fairly, in telling and expounding of it.

*{The King spake}* The king, likely, perceived Daniel was not troubled for want of knowledge of the interpretation; but because of somewhat that was to befall the king; as, in all likelihood, he could expect no good from such a dream and vision: as neither Belshazzar from that, Chap. 5.5,6.

*{my Lord}* When God’s messengers are to deal with great men, they must do it wisely; as Daniel did here with the king; and so did Nathan with David, by a parable, bringing his fault to his mind, 2 Sam. 12. And surely greater wisdom and skill, could not be used, than Daniel here shows, to decline the king’s offense, and to qualify the bitterness of the interpretation.

*{to them that hate thee}* That is, would to God, the sense of this dream might be directed against your enemies, rather than against you: a kind of protestation, to show, that in the interpretation thereof, he was not prepossessed with any hatred, or passion against the king; but continued, full of dutiful love and affection towards him.

**V. 20.** *{The tree, etc.}* Vers. 10,11,12.

**V. 22.** *{It is thou}* That is, This great empire which you possess, is meant, which was signified by the tree, as above, Chap. 2.38.

**V. 23.** *{Hew the tree down}* Vers. 14. The whole tree; not some branches. He lost his whole empire, for the time. The speech seems to be directed to the Chaldeans.

*{with a band of iron}* It may be, that Nebuchadnezzar himself, at the beginning of his madness, was tied in bonds, and after suffered to run loose abroad; and the rather, by reason of his dream and interpretation, famously known amongst the Chaldeans. However, that speech against the Assyrian, Esay 37.29. is, doubtless, metaphorical.

*{till seven times pass over him}* See above, vers. 16.

**V. 25.** *{Drive thee from men}* Chap. 5.19,20,21. Because, for his madness, he was odious to them, or in his madness, might many ways hurt them. Or rather, taking it impersonally, For *you shall be driven*, as in the Chaldee and Hebrew phrase, is usual, we may so understand it of his own madness, that that should drive him from the company of men, to the company of beasts: As having the heart of a beast, thinking himself to be a beast; and so coveting, as beasts do, rather to be in company of beasts than of men.

*{Oxen}* Not that his shape or form was changed into a beast, but that he was stricken mad, and so avoided man’s company, and so wandered among the beasts, and did eat herbs and grass; as thinking that food, though not so pleasing or nourishing, did yet properly belong unto him. See Job 6. vers. 7.

*{the most high}* Daniel shows the end, why God thus punished him; which proved true in him, vers. 34,35,36,37.

*{to whomsoever}* Jer. 27.5. & 18.4.

**V. 26.** *{after that thou}* After that you shall be tamed, or humbled by this horrible accident, you shall then recover your understanding, having quitted your ambition which possessed you: and being revested with humility towards God, by whose only favor you do reign; you shall then also surely reign again; as he did at the making of this proclamation, and a year or two after.

*{the heavens do rule}* That is, God, which dwells, and reigns in glory in the heavens; as Mat. 21.25. Luk. 15.21.

**V. 27.** *{Let my counsel, etc.}* Great men have need of counsels, Prov. 11.14. & 12.15. & 15.22. & Chap. 19. ver. 20. & 24.6. & 27.9. 1 King. 12. Thus Exod. 18. vers. 19. And hence, likely, the Romans’ chief magistrates were called consuls.

*{break off thy sins}* Of tyranny, pride, etc. Chap. 5.19,20. of oppressing God’s people; cease from provoking God any longer to anger, by your sins, that he may mitigate his punishment, if you show, by your upright life, that you have true faith and repentance: for God’s greatest judgments have an end, when they bring forth such good fruits, Esay 26.9,16.

*{by shewing mercy to the poor}* Luk. 11.41. Esay 58.7–12. Ezek. 18.7. Act. 10.2,4,31. This would have been a good fruit, and sign of his repentance, if he had showed mercy to the poor, specially to the poor afflicted Jews, now in captivity under him: He had done otherwise, Chap. 5.19. But now he should thus seek to redeem, as Ephes. 5.16. and take off this punishment of his iniquities, get mercy from God, by showing mercy to the poor.

*{if it may be}* The like phrase, Exod. 32.30. & Josh. 14.12. & 1 Sam. 14.6. Intimating some hope; yet leaving it doubtful, what might be God’s will, 2 Sam. 12.14. and what would be the king’s repentance.

*{a lengthening of thy tranquillity}* Or, *an healing of thine error.* Others read, *It shall be a lengthening of your peace and prosperity*: and indeed there was some lengthening, to wit, for a year, at least, as may appear by the 29 verse. The like example of lengthening, is of Ahab, 1 King. 21.29. And not much unlike is that of Ezekiah, Esay 38.5. and of the Ninevites, Jonah 3.10. See Jer. 18.8. But the small repentance of this king appears, vers. 30.

**V. 29.** *{twelve months}* After that Daniel had declared this vision; so patient is God, Rom. 2.4. And haply by that time, the king, continuing still in his glorious felicity, grew to that opinion, that the interpretation of the dream and vision was false. However, his pride so continuing, declares, that it is not in man to convert to God, except his Spirit move him; seeing that these terrible threatenings could not move him truly to repent, Chap. 5.20.

*{in the palace}* Or, *upon the roof being flat;* as usually: whence he had the view of the city, which haply puffed up his spirit, and recalled his former thoughts.

**V. 30.** *{Is not this great Babylon}* Of the greatness of it, see Herodotus, book 1. Pliny, book 6. yet Nineveh once surpassed it, Jonah 1.2. & 3.3. And yet Fez, Grand Cairo, and Constantinople seem not to come far short of them this day. Of his Luciferian pride, see Esay his prophecy, Chap. 14.12,13.

*{that I have built for the house}* That is, adorned, and beautified with many marvelous edifices, and pensile, or hanging gardens, and orchards, and enlarged and compassed with a new wall, so that it may seem a city altogether new: though first built by Nimrod, Gen. 10.10. and after that much enlarged, beautified, and strengthened with walls by Semiramis the wife of Ninus. It may be the king would have added to these words, some other to this effect, *How then can they drive me from men, and from my Kingdome?* as vers. 25.

*{a voice from Heaven}* Framed by that Watcher and Holy One, vers. 13,23. like a thunder, with the bolt in it.

**V. 31.** *{is departed}* That is, shall, as is decreed, presently depart, vers. 33.

**V. 32.** *{drive}* See vers. 25.

**V. 33.** *{The same hour}* Presently. So Herod, the nephew of Herod the Great, and son of Aristobulus, was instantly smitten, Act. 12.22,23.

*{his hairs—his nails}* Being uncut, and unpared, so very long a time. And not unlikely but hair did extraordinarily grow upon his body.

*{like Eagles}* He was compared to an eagle the queen of birds, Jer. 48.40. & 49.22. Ezek. 17.3.

**V. 34.** *{at the end of the days}* When the term of these seven years was accomplished: then from a beast in heart, he became a man again; then, and not before, his reason returned unto him.

*{I praised}* Wicked men can praise God, when miracles are wrought for them.

*{an everlasting Dominion}* Chap. 7.14. Mic. 4.7. Luk. 1.33.

**V. 35.** *{and all the Inhabitants}* Isa. 40.15,17. Psal. 62.9. And so himself, that formerly was so high and haughty, would be a God, was now become in his own eyes, as nothing. Thus affliction works, and God’s judgments, Job 33.14–19.

*{his will}* He confesses God’s will to be the rule of all justice, and a most perfect law, whereby he governs both man, and angels, and devils; so that none ought to murmur or ask a reason of his doings, but only to rest content therewith, and give him the glory.

*{army of heaven}* That is, in the celestial bodies, and among the angelical creatures.

*{inhabitants of the earth}* Even kings and princes, pulling down one, and putting up another, 1 Sam. 2.8.

*{stay his hand}* Chald. *strike back.* Nothing can hinder what God has foretold; none of God’s predictions fail, Esay 46.10. Psal. 33.10,11. Prov. 19.21. Job 42.2. Jer. 50.44.

*{what dost thou?}* Job 9.12. Isa. 45.9.

**V. 36.** *{my reason returned unto me}* That is, my understanding, ver. 34. this, as the chief point is redoubled. And also that kingly presence, and other heroic qualities, fit to uphold the dignity of an empire, were returned to me.

*{brightness returned unto me}* The same Chaldee word is used, Chap. 1.31. and is here most fit to expound the former, what was meant by the brightness of the image.

*{and my Counsellors}* It is likely, his son reigned in this time of seven years’ madness. And his counsellors, and all famously knowing, vers. 6,7. the dream, and interpretation; and seeing all fulfilled accordingly, did confidently expect, till these seven years were ended, for his return to his wisdom and kingdom again.

**V. 37.** *{praise and extol and honour the King of heaven}* A public solemn confession and doxology, wherein he does not only praise God for his deliverance; but also confesses his fault and punishment, that God may only have the glory, and man the shame; and that he may be exalted, and man cast down.

*{works are truth}* Rom. 3.4. Psal. 145.17. Deut. 32.4.

*{that walk in pride, etc.}* As himself had experienced, and so become an example and famous monument thereof to all posterity. A like confession to this, Nebuchadnezzar had made before, Chap. 2.47. and again, Chap. 3.28,29. And yet was not a man thoroughly or truly converted.

## CHAPTER 5

**Vers. 1.** *{Belshazzar the King}* Daniel writes this history of *Belshazzar,* Evil-Merodach’s son, (the time of whose reign is here passed over; as also the short reigns of Neriglissorus, and his young son Laborosarchodus, being intruders, to show God’s judgments against the wicked, for the deliverance of his Church: and how the prophecy of Jeremy was true, that they should serve Nebuchadnezzar and his son, Evilmerodach, and his son’s son, this Belshazzar, Jer. 27.7. And that they should be delivered after seventy years, Jer. 25.11,12. This Belshazzar seems to be called Merodac, Jer. 50.2. which was a name common, as to their idol Bel, so to many of their kings. See Esay 39.1. and signifies in Chaldee, a Scepter-bearer. This history we see fell out in the very end of his reign and of the Babylonish Empire. How many years this king reigned, historians do very much vary amongst themselves. Some say three; some, five; some, seventeen.

*{a great feast}* The Chaldee word is very like that which signifies bread. This feast seems to be in the evening; or at least continued till far in the night, vers. 5,30. And suppers usually were the most common times for feasts, Luk. 14.16. Mar. 6.21. Joh. 12.2. Alluding to this, Jeremy calls him the King *Sheschak,* Jer. 25.26. that is, the king keeping a feast; pointing it seems hereby, at this feast, and at the very day of his fall, and Babel’s fall in it. See Jer. 57.39,41,57. Esay 21.5. This unseasonable feast he made, when Cyrus straitly besieged the city, either to show his own confidence and security, and his slighting of the enemy; or because of the good service these thousand lords had done him in the siege: or rather, for that it was some anniversary feast, either for his own birth, as Gen. 40.20. Mar. 6.21. or for some ancient and public cause, religious or civil, of custom solemnly celebrated by the Babylonians. For heathen histories record, that Cyrus knew of this feast, and took the opportunity of it, to enter and conquer the city. And this being the last day of his reign, it appears, that the seventh and eighth chapters following, go in time before this.

*{to a thousand of his Lords}* So Ahasuerus afterwards to show his power and glory, Esth. 1.3,4.

*{drank wine}* This word signifies a solemn drinking, in a public feast.

*{before the thousand}* The kings of the East parts, then used to sit alone commonly, and disdained that any should sit in their company. Here the more to show his condescension of love and familiarity, he sat likely at a table by himself, but in the same hall with and before the thousand princes and lords, and his wives and concubines. So Joseph did, Gen. 43.32. Here the Latin Trent Bible instead of these words, *and drank wine before the thousand,* have these, *and every one did drink according to his age:* expounding it by that, Esth. 1.8. But this is otherwise in the Chaldee, and in all translations old and new.

**V. 2.** *{he tasted the wine}* Liked the taste, took pleasure in it, and disposing himself to drink largely: or, when he had well drunk was heated with wine, and began to be merry, Esth. 1.10. Great men in their jollity do often that which offends God, and grieves good men. And specially in their drink: for when the wine is in, the wit is out, Hos. 4.11. & 7.5. See Gen. 19.32. Esay 28.7. And are often most dissolute and negligent, when their destruction is at hand.

*{his father Nebuchadnezzar}* Meaning his grandfather. See Annotations on vers. 11.

*{taken out}* Chald. *brought forth;* sacrilegiously: and here now sacrilegiously and profanely abused, in drunken feasting by him, and his concubines. See the number sent back by Cyrus, Ezra 1.11. viz. 5400, which sum arises out of the particulars there numbered, and others unnumbered. And yet these were the whole ones; for some had been broken in pieces, 2 King. 24.13.

*{his wives and his Concubines}* Multitudes usual of them to heathen kings, particularly to the kings of Babylon, and Persia. Queen *Vashti* and the women feasted themselves, Esth. 1.9,11.

**V. 3.** *{and his Princes}* It is easy for great men to have partners in sins, and in the most sacrilegious abuse of sacred things.

**V. 4.** *{and praised the gods of gold}* This was usual in the feasts of the heathens: attributing to them their victories over God’s people, and the spoils of God’s house: feasting in honor of them, in contempt of the true God, praising their idols, and that above the God of the Jews. 2 Chron. 32.13,14. Esay 10.11. Not that they thought the gold or silver were gods, but that there was a certain virtue and power in their images of their gods to do them good; which is also the opinion of all idolaters, vers. 23.

*{of silver}* Men have no measure in idolatry, and dishonoring God: make gods of everything.

**V. 5.** *{in the same hour}* God meets with men sometimes in the midst of their sins, Psal. 78.30,31.

*{came forth}* From heaven, on the wall, ver. 24.

*{fingers}* Not the whole hand, ver. 24.

*{of a man’s hand}* As of a man’s hand, like them.

*{over against the candlestick}* That it might better be seen; with a candle in it, and lighted, as is most likely; and not prepared only against the night season: for till the night, and in it at leastwise, such great feasts are usually continued.

*{and the King saw}* The fingers writing; as the rest also did, vers. 8. He then was no ear-witness, but an eyewitness of it. He could not doubt of it; and his guilty conscience misgave him, that such a strange and prodigious thing concerned him, was a summons to him.

**V. 6.** *{then}* God does smite princes, offenders, sometimes first, and most.

*{countenance}* Chald. *brightnesse:* *vultus animi index,* in the height of all that jovial and festival hilarity. See *Annotations* on Chap. 3.19.

*{was changed}* Chald. *changed it:* from joyfulness and ruddiness, Prov. 15.3. to fear, and paleness, and ghastliness. In the light of his countenance formerly was life, Prov. 16.15. now trouble; as Ezek. 27.35. it was cast down, as Cain’s, Gen. 4.5. sad, as Nehemiah’s; it gathered blackness; as Joel 2.6. not much unlike that change of the Nazarites, Lam. 4.7,8. even dread and death was in it. Another-gates change was that of our Savior’s, Luk. 9.29. Mat. 17.2. Here we see the sudden change of worldly joy, Hos. 2.11. Amos 8.10. Prov. 14.13.

*{Joints of his loins}* Or, *girdles.* Chald. *bindings or knots.* It may seem a proverbial manner of speech, Isa. 5.27. taken from soldiers amazed, and flying, that cast their weapons from them: the sense is, all spirit and force failed him. See Nah. 2.1,10.

*{his knees smote one against another}* So he that before contemned God, was moved by this sight to tremble for fear of God’s judgment. See Ezek. 7.17. and 21.7.

**V. 7.** *{Cried aloud}* Chald. *with might.*

*{Astrologers}* Thus the wicked in their troubles seek many means, who draw them from God, because they seek not to him who is the only comfort in all afflictions.

*{clothed with scarlet}* Or, *purple;* a sign of the honor of great officers and seigniories, Gen. 41.42. such as consuls, and kings do use, Mar. 15.17. Apoc. 18.16. 1 Mac. 10.62. See how great princes promise honors to others, when they are near to lose their own.

*{chain of gold}* Vers. 29. Gen. 41. vers. 42.

*{third Ruler in the Kingdom}* Next to me, and the queen my mother, likely, or his son, if he had any, vers. 29.

**V. 8.** *{they could not read the writing}* It may be probable that the letters were written without distinction, or space between the words, so that they knew not how to put them together, or frame them to make any sense of them. Others say, the Chaldeans knew not God to be one, and to rule men’s affairs; neither knew they their own impiety; but said in their hearts, *There is no God:* wherefore though they could have read it, yet they knew not who should be weighed, specially in the balance: and touching the Medes and Persians: now their besiegers, they little thought that they should surprise the strong Babel; and bragged, how they were victualled, for 20 years’ siege, as Xenophon records. But the text says more, they could not so much as read it: the writing then, either being in the initial letter only; as *מכבי*, i. e. *Michemocha baelohim jehovah,* the Maccabees’ ensign, and the words of Exod. 15.11. As also *S.P.Q.R.* for *Senatus Populusq; Romanus:* and as we use to write *J.N.R.J.* for *Jesus Nazarenus Rex Judæorum:* or being in an unknown hand; neither Chaldee, nor Hebrew letter; for little doubt but these wise men knew both languages and letters, 2 King. 18.28. or else rather, God in his judgment blinding them all as to that point of reading itself; as Gen. 19.11. 2 King. 6.10. that *their eyes were holden*, as the two disciples were, going to Emmaus, Luk. 24.16. And this for the greater honor of Daniel, and God’s in him. For as for the short way of writing by characters now, or obscure way by hieroglyphics of old, or sundry secret ways by compact between man and man, they are not imaginable to be consistent with the words of the text.

**V. 9.** *{troubled}* The second time; being frustrated of his hope he had in his wise men, and of any comfort by them. His consternation increases; he goes on, as formerly in sin, from evil to worse, so now in fright more and more; and his lords the more at his terrors.

*{Countenance}* Chald. *brightnesses.* See vers. 6.

*{and his Lords were astonied}* Those vers. 1. So with Herod, all Jerusalem was troubled, Mat. 2.3. And they had just cause, both for the wonder of the thing, and the slaughter among them that followed that night. But none of these lords gainsaid the king in any of his wicked courses during this feast, but rather applauded and flattered him therein.

**V. 10.** *{Queen}* Not his wife, for his wives there were before, ver. 2,3. but his mother Evilmerodac’s wife, and queen, that famous Nitocris in Herodotus, or else his grandmother, as Maachah was to Asa, 1 King. 15.2,10,13. the wife of Nebuchadnezzar: which for age likely, was not before at the feast, but might well be then alive, that history in the fourth of Daniel being but one year or two before Nebuchadnezzar’s death, and came thither when she heard of these strange news.

*{by reason of the words}* Words of amazement among them, and fearful prognostications, as would be in such an ominous case; and these related to her.

*{came into}* Absent before from that unseasonable and immoderate feast; as best; became her age and matron-like gravity.

*{O King}* Chap. 2.4.3.9.

*{trouble thee}* Because all your wise men cannot satisfy you, in the reading and interpretation, vers. 8,9. a woman’s wisdom and counsel here outstrips, all the lords and wise men.

**V. 11.** *{there is a man}* Chap. 2.48.

*{in whom}* Chap. 4.8,9,18. She speaks as a heathen, yet retains a grateful and honorable remembrance of Daniel; Much better herein, than Pharaoh’s butler was to Joseph, Gen. 40.14,15,23. though upon a like occasion he makes a like mention of him for his own advantage, Gen. 41.9–13. she takes notice, and makes use of God’s gifts in others.

*{spirit of the holy gods}* God sometimes gives his servants such rare gifts, that the greatest of the heathen have them in admiration.

*{in the days}* In the fifth year of his reign. See *Annotations* on Chap. 2.1. And again, about the 36 year of his reign. See *Annotations* on Chap. 4.4.

*{thy father}* That is, grandfather Nebuchadnezzar. So again twice in this verse, and vers. 13. and 18. So, 2 Sam. 9.7. The acceptation is usual: and so the word Mother, 1 King. 15.2,10,13. is taken for grandmother; and son for grandchild, vers. 22. of this chapter: yea, Father signifies any ancestor; and Son any posterity whatsoever. So Abraham is called the father of all the Jews in Esay’s time, Esay 51.2. In our Savior’s time, Joh. 8.56. Luk. 1.73. And David is called the father of Josiah, 2 Chron. 34.2. and our Savior is called the son of David, and of Abraham, Mat. 1.1.

*{Master of the Magicians}* Chap. 4.9. See the note, Chap. 4.6. This declares that he used not those vile practices, because he was not among them when all were called.

**V. 12.** *{Dissolving}* Or, *of a dissolver.*

*{doubt}* Chald. *Knots,* that is, perplexed sentences, and obscure.

*{named Belteshazzar}* Chap. 1.7. likely when the kings that favored Daniel were dead, the Chaldeans were content that old Daniel, now above 90 years of age, was suffered to hold his own name, and to want that which they thought glorious, and he loathed.

*{he will—}* Such was her assured persuasion of Daniel.

**V. 13.** *{brought—}* Necessity and extremities drive the wicked to seek help of God’s servants.

*{art thou that Daniel?}* It seems the king had him now in no great regard, seeing he knew him not. Thus was it with Joseph, Exod. 1.8.

**V. 14.** *{Spirit of the Gods}* The idolaters thought that there were many gods, and that the Spirit of prophecy and understanding came of them.

**V. 15.** *{and now the wise men}* Vers. 8.

*{they could not shew the interpretation}* Read it, vers. 16. The vanity of those false arts betrays itself: and is acknowledged for such even by the maintainers of them.

**V. 16.** *{I have heard of thee}* Vers. 11,12. The gifts and graces of God in his child, makes them famous in the eyes of heathens, and sought to by them.

*{make interpretations}* Chald. *Interpret.*

*{thou shalt be clothed}* Vers. 7. Chap. 2.6.

*{third ruler in the Kingdom}* It is wisdom in princes to prefer wise men to great places of honor.

**V. 17.** *{Let thy gifts}* Himself being old, and knowing Belshazzar’s death at hand, he refuses his gifts, and honors, as neither useful to him, or the Church and people of God; not so, Chap. 2.48,49. yet he withstands not: but he suffers these things to be done unto him, vers. 29.

*{be to thyself}* Good men, receiving good parts from God, do not so much look at reward for them, as at honoring God by them; *freely ye have received, freely give*, Mat. 10.8.

*{yet I will read}* He fears not to do it, though the interpretation were so fatal to the king.

**V. 18.** *{O thou King}* Before he read the writing, he declares to the king his great ingratitude toward God, who could not be moved to give him the glory, considering his wonderful work toward his grandfather, and so shows, that he does not sin of ignorance, so much, as of malice.

*{god gave}* Chap. 2.37,38. & 4.22. *promotion cometh neither from, etc.* Psal. 75.6. *by me Kings reign, etc.* Prov. 8.15. *thou subduest the people under me*, Psal. 18.47. Rom. 13.1,2.

**V. 19.** *{trembled and feared}* Of fear due to magistrates, see Rom. 13.3,4,5,7.

*{whom he would he slew}* Much in this is tyranny: as some part is in that, 1 Sam. 8.11, etc.

**V. 20.** *{but when his heart}* Chap. 4.30. instead of thankfulness to God.

*{hardened in pride}* Or, *to deal proudly against God:* notwithstanding Daniel’s admonitions, Chap. 4.27. yet 12 months after, etc. vers. 29. These are high provoking sins, obstinacy in sin, hardness of heart, and pride.

*{he was deposed}* Chald. *made to come down,* Chap. 4.31, etc. when men abuse their greatness, God sometimes takes it away. Pride goes before a fall, Prov. 16.18. & 11.2.

**V. 21.** *{driven from the sons of men}* Chap. 4.23. Chald. *Enosh*. The names of *Adam* and *Enosh* are in Scripture the name of all their sons, Psalm 8.4.

*{his heart was made like}* Or, *he made his heart equal,* etc.

**V. 22.** *{Hast not humbled}* Applies the case to Belshazzar. Histories and examples in Scripture should be thus applied, Luk. 17.32. They should serve as looking-glasses; as hands in the margin of a book; as waymarks: they are seconds to laws and rules: yet some men’s hearts are not moved with God’s hand upon their nearest friends; as Belshazzar here.

*{though thou knewest all this}* This aggravated his sins more than Nebuchadnezzar’s; he having such an example before him, which the other wanted. Sins against conscience are most dangerous.

**V. 23.** *{But hast lifted up thyself}* As well as your grandfather, Esay 14.12,13,14. is applicable as much, if not more, to Belshazzar, than to Nebuchadnezzar; as he in whom the empire of Babylon ended: Daniel freely and boldly tells the king his sins: As Elias, John Baptist, and other Prophets did.

*{brought the vessels of his house}* Ver. 2,3. Sacrilege in detaining and abusing the holy vessels. This a grievous sin, Rom. 2.22. Act. 19.37. 2 Macc. 13.6. and to be reproved in the greatest king. See 2 Mac. 4.39,42.

*{have drank wine in them}* As if they were fitter for your house and table, than for God’s house and use. This was more than Nebuchadnezzar did.

*{praised the gods}* Ver. 4.

*{which see not}* Psal. 115.5, etc. Jer. 10.3, etc. no more than the gold or metals whereof they are made.

*{in whose hand thy breath is}* The Chaldee word signifies Spirit and Soul, Esay 42.5.

*{whose are all thy ways}* Who by his sovereign power rules all your actions, and issues; all events and enterprises. Him you have not praised and glorified, but dishonored.

**V. 24.** *{Then}* After that God had so long time deferred his anger, and patiently awaited for your amendment, ver. 5. and your iniquity was now ripe and full; as Gen. Chap. 15. ver. 16.

*{sent from him}* As a messenger of his vengeance; which though long, yet comes at last, and pays home, Psal. 34. ver. 16.

*{was written}* It is no illusion of the mind, or delusion of the sense of sight, but a real writing.

**V. 25.** *{Mene, mene}* This word is twice written for the certainty of the thing; as the dream was doubled, Gen. 41.32. showing that God had most surely numbered; implying also that God had appointed a term for all kingdoms, and that a miserable end shall come on all that raise themselves against him. The Trent Bible reads only, *Mane, Thekel, Phares.* The words are Chaldee. How the wise men should not be able to read them, see *Annotations* on vers. 8.

**V. 26.** *{God hath numbered}* Indeed he numbered and set the time, Jer. 25.12. And more particularly for Belshazzar’s case, thus; God has suffered you so long, and is now come to reckon with you about your administration; and having now cast up your accounts, he requires now payment of you: words borrowed from creditors, or merchants.

*{and finished it}* Both the number, and the kingdom. He it is, that sets the fatal periods of kingdoms and states, and of the lives of men. The stars do it not; nor can star-gazers foretell it.

**V. 27.** *{weighed}* As goldsmiths do, and merchants. The sense is, God has examined you: and thus one day shall all men be weighed and examined. Wisdom 11.20.

*{and are found wanting}* Wanting weight, too light: as light money wanting many grains of those regal virtues and qualifications requisite in a monarch, and so unworthy of the degree and place you hold. And such indeed are all men in themselves out of Christ, that is light and vain, Psal. 62. ver. 9.

**V. 28.** *{Peres}* Ver. 25. *upharsin,* they divided. Here, *Peres* in the singular number. God did it by the Medes and Persians.

*{divided}* Like false money cut in pieces: or, there is, or may be, an allusion between *Pereths* and Persia.

*{Medes and Persians}* To Darius the Mede, ver. 31. and to Cyrus the Persian, Chap. 6.28. Cyrus being the son of Mandane, who was the daughter of Astyages or Ahasuerus, and half sister to this Darius, otherwise called Cyaxares.

**V. 29.** *{Then commanded Belshazzar}* Terrified with his own daughter, and astonished at such divine skill in Daniel; both to be as good as his word, (as Herod, Mat. 14.9.) and to have such a man his hope and help in greatest need; he commands as follows. And Daniel as he did not desire them, ver. 17. so he would not reject them; but suffered them to be done unto him. Belshazzar knew not; nor could gather by Daniel’s interpretation, that so soon and suddenly, as that night, this judgment and execution should follow. It might have been true, and irrevocable, and yet delayed for a longer time.

**V. 30.** *{In that night}* Confer with this place the Scriptures, Isa. 13.17. & 21.2,4,5. & 47.11. Jer. 25.12,16. & 51.11,39. Hab. 2.5.

*{slain}* By Gobrias, and Gadatas, or Cadratas, chieftains of Cyrus: as Xenophon in his seventh book witnesses.

**V. 31.** *{Darius the Median}* To distinguish him from Darius the Persian, who was the third king after Cyrus, of whom mention is made, Ezra 4.5,24. This Darius the Mede, was also called Ahasuerus or Cyaxares the second, the son of Ahasuerus, Dan. 9.1. or Cyaxares the first, king of the Medes, the brother of Astyages. And Astyages his daughter Mandane, being niece to this Darius, was wife to Cambyses, a mean Persian, and mother to Cyrus as was formerly said. He by the consent and delivery up of Cyrus the conqueror, took the kingdom of Babylon: Or, as their joint forces won it; so they jointly reigned both together, as fellows in empire, 2 Chro. 36.20. But Darius is only here mentioned, because matter follows touching him only, not Cyrus, in the next chapter. And yet they seem both joined in the last verse of that chapter, and Ezra 6.14. And as Darius here receives the kingdom of Babylon of Cyrus; so Cyrus received after the daughter of Darius for wife, and the kingdom of Media for dowry with her. Xenophon in his 8 book of the Institution of Cyrus. Yet some make this Darius to be the son of Astyages, and brother of Mandane, and so uncle to Cyrus.

*{threescore and two years old}* Darius being 62 at the fall of Babel; which empire lasted 70 years, falls to be born at the eighth of Nebuchadnezzar, when he carried away captives the King Jeconiah, and all the nobles, and ten thousand valiant men, and all Jerusalem, and all saving the base of the land; and carried away all the treasure of the house of the Lord, and broke all the vessels of gold, which King Solomon had made in the Temple of the Lord. See 2 King. 24.13,15. even then Madai, who with Elam must revenge the cause of Judah, had a prince born: and to this purpose, Daniel seems to tell the king’s age, to show how God provided a remedy when he struck.

## CHAPTER 6

**Vers. 1.** *{It pleased}* Upon deliberation and consultation had about the settling, ordering, and well governing of this new gained monarchy. The Chaldee, wherein this chapter is written, is mixed with Arabic: which tongue the Persians here seem to have much used.

*{Darius}* See on Chap. 5.31. And this in the first year of his reign, ver. 28. He reigned but two years, and two months. And this fell in with the year of the world 3470, or little less. And this was a wonderful year; for Babel’s fall; lions’ humbleness, ver. 22. The Angel’s oration, Chap. 9. two heathen emperors’ Christian proclamations, ver. 26,27. Ezr. 1.1. and a general subsidy, as it were, over an hundred and twenty nations, for Judah’s return, Ezr. 1.4,6.

*{to set over}* Kings and monarchs must have under-officers, and princes to help in government, 1 King. 22.6. Esth. 11.13. Exod. 18.21, etc. Deut. 1.9–18. And yet themselves not be idle; but have enough to do besides, Rom. 12.8.

*{an hundred and twenty}* Likely there were 120 nations or provinces, Est. 1.1.127.

*{the whole kingdom}* Each one in his several share; for matters both of peace, and war: civil, and military.

**V. 2.** *{and over these}* Darius his wisdom, over a number of great governors, to appoint some few superiors and overseers.

*{Daniel was first}* And chiefest of the Triumvirs, vers. 3. Thus still is unity sought after, as best for peace: and parity avoided, as tending to division and confusion. Likely Belshazzar’s advancing of Daniel, and the cause of that, gave occasion to this advancement. For how could Darius otherwise do; Considering the great fame of Daniel’s wisdom, worth, and integrity, both in Babylon, above all wise men there, and in foreign nations, Ezek. 28.3. and his preferments thereupon by Nebuchadnezzar; and newly by Belshazzar himself, though he received that heavy doom from Daniel’s mouth, and withal foretelling from God this great monarchy to the Medes and Persians; but in all reason be moved thereupon thus to prefer him: to do as much at least, as Belshazzar himself had done. See Gen. 41.38,38,49,40. The eminency of God’s graces in his servants thus prefers themselves. And happy is that kingdom, where preferments are bestowed according to men’s worth and deserts.

*{give accounts}* Luk. 16.2. as we all must to God one day, Rom. 14.12. 1 Pet. 4.5.

*{and the King}* And to the kingdom in him. He the less care; and the kingdom the less hurt by mal-administration.

**V. 3.** *{Then}* The original here to some seems to be our language.

*{was preferred}* This is God’s mercy to his Church in providing a man for their countenance, and comfort. And so was Joseph in Egypt. And this was the cause or occasion of the envy and malice of the presidents and princes against him. For thus envy works, Eccl. 4.4. for the chief priests delivered Jesus to Pilate, Mar. 15.10. Thus self-love, and pride, envying at the worth of others, and at their honor and happiness above their own, sets itself on work to do mischief. Even Rachel envied her own sister: and Joseph’s brethren envied him. See Prov. 14.30. Gal. 5.21,26.

*{an excellent spirit}* Of knowledge, wisdom, government, integrity.

*{the King thought}* Likely further to make him a Viceroy; as Joseph was, Gen. 41.40–45.

*{whole Realm}* Many, if not most interpreters, understand this, of Media; as if Darius carried him thither. But we rather take it to be meant of Babylon, where Darius and Daniel now were: which being newly conquered needed most such a governor, and wherein Daniel had still been so well versed and experienced. Neither does anything in the text make the least show against it. This further intended advancement of Daniel, if known to the Presidents and Princes, did likely set them further on, to envy him, and seek occasions against him.

**V. 4.** *{Then the presidents and princes}* Here is a unity in conspiracy, unity among wicked men, is often dangerous to good men, Joh. 9.22.

*{sought to find occasion}* Thus courtiers out of their own ambition, are envious at the preferments of others: especially of strangers. And generally the wicked cannot abide the graces of God in others; but seek and search by all occasions to deface them, and detract from them. See *Annotations* on Chap. 2.13. Therefore against such assaults there is no better remedy then to walk uprightly in the fear of God, faithfully and diligently in our callings, and to keep a good conscience, Tit. 2.9,10. Act. 24.16.

*{concerning the kingdom}* *Ex latere Regis,* says the Trent Bible, contrary to the Chaldee, and all translators, and all good sense. The meaning is, concerning the affairs of the kingdom, and his administration of them in his high office.

*{none occasion}* Subject matter of accusation; no color of suspicion of negligence, or unfaithfulness in him, or his doings.

*{faithful}* To the King, in the affairs of the kingdom: as he was also to his God.

*{neither was there any error}* A singular example of prudence, industry, and faithfulness in our callings, and offices of highest trust: that so many, and mighty, envious and subtle enemies, in all their deep devices, yet despaired to find him faulty, or colorably faulty, in his management of so great affairs in so high an office.

**V. 5.** *{we shall not}* Innocence wins testimony of its adversaries, and forces it from them.

*{except we find}* In his life and manners they could not. In his religion they made account they should: knowing that Daniel would lose honors, and offices, and life also, rather then offend the law of his God: which makes much also to Daniel's commendation. And shows withal, that wicked men's consciences tell them, that good men are constant in their religion, and will not transgress the laws of it.

**V. 6.** *{Then these Presidents}* Not only the other triumvirs, but the 120 Princes or Presidents, if we may believe their own words, verse 7. Thus the wicked are restless, and their hatred implacable against the godly. If they cannot for their faults, they will for their virtues attach them; and persecute them for their piety.

*{assembled together}* Or, *came tumultuously.* The Chaldee term of Daniel is also Hebrew, and from Psal. 2.1. *wherefore did the Heathen rage tumultuously, or keep a stir?* The Popish translation of *surrexerunt,* is not so proper; some of themselves do seek to help it, by substituting *surrepserunt* instead of it. And stick not to charge their Trent Bible, or at least the edition of it, with many the like errors. See Pintus on this place. These Presidents here leave and neglect their places and charges, to assemble and plot, and all against Daniel. Innocence cannot be free.

**V. 7.** *{all the Presidents}* They pretend an universal, and unanimous consent of all, thereby the rather to win the King's consent, and as it were tumultuously, and by joint importunity to enforce him upon it. But they speak more then truth was. For Daniel the chief was none of their council. Nor were all here spoken of by them, cast with their families into the Lions' Den, verse 24. For it may be many of them knew not the mischievous plot of the contrivers, and inventors of it; but were drawn on among the rest; as those, 2 Sam. 15.11.

*{to establish}* To present it, and to obtain from the King the confirmation; that so it might receive the force of a law. For he had the negative voice, and sole power of making it a law and statute. So appears, verse 8.

*{a royal Statute}* In Chaldee, *a Statute for the King.* The pretense of projectors, and evil counselors, is the honor, or benefit of the King: but they aim at their own ends; though it be in the ruin of the subjects. They flatter the King; but such flattery and adulation of Kings, is the plague sore of Kings, and kingdoms.

*{Decree} Or, interdict.*

*{that whosoever shall ask}* The most probable pretense of this decree, is, that by this means, Darius might have occasion to show his royal grace and bounty, and his divine munificence, to all his subjects that petitioned him; so to gain to himself, in his new kingdom, the more love, strength and assurance. But their drift is against Daniel, for their own ends, and to the King's hurt: knowing Daniel's conscience and custom, and constancy in prayer to his God; which for any danger, upon any pretense, he would not forego. This it was, that made them fix upon this particular decree.

*{of any God}* They will rather do dishonor to their own gods, then not satisfy their own ambitious, envious, bloody mind, in taking him out of the way, that stood in their light, as they conceived, and stopped the way of their greater advancements.

*{or man}* Many of his subjects, in his large empire, might stand in that time, in extreme present need of man's help, where the King could not be present; nor they possibly have access to him, which yet, by this decree, should be unjustly, inhumanely, and foolishly barred from asking help of God or man.

*{for thirty days}* This was time enough for their purpose. They knew Daniel would not so long abstain, from making petition to his God.

*{cast into the Den of Lions}* Bloody minded men, no less will serve them. They must suffer the Lions' cruel paw, and hungry jaws, and be devoured of them. Thus adulation and flattery, is a nurse of tyranny. This kind of capital punishment, was not unusual among the Babylonians, the Medes and Persians; and among the Romans also, with whom it was a common saying, in Tertullian's time, Let the Christians be cast to the Lions.

**V. 8.** *{Now, O King}* Thus good Kings, may be bought and sold by evil counselors. Thus Haman dealt with Ahasuerus, Esth. 3.9. Kings had therefore need to be the more wary and circumspect.

*{that it be not changed, according}* Esth. 1.19. and 8.8. Therefore Ahasuerus would, or could not recall the decree against Vashti, Chap. 1. nor against the Jews, Chap. 3. but provides for himself, and for them another way, Chap. 2.1,2. and Chap. 8.11. These Presidents and Princes, here, some way flatter Darius, (partner with Cyrus in the conquest of Babylon, and now King of it) in asking him, being King of Media, to make a law, here in Babylon, after the manner of the law of the Medes.

*{according to the Law}* Esth. 1.19. and 8.8.

*{altereth not}* Chald. *passeth not.*

**V. 9.** *{Darius signed}* Too rashly and suddenly; ascribing too much trust to his Princes and Presidents, who indeed, ought thoroughly to have weighed the nature of the decree; and did best know, the natures and conditions of the several provinces, and people, how they would brook it. And thereupon he signed it; with due examination, by himself, of the right and nature, and end of the decree. It seemed indeed, to set him up, in some sort as a kind of god, or demi-god: For, as an Heathen could say,

*Qui fingit sacros auro vel marmore vultus,*

*Non facit ille Deos, qui rogat, ille facit.*

Mart. lib. 8. Epigr. 24.

And therefore, also the rather, he readily, out of self-love, signs; not searching into their wicked end in proposing it.

**V. 10.** *{Now when Daniel knew}* After it was signed; not before else he would have labored to have prevented it.

*{He went into his house}* Withdrew himself from the sight of public impieties, to the exercise of domestic worship, and service of God.

*{and his windows being open}* Not in hypocrisy, for vainglory; the thing forbidden, Mat. 5.6. but to show his resolution and constancy, against the fear of the King's decree, though he knew it to be irrevocable; and that he would not, by his silence seem to give consent unto it, or comply with it; or forego his daily duty to his God; for which he had so many obligations lying upon him.

*{in his Chamber}* Or, *upper room;* as Act. 1.13. & 10.9. See 2 King 23.12. Not in a low and obscure place. He did not fear to be seen at this time, upon this occasion.

*{toward Jerusalem}* In conscience of his duty, and with faith in the promise, 1 King. 8.33,34,44,48. Psalm. 5.7. Though now, city and Temple, lay in ruins. Now the case is altered; as Joh. 4.21,23.

*{he kneeled}* 1 King. 8.54. Act. 7.60. & 20.36. The usual, humble, seemly gesture of prayer. Indeed standing also, is used in Scripture; and allowed, Mar. 11.25. And so that gesture seems to be used, 1 Sam. 1.26. 1 King. 8.22,55. Neh. 9.5. Mat. 6.5. And according thereunto, are those words of Naaman, 2 King. 5.11. And in the primitive Church, by ancient tradition, standing at prayer was used, if not enjoined, on every Lord's day, and in all the time, between Easter day and Ascension day, and Whitsunday also. And with more reason then, when Christ's resurrection and ascension, were so much questioned, then can be now, *Justin. q. 115. ad Orthodoxos:* Yet kneeling, or prostration upon the ground, are most usual and seemly in prayer. And unseemly it seems to be, to do it, either sitting, or with the head covered in our assemblies; as these gestures and rites, are now in esteem with us; unless some extraordinary, and just cause do warrant the contrary.

*{three times a day}* Morning, Noon, and Evening; before he went forth, or set upon his public employment, and state affairs; at his return to dinner; as Gen. 43.16. and after the end of the day, and dispatch of his business. To which times, David seems to have reference, Psal. 55.17. and may seem to be the three times of the daily prayers of the Jews. The morning and evening seem to be so ascertained, by their appointed daily sacrifices, and the daily burning of incense, on the golden Table of Incense, at those times, Exod. 29.39,42. and 30.7,8. And punctually, the morning time, is deemed the third hour, which is our ninth. When the Holy Ghost also came down upon the Apostles in cloven tongues of fire, Act. 2.15. and the evening time is the ninth hour, which is our third in the afternoon; called *the hour of prayer*, Act. 3.1. When Peter and John went into the Temple; and Cornelius prayed, and saw the vision, Act. 10.3,30. When also our Savior was crucified and died, Mat. 27.46,50. Mar. 15.34,37. The middle is the middle hour, their sixth, our twelfth, or midday, Joh. 4.6. When Peter prayed, Act. 10.9. When also our Savior was condemned, and delivered to be crucified, Joh. 19.14. When also the general darkness began, and continued till the ninth hour, till our Savior died, Mat. 27.45. Mar. 15.33. Luk. 23.44. And in the primitive Church, the Christians had those three hours of the day, in so much use and regard for prayer, as they came to be called, Canonical hours. But yet from this duty, no time is exempted, Psal. 119. verse 62. & verse 164. Luk. 18.1. Ephes. 6.18. 1 Thess. 5.17.

*{prayed and gave thanks}* Phil. 4.6. 1 Thess. 5.17,18. For prayed, the Trent Bible has, worshiped; erroneously.

*{as he had aforetime}* No danger can keep good men, from the accustomed works of piety, Act. 4.19. & 5.29. He was not now, either more slow, or secret in it.

**V. 11.** *{Then these men}* The practice of wicked men; as Psal. 10.8,9. curiously prying into other men's doings, and not seeing beams in their own eyes. They thought now they had won the day, and gained their desires.

**V. 12.** *{Then they came near}* They speedily pursue their mischievous, and bloody purpose.

*{concerning the King’s Decree}* Of that first, to renew the strength of it; out of the King's own mouth: to circumvent, and entangle him in his own words. Thus cunningly they deal; pretending only the defense and maintenance of the law, and the King's authority. Flattering him to his own hurt, and for their own sanguinary design, in Daniel's ruin.

**V. 13.** *{that Daniel}* Not till now, they open their mean drift, and directly accuse. And now they think they may be bold to do it: and they do it with all aggravations; that Daniel; so desiring to speak contemptuously of him.

*{of the captivity}* To render him despicable, from his slavish, and sordid state of captivity, and from his nation of the Jews. They had many captives of other nations also among them, at that time; as may be gathered out of Jer. 25. and 27. Chapters.

*{regardeth not thee}* So Chap. 3.12. Under a color, they falsely and maliciously slander Daniel of ingratitude, for his honors, of contempt and rebellion. They accuse him, as the Jews did Christ, as being an enemy to Cæsar.

*{his petition three times a day}* They say not to his God; lest that might sound anything for him. But indeed they charge that upon him, as his heinous capital offense. Thus piety, prayer and devotion, are wrested to a wrong meaning, by the wicked, and counted for crimes. Now the ungrateful, and unpleasing conclusion of all these premises they conceal, the death of Daniel; or leave that to the King, to be gathered, and made by him, as necessarily following thereupon.

**V. 14.** *{then the King}* Seeing their fraud and malice; and his own error and circumvention. These evils, Kings, otherwise good, are subject unto, by evil courtiers.

*{sore displeased with himself}* For making so rashly such a decree, so injurious to God and man, so full against the life of Daniel, and against himself, in the loss of such a subject. And no doubt, displeased also with these accusers of Daniel, for their so fraudulent, mischievous, and pernicious dealing against him and Daniel. These sore grievances, by such means, Kings, many times, are put unto.

*{and set his heart}* This was well; to think how to amend, what he had done amiss. And better were it to repeal such decrees, then to pretend to the maintenance of his authority, by the maintaining of them. But best it had been, to have been more advised, circumspect and cautious, ere he had made them.

*{till the going down}* He was sad and serious in it, to save the life of innocent and precious Daniel. A long time was this, (for they were at the King, no doubt, betimes) wherein with all his heart and might he labored to outmaster their malice, to wind himself out of their mischievous wiliness, to interpret, qualify, or nullify the decree, and his signing of it. A good example for Christian Kings in like cases.

**V. 15.** *{Then these men assembled}* Here they set upon the King, with multitude, and unanimous consent to overbear him.

*{Know, O King}* They press on him with all instance: they wax bold, if not impudent, notwithstanding the King's sore displeasure, Prov. 16.14. Thus far envy carries them: and mischief begun, must be carried on, whatsoever comes on it; though it be to their future undoing. They pretend, now, the right of law, and the King's honor and authority in it. Thus are Kings many times overruled and abused.

*{may be changed}* Thus the wicked maintain evil laws, by color of constancy and authority; which is oft times either ignorance or stubbornness; when as the innocents thereby perish: and therefore governors neither ought to fear, nor be ashamed to break such; though good laws with all constancy are to be maintained. Thus Charidemus lost his life, Diod. Sic. l. 14.

**V. 16.** *{Then the King commanded}* He perseveres not in his good purpose; but against his conscience he yields, and becomes a servant and minister of their cruelty. Such slaves sometimes are Kings forced by bad servants to do things they like not, 2 Sam. 3.39. And so unsafe is it for favorites to trust in them.

*{and they brought Daniel}* Not to answer for himself, and plead his cause, and make his apology; for absent and unheard was he condemned already. But they brought him to execution; and that speedily, lest any way by delays, he might happen to escape. So swift are their feet to shed innocent blood.

*{the den of lions}* See *Annotations* on verse 7.

*{The King said}* To comfort Daniel; and clear his innocence.

*{thy God}* He says, not my god. So verse 20.

*{whom thou servest continually}* A royal testimony of Daniel's piety, and innocence of his faith, and constancy in God's service, against the terrors of death. And that the King also approved of what Daniel had done in his daily prayer and service of his God.

*{continually}* Notwithstanding my decree and edict to the contrary.

*{he will deliver thee}* It is his wish and prayer; not prophecy. He can; I cannot. It is hope; hearing belike how God did deliver his three companions out of the fiery furnace, Chap. 3. Thus God bows the minds of Heathen in compassion towards his; makes them acknowledge the omnipotence of the true God; the impotency of themselves, and their false gods, verse 26,27.

**V. 17.** *{and a stone}* Comparing this and the 25 verse together, they may seem to discern that the Lions did not straight seize upon Daniel: and therefore would have a stone sealed, &c. See Mar. 27.66. and Lam. 3.53.

*{the King sealed it}* Having once begun to yield to his Lords, they drew him on, and he proceeds to yield further, Esth. 3.12. and 8.8. 1 King. 21.8.

*{signet of his Lords}* Lest the King out of his love to Daniel, his sorrow for him, and care of him, should have him brought out; or lest the King's friends should do it, or Daniel's countrymen the Jews; or his other followers and friends, as he had many, by his eminency in gifts and graces, by his esteem with the King, by his fidelity, justice, piety, &c. Thus they show their distrust in the King; (a just reward for him) and their trust in their own carnal wisdom, which yet deceives them.

*{that the purpose}* The Trent Bible here wrongly translates the Hebrew text, and varies the sense.

**V. 18.** *{Then the King went}* Late, verse 14. and sad; through his love to Daniel, and the guiltiness of his own conscience, that accuses him, Rom. 2.14,15. and stings him; though he had no judge on earth above him. This is well in the King, and it is a good work of his conscience, thus to affect him: whereas Daniel's accusers had their consciences seared, and hardened in sin, to their final ruin.

*{fasting: neither——}* Fasting and watching agree well together: and sorrow, and fasting justly exclude music, and all mirth.

**V. 19.** *{then the King arose}* The workings of love, and of a guilty conscience, suffer him not to sleep, or rest.

**V. 20.** *{with a lamentable voice}* Thereby speaking his lamentation and sorrow; his love and care, and fear of Daniel. The tenderest father could have done no more towards his dearest son. And this is so much the more in so great a Monarch: which commends both him, and Daniel's worth. Against hardhearted men at the miseries of the godly, Rom. 12.15. Hebr. 13.3.

*{Is thy God}* This shows some hope in Darius, of Daniel's safety, and God's power and mercy; though mixed with much fear.

**V. 21.** *{then said Daniel}* With a voice not distressed, as that of the King's.

*{live for ever}* Chap. 2.4.

**V. 22.** *{hath sent}* He acknowledges his God the author of his deliverance. It is the right hand of the most High that saves Paul from the mouth of the Lion; Peter from prison, &c.

*{his Angel}* They are God's ministers, Heb. 1.14.

*{shut the Lions’ mouths}* The glorious presence of the Angel might strike fear into the Lions: so Balaam's ass was afraid of the Angel, Num. 22.27. and 33. or the Angel might otherwise, and that many ways, restrain the Lions' rage, Heb. 11.33. Daniel is understood to do it; as likewise the like is said of his three companions, verse 34. They did it; as Elias brought the famine, Jam. 5.17,18.

*{forasmuch}* Thus God sometimes by miracle shows and proves the innocence of his servants, Psal. 37.5,6.

*{innocency was found}* In this particular of my behavior, towards you and your decree. So Psal. 18.20. He speaks not of the point of his justification from sin before God, Phil. 3.9. He minds the innocence of his present case and cause: not yet that this innocence merited this miraculous deliverance; no proper true merit can be in man towards God, but that God saved him of his mere mercy, and gracious good pleasure. See 1 Tim. 1.8. 2 Thess. 1.5,7.

*{and also before thee}* Though my accusers charge me, that I regard not you nor your authority; for our obedience to Kings and Magistrates, must be only in God, and for God. No other Active Obedience is due unto them.

*{no hurt}* Even in your own judgment, ver. 14,16. For he did disobey the King's wicked Commandment, to obey God; and so did no injury to the King, who ought to command nothing, whereby God should be dishonored.

**Verse 23.** *{King exceeding glad}* Rid of that heavy plight wherein he was, ver. 18. and cheered up his Spirit and conscience wonderfully in such a manner of deliverance, of so precious a subject. More spiritual was the joy of the Church, at the deliverance of Peter and John out of the Prison, Act. 4.24, &c.

*{take Daniel up}* Not staying, it seems, for those Lords whose Seals were upon the stones; nor for the coming of the accusers. For no doubt he had witnesses enough, of the thing with him: and the preservation itself was an ocular demonstration of the miracle: and the view of it raised up Darius his spirit to the height of Courage and resolution; without them to amend what he had done amiss; being also that the sentence of the Decree against Daniel was already satisfied.

*{believed in his God}* Because he committed himself wholly unto his God, whose cause he did defend, he hoped, trusted, was assured by his promises, that nothing but good could come unto him, whether by life or death; wherein we see the power of Faith, and the Nature of it. Heb. 11.33. and throughout that whole Chapter. In which grace of Faith in Christ, the Covenant of the Gospel on man's part does wholly consist. And therefore so many, and so great things are spoken of Belief & Faith in Scripture.

**Verse 24.** *{and the King commanded}* The absolute power of this King. The like frequent in Scripture. His just wrath was so incensed; the miracle so wonderful, and the guiltiness of the malefactors so evident, that without any process or further condemnation, he commands their execution.

*{accused Daniel}* See note, Chap. 3.8.

*{cast them into the den of lions}* This is a terrible example against all the wicked, which do against their conscience make cruel laws to destroy the children of God; and also admonishes Princes how to punish such when their wickedness is come to light; though not in every point, or with like circumstances; yet to execute true Justice upon them. This just on God's part by the law of Retaliation. Psal. 7.15,16. and 9.15,16.

*{their children, and their wives,}* The like or more, we may read of Achan and his family, Josh. 7.24,25. Haman was hanged up, and his sons, Esth. 9.14. By the law of Nature, and Nations, Rebels are punished, also in their wives, children, and posterity, though not with death; yet considering that law, Deut. 24.16. with that 2 Chr. 25.4. either Darius seems here to be unjustly cruel, or these wives and children were accessories to the crime of their husbands, and parents.

*{brake all their bones}* Persecutors oft-times have bloody ends. Here was no stopping of the Lions mouths, no taming of their rage and cruelty, no overmastering them.

*{or ever they came at the bottom}* This adds luster to Daniel's miraculous deliverance, that it was not for want of hunger, and Natural ferocity that the Lions spared Daniel; but that it was God's hand overruling them: as we see God's hand in ruling that Lion, 1 King. 13.24,28,29. all God's creatures are at his hest, to do his will; for reward or punishment. That fancy, that the Accusers alleged the Lions to have been full of food, and therefore for that space of time to have spared Daniel; and that the King thereupon should have them fed, and thereafter have the Accusers cast in to them, is a foolish fiction without any ground.

**Verse 25.** *{Then Darius}* This marvelous work of God had a marvelous good effect in the heart of Darius. God's works should not be slightly passed over; but accordingly made use of, Psal. 92.4,5. and 83.4. Of this Darius, see *Annotations* on Chap. 5.31. Many Kings of the Medes and Persians were after him called by this name; as the Heathen Histories of times do declare. There was after this first Monarch Darius the son of Hystaspes, Darius Artaxerxes Longimanus, Darius Nothus, Darius Artaxerxes Ochus, Darius Codomannus, whom Alexander the great conquered. And the sacred Histories of the books of Ezra, Nehemiah, and Esther, fall within these times; Begin with the beginning of the Persian Monarchy, Ezra 1.1. and reach to the final period of it by Alexander then Great Greek, in the H. P. Jaddua's time, Neh. 12.11.

*{wrote unto all the people}* Chap. 3.19. & 4.1.

*{languages}* More now than those, Chap. 2. verse 4. and 4.1.

*{in all the earth}* All in his dominion and lands. So expounded, ver. 26. So Luk. 2.1. Ezra 1.1,2.

*{peace}* Chap. 4. See the *Annotations* there. This word in the Hebrew, and Chaldee, and all languages, comprehends all outward prosperity; and worldly felicity. and therefore is so usual in all salutations and greetings.

**Verse 26.** *{I make a decree}* Chap. 3.29. a good example to Christian Kings.

*{tremble and fear}* God makes heathen Princes sometime to acknowledge his power. See Chap. 3.29. The true fear of God is the fountain of wisdom, Prov. 1.7. & 9.10. the warder of the sluice for all sin. It comprehends the whole worship and service of God, all acts of Religion.

*{the God of Daniel}* His God, in a peculiar way, whom only, and no other, Daniel owns, worships, serves, obeys, and who thus owns, honors, and defends Daniel.

*{the living God}* Which has not only life in himself, but is the only fountain of life, and quickens all things; so that without him there is no life, 1 Sam. 2.6. Act. 17.25.

*{which shall not be destroyed}* Chap. 2.44. & 4.3,34. & 7.14,27. Luk. 1.33. Mic. 4.7.

**Verse 27.** *{He delivereth}* God to be acknowledged, and praised in his works. The Psalms are full of it.

**Verse 28.** *{prospered}* Flourished in his state and honors; in offices and employments; in spite of the envy and malice of all wicked men. He prospered, as Joseph, Hezekiah, Mordecai, and others, Psal. 1.3. when as the wicked prosper not; as Coniah, Jer. 22.30. but are holden with the cords of their own sin, Prov. 5.22. Psal. 7.16. and 9.16. Prov. 22.8. Of their prospering for a while, see Job 21.7–14. Jer. 12.1. Psal. 73.2–12.

*{of Cyrus the Persian}* Chap. 1.21. Thus God provides men in honor, for the comfort of his Church in her calamity.

## CHAPTER 7

**Verse 1.** *{In}* Now Daniel proceeds in his Narration from Histories, in the former part, to prophecies in the latter half of this book; Therein foretelling the succession of times, and of the Kingdoms of the world; and what things therein should befall the Church of God, the people of the Jews, until the time of the coming of Christ (numbered up in him) and the time of the promulgation of the Gospel.

*{the first year of Belshazzar}* Chald. *one year.* So Chap. 1.1. and 2.1. That Daniel might join together Babel's stories, and such as did depend upon them without interruption, therefore he displaces the two visions which now follow; the one, in the first of Belshazzar of Babel's fall, Chap. 7. the other, in the third of Belshazzar, of the Persians fall, Chap. 8. Both which in order of time go before the fifth Chapter. Of this Belshazzar, see Chap. 5.1.

*{Daniel had a dream}* Chald. *saw a dream.* Different in vision; but of the same matter and argument, with that of Nebuchadnezzar, Chap. 2, interpreted by Daniel there: only what is there more generally shadowed out, is set forth in succeeding times, more particularly, with many circumstances, in this Chapter, and in those which follow. This dream was 70 years after that dream of Nebuchadnezzar, Chap. 2. If we reckon 30 years to the reign of Evilmerodach, and Daniel now was near one hundred years old.

*{and visions of his head}* Visions in his dream. Not that these were distinct from his dream. Visions and Oracles were the two ways, by which God revealed his Will to his Prophets, Num. 12.6,7,8. Visions were vouchsafed to them, sometimes waking, as Zach. 1.8, &c. and Chap. 3.1. Jer. 1.11,13. sometimes sleeping in dreams, Job 33.15. So Gen. 28.12. Act. 2.17. So here to Daniel: presenting not to the external sense, but internally to the imagination and phantasy, visible things, as representments of God's Will and pleasure signified thereby, Dan. 4.5.

*{head}* The shop and seat of dreams and visions.

*{upon his bed}* Where he slept and dreamed in the night, ver. 2. a place and time fit for such kind of Revelations.

*{then he wrote the dream}* He the Writer of this book; and no other: and he wrote this Dream; left it not to be told only by tradition; but in a surer way wrote it, by God's inspiration and instinct, 2 Pet. 1.19,20,21. And that because it did not concern him alone, but the whole Church in after ages; to confirm and comfort them against the great calamities that were to come. For whereas the people of Israel looked for continual quietness after these seventy years, as Jeremiah seemed to them to have declared; he shows that this rest shall not be a deliverance from all troubles, but a beginning; and therefore encourages them to look for a continual affliction till the Messiah be revealed, by whom they should have a spiritual deliverance, and all the promises fulfilled; whereof they should have a certain token in the destruction of the Babylonian Kingdom. See 1 Cor. 10. ver. 11.

*{and told the sum of the matters}* Chald. *the head of the words.* Verse 16. He wrote and told them, to wit, to his people; because for their instruction and consolation, against persecutors to come, God showed that dream to Daniel. The chief heads therefore and material points, for the use of the Church, he relates and records in these sacred Commentaries.

**Verse 2.** *{Daniel spake and said}* The Scripture then is not a dumb and dead letter: but speaks; and therein we hear the Prophets and Apostles speaking; and so fittest to teach all truth, and convince all Error, and to be the judge in controversies of Religion, 2 Tim. 3.16,17.

*{by night}* In time of silence and sequestration, from worldly affairs: so, usually. And yet sometimes by day; as Acts 10.9,10. & 22.17. See Psal. 16.7.

*{the four winds}* Here some, with more curiosity and boldness, than modesty and soundness, by the winds, will understand Angels, and some of them good Angels, the guardians of these Empires, whereto they apply that, Chap. 10.13,20,21. & Chap. 11.1. & Chap. 12.1. Others, understand bad Angels: But we rest in the letter of the Dream, and understand only the mighty concussions, stirs, tumults and troubles, that were the rise and original of these several Empires. And therefore of these winds, the Angel in his interpretation to Daniel, makes not any mention, but only of the Empires themselves. By the winds then blowing, we only conceive those horrible troubles, afflictions and mutations, whereby one Empire is exalted, and another abased; as the waves of the sea are, by the boisterous winds: especially, if all the four winds at once, blow together upon it. Yet all sent, and ruled by God's providence.

*{strove}* One against another, to trouble the sea: *imo miscentes æquora fundo, Vastos volvuntque ad littora fluctus.*

*{great sea}* Midland sea. So called, in relation to the Dead sea, and the Sea of Tiberias: which were but Lakes in comparison of it. Yet in sacred Idiom, the great vessel itself, for water, in the Court of Solomon's Temple, is called a Sea, 2 Chron. 4.2,10. By the Sea, here, is signified the world, ver. 17.

**Verse 3.** *{beasts}* Empires deciphered in this manner; because the acquiring of them is not gotten, nor the possession of them maintained, nor the government of them exercised, but by brutish violence and tyranny.

*{came up}* Not altogether, but in process of time, successively, as the verses following do declare.

*{the Sea}* Not calm, but troubled, and tossed thus with tempests.

*{divers one from another}* So figured before in the divers metals of Nebuchadnezzar's Image, Chap. 2. and so described diversely, in the verses following.

**Verse 4.** *{the first}* The golden head, Dan. 2.38. and the great tree, Dan. 4.20,21,22. is the power of Babel, the first Empire, in Daniel's book. This in its best and flourishing estate.

*{a lion}* The Trent Bible has *a Lioness*: contrary to the Chaldee and Hebrew; and in a manner all Translators. The Lion here represents the Empire of the Chaldeans, in regard of his power, cruelty, and capacity, Isa. 5.29. Jer. 4.7.

*{Eagle’s wings}* As the Lion is the King of Beasts, so the Eagle is the King of Birds. Both make to magnify this Monarchy, both for courage, power, and speed. Wings here point out the great celerity of Nebuchadnezzar's coming to his power and possession of kingdoms; as if the Babylonians had wings to fly, Isa. 5.26. Jer. 4.13. & 48.40. & 49.19,22. Lam. 4.19. Ezek. 17.3. Hab. 1.6.8. So Ashur has wings, Esay 8.8.

*{plucked}* These Eagles wings were great, long; full of feathers, of divers colors, Ezek. 17.3. But now Daniel, in this vision foresees them plucked. Armies and Provinces, fell from them; the Lydians, the Medes and Persians, that had formerly obeyed and served them, were risen up against them, and so plucked their wings, that they went on their feet, and were made like other men. This was their declining estate; and at last fulfilled, under *Belshazzar*. See, Jer. 51.30–64.

*{and it was lifted}* Or, *Wherewith it was lifted above the common sort of other men*. Though some translate and understand, as if it were taken away from the face of the earth, and perished.

*{made stand}* Compelled to stand: and made to understand its own fragility and imbecility, and the world's inconstancy.

*{a man’s heart}* This has not reference to *Nebuchadnezzar*, Chap. 4.16. This respects not any singular person, but the State of the whole Empire; that of *Nebuchadnezzar* was past; this to come, in the fall of the Empire. When they came to have a man's heart, after their wings were plucked, that is, a soft, fearful, broken, and dejected heart. So Cyrus dealt with them, & abased them; as Xenophon shows, in his seventh Book of the Institution of Cyrus. Then their Lion-like heart, that insolent proud heart, Chap. 3.15. Esay 14.13. was gone. Now they well knew themselves to be but men, Psal. 9.20.

**Verse 5.** *{a second}* This is omitted in the Trent Bible.

*{a Bear}* Meaning the Empire of the *Persians*, Dan. 2.39. & 8.3. This follows the Lion. And so the Lion and Bear, are often joined together in Scripture; as Prov. 28.15. Hos. 13.8. Amos 5.19. Lam. 3.10. Some here say, the Persian Empire is represented by the Bear, because Persia, a mountainous country, much abounds in Bears; and those of a crueler kind.
