The blessing of the high sacrifice is in the same speech, Numb. 6.25. Psalm 80.7,19.

*{upon thy sanctuary}* Exod. 15.17. Psal. 51.18.

*{That is desolate}* A place of briers and thorns; an habitation of wolves and owls. This the godly do most bemoan, Lam. 1.4,10. and 2.1,6,7. and 4.1. and 5.18. Esay 63.15,18. and 64.10,11.

*{for the Lord’s sake}* *For your own sake*: or, as some others, for the promised Messiah and Mediator's sake; whom God has made *both Lord and Christ*, Acts 2.36. Luk. 1.43. and 2.11. for his merit and mediation sake. And so some expound that, Psal. 80.15,17. and 84.9. and 2 Sam. 7.21. with 1 Chron. 17.19. and that, 2 Sam. 12.25. The phrases *for David's sake*, 1 King. 11.12,13. and 15.4. 2 King. 19.34. Psal. 132.10. and *for Jerusalem's sake*, 1 King. 11.13. import only thus much, for God's free promise sake, made to David, and concerning Jerusalem. Daniel pleads here no merits of their own. Their merits were demerits.

**Verse 18.** *{Incline thine ear}* Daniel's Hebrew has the very letters of Hezekiah's prayer, Esay 37.17. His earnestness in prayer; that God will mercifully both see and hear.

*{which is called by thy name}* Heb. *whereupon thy name is called.* So Esay 4.1. and 63.19. and so Gen. 48.16. the same expression in the same sense is used.

*{present our supplications}* Heb. *cause to fall.* So Jer. 38.26. and 42.2,9. 2 Sam. 2.22. Ezra 9.6. Luk. 18.13. Noting out their humility, and abasing themselves with fear and care in prayer.

*{our righteousnesses}* Declaring that the godly fly only unto God's mercies, and renounce their own works, when they seek for remission of their sins. So Psal. 25.6. and 51.1. and 69.16. and 143.1,2. Esay 63.7. and 64.10, &c. Jer. 14.7. David, Ezekiah, and Nehemiah sometimes plead their righteousness, innocency, and integrity, the better to prevail in their prayers; but not for the merit of them: but thereby to strengthen their faith in prayer, that they are such persons, and so qualified by God's free grace, as to whom God promises freely to hear their prayers. Against popish merits alleged in their popish prayers.

**Verse 19.** *{O Lord, hear}* His fervency in prayer; and growing greater towards the latter end, Jam. 5.16.

*{for thy own sake}* Psal. 57.5,11. and 79.9,10,13. and 83.18. and 115.1. God's free grace, and glory, the chief argument of our faith in prayer.

*{for thy city and thy people are called by thy Name}* In Hebrew, *for thy name is called upon thy city, and upon thy people;* that is, they are, and profess themselves to be yours, and do bear the badges and marks of it. The Jews at this day repeat on their expiation feast, this prayer often, in *Cetber Malcuth,* a book of their common prayers; but stop their ears against the Angel's word from God, touching Christ, the true worker of this expiation.

**Verse 20.** *{and whiles}* God's answer to Daniel's prayer.

*{whiles}* Vers. 21. Chap. 10.12. as soon, if not before he had ended his prayer. So ready is God to hear prayer according to his promises, Deut. 30.1,2,3. and 4.29. 1 King. 8.46, &c. to which prayer Daniel seems to have reference, using the same words and arguments. See Esay 30.19. and 65.24. Psal. 32.5. and 65.2. and 145.18,19.

*{speaking}* Voice and all outward helps to be used, specially in solemn prayer, to further us therein.

*{my sin}* Even Daniel's, so beloved a Saint. Yet he without hypocrisy in true humility, and sorrow, makes this confession. He no Pelagian, Donatist, Anabaptist, or Papist.

*{my people}* See vers. 24. after he had first begun with his own sins.

*{for the holy mountain of my God}* This his chief care. So it should be ours, Psal. 87.7. and 122.6, &c. The Church's miseries should much more affect and afflict us than our own, Psal. 137.6.

**Verse 21.** *{yea whiles}* No doubt Daniel had prayed oft before; yea, every day during the captivity, Chap. 6.10. for their return and restauration; but referring it to God's time. And God in his time readily hears, and helps, when there is most need. See here the effect and fruit of prayer; it brings down an Angel from heaven.

*{the man}* Angel in shape of a man; as Chap. 8.15.

*{Gabriel}* Chap. 8.16. when this Angel Gabriel tells Zachary of his name Gabriel, Luk. 1.11,19. and is sent unto the blessed Virgin Mary, ver. 26. he called them to think upon this text; that by conference of Moses, Numb. 24. and Daniel here, they might better consider the time of the promise.

*{whom I had seen}* Chap. 8.16. so well he remembered what, and by whom he had formerly learned, and been instructed, 2 Tim. 3.14.

*{being caused}* God's power and authority to command, and send, (he went not unsent) and the Angel's swift obedience.

*{swiftly}* Heb. *with weariness,* or, *flight:* tired as it were, with his speed-making.

*{touched me}* Chap. 8.18. In token of encouragement to hear these mysteries, and of infusion of new divine virtue to understand them, and to keep them in memory, and of comfort in these promises.

*{about the time of the evening oblation}* The Jews had both the morning and evening daily sacrifice, Exod. 12.6. and 29.39. Num. 28.4. At the time of the morning sacrifice, Elizaeus wrought that miracle, 2 King. 3.16,20. At the time of the evening sacrifice, Elias wrought that miracle, 1 King. 18.19,36. And these times were the set times of their daily prayers, Acts 3.1. And at the time of the evening oblation, our Savior made himself that oblation which here the Angel foretells, even most exactly when it should be performed.

**Verse 22.** *{Skill and understanding}* Heb. *skill of understanding.* This oration contains an abridgment of the New Testament, and a light of the old; and Daniel knew the substance of all before, but the time, which brings a greater clearness unto all. Therefore the time considered with the matter, is that which he calls here, *the skill of understanding;* And it must be counted a great error, to hold that a small matter; and specially the disannulling of God's truth, by heathen forged, most vain, and jarring antiquities, to make from Babel's fall to Tyberius eighteen, wherein our Lord died, 590 against the express 490, which the Angel counts to be wisdom, which he speaks of; yet old heathen Greeks did so. Thus fairly and resolutely do some learned men express their judgment in this case: whereof yet see more on ver. 25.

**Verse 23.** *{Commandment}* Heb. *word.* God has revealed to us Angels, and to me especially the secret of his counsel, concerning the restauration of Jerusalem, and the duration thereof, even to the Messiah, and has sent me to declare it unto you. See Dan. 10.12.

*{greatly beloved}* Heb. *a man of desires.* As Solomon was called Jedidiah, 2 Sam. 12.25. So here Daniel is called *a man of desires*: not because of his earnest desires after the knowledge of these sacred mysteries; but because of God's desires of love towards him, as one singularly beloved of God, and favored with his grace, Dan. 10.11,19. *Chamudoth* is the most amiable term of any which can be given. As the attribute is, so is the matter here, full of grace. And when the Angel spoke to the blessed Virgin in Hebrew, he could not speak better than *Bath Chamudoth*, *daughter of desires*, rendered as Daniel. God therein prevented our self-will worship, in giving Mary no terms, but such as others had; that we should not entreat or entertain novelty in religion; so we read *desirable raiment*, Gen. 27.15. and *bread of desire*, Dan. 10.3. and Chap. 11.8. and 2 Chron. 20.25. *Vessels of desires*.

*{understand the matter}* By this command, doubled for vehementer charge, he excites Daniel to all care, attention, and desire to understand this abstruse mystery, this sacred and wonderful secret that was to be revealed, and he condemns the world, that regards not to be instructed in this doctrine, sent from heaven by an Angel unto Daniel, and penned for all nations' use: wherefore we must give better heed unto these speeches. For if *the word spoken by Angels fall out sure*, and *every trespass received just recompense*, *how shall we escape*, neglecting so great a charge of looking to this, that so nearly concerns our salvation? See Hebr. 2.1,2,3.

**Verse 24.** *{Seventy weeks}* The reader is to know, that in the Hebrew we have word for word, *sevens seventy is pared out for the people*. *שבוע* signifies also a week; or, as we term it, a seven-night or sennight. As likewise in Greek, and Latin, it is so called, of the number of seven: and it signifies sometimes the space of seven days; as Dan. 10.2. Deut. 16.9. and sometimes it contains the seven year, Lev. 25.8. so here. The seventh day was the sabbath day; and the seventh year, the sabbath year; and seven sabbath years, made up the account for the Jubilee. And ten Jubilees, counting nine and forty years to each, make up 490 years, which are these 70 weeks of solar years here mentioned. About the beginning, and about the ending of these 70 weeks, there is much difficulty, and much variety of opinions among the learned; as will after appear.

*{are determined}* Heb. *is pared out,* or, *cut out or determined,* not shortened. A verb singular being joined to a substantive plural; as Job 12.9. Prov. 3.18. teaches an exact account. That every one of those weeks of years, particularly from the first to the last, shall be precisely complete, and just so many, not one more, or less. Now seventy sevens make four hundred and ninety in ordinary speech; we may not then as some would, understand by them 493 ½. Now that Daniel might conceive how at the beginning of his prayer, upon considering of Jeremy for that seventy years of captivity ended, God took notice of his meditation, the Angel touches as it were upon that seventy; showing how exactly seven times that space is declared aforehand, for the Jews' prerogative, continuance of ceremonies, and meditation how reconciliation of sin is truly made; that every sabbath, or seventh day, in the meanwhile, they might learn to enter into the meditation of that rest of Christ.

*{upon thy people}* As Daniel prayed for God's people, God's city, God's holy mountain, God's sanctuary, vers. 15,16,17,18,19. so he has this kingly honor vouchsafed him, that they are termed his people, he being so great a man among them, and so tender in affection towards them, and care over them, and therefore out of God's love to him, they could not be void of God's favor under such a protector. So Chap. 10.14. and Chap. 11.14. and Chap. 12.1. God called them Moses' people, Deut. 9.12. as seeming there to own them the less himself, because of his hot displeasure against them for their sin.

*{Thy holy City}* Your; So again to honor Daniel, and to show the thing that was the dearest jewel and joy of his heart.

*{holy City}* Saint Matthew naming Jerusalem *the holy city*, only at the beginning and end of our Lord's confirming the covenant, Mat. 4.5. and 27.53. seems to call upon us to consider this text. But when this time of 490 years was expired, it had that title no longer, then the many brought to the kingdom of heaven, Dan. 9.27. Mat. 8.11. are *the high Jerusalem*, Gal. 4.26. and *new Jerusalem*, Apoc. 3.12. and *the holy city*, Apoc. 11.2. and *holy Jerusalem*, Apoc. 21.10. as also in Isa. 52.1. where Jerusalem *the holy city*, bidden to put on her garments, requires us to frame our policy so, that all be taught of God, what white linen makes the garment, which is *the justification of Saints*. See Apoc. 19.8. and this further shows, in the opinion of many, and the best, that the end and period of the city, is the end and period of this prophecy of the seventy weeks, vers. 26,27. Of this see more on ver. 25.

*{to finish the transgression}* Or, *to restrain.* This is the rich benefit and precious part of this prophecy. All the following blessings come by the Messiah as is clearly in the words following expressed: however the Jews will not here endure to hear anything here of Christ. He shall sacrifice for the sins of the world by his death, and shall establish the true righteousness of the Gospel, which shall remain forever; through which all believers shall be absolved and justified; and by his Spirit be regenerate to a new and godly life: he shall fulfill all that was foretold of him; and he shall be consecrated, and made an everlasting King at the right hand of God his Father, having obtained the fullness of God's Spirit, as head of his Church. And after all this, the city and nation shall be destroyed by the Romans: this finishing transgression here, is not to make up the full measure of it, as Gen. 15.16. Mat. 23.32. 1 Thess. 2.16. But to make a full end of it; as the words following do further explain. Here the first degree is by taking away the dominion of the reigning power of it, Rom. 6.11,12.

*{and to make an end of sins}* Or, *to seal up;* that they never after be seen or discovered, Col. 2.14. and so can never appear to accuse us. As we seal up things we have done, with things we would be sure should not after appear; as the Lions' den, and our Savior's sepulcher were sealed. This is a second degree removing the evil of the accusation of sin.

*{and to make reconciliation for iniquity}* By his propitiatory sacrifice, taking away the guilt and punishment of it, which is a third degree, Esay 53.5. Rom. 8.1,34. See Esay 44.22. and 40.2. In which place of Esay, to note this by the way, the Trem. Bible has the error of *malitia* for *militia:* as also, Psal. 42.2. *Deum fontem vivum,* for, *Deum fortem vivum;* with many such others, even by the confession of the learned Papists themselves.

*{and to bring in everlasting righteousness}* 2 Cor. 5.21. Most perfectly fulfilling the law for us, by doing, and suffering; and imputing the same unto us that believe. After the removing the evil, this is, the good Christ brings us, 1 Cor. 1.30. See Psal. 85.11,12. Esay 53.11. Jer. 23.5,6. and 33.15,16.

*{and to seal up the vision}* All the visions and prophetical predictions, concerning Christ, were fulfilled in, and by him; and so the truth of them sealed up and confirmed, and completed in the accomplishment of them. Hence is that saying, so often in the Gospel, *That it might be fulfilled, &c.* Mat. 27.9,35. Luk. 4.21. and 22.37. and 24.44. and in John very often. Our Savior expounds this place, saying, that *the Law and Prophets reach unto John*, Mat. 11.13. Also Maimony, from the Jews' common consent, says, that all the Prophets prophesied but unto the days of Messiah. That speech of theirs must be taken hence. And themselves see and confess, that they have had no vision, Prophet, nor prophecies, never since our Savior Christ Jesus came. See 2 Cor. 1.20. Heb. 10.1. 1 Pet. 1.10, &c. Christ by his prophetical office, shut up, and sealed up all former visions, Prophets, and prophecies.

*{prophecy}* Heb. *Prophet.*

*{and to anoint the most holy}* Heb. *the holiness of holinesses.* The abstracts have somewhat more vigor and strength in them, than the concretes. The meaning is, to anoint him; that is, holiness itself, and in whom consists all the Church's holiness. Who is, in spirit and truth, that which the ark, the propitiatory, the altar of incense, and of burnt offerings; the table and candlestick, the sanctuary, and *Sanctum Sanctorum*, which were called most holy things, and consecrated by unction, Exod. 30.26. Lev. 8.10, &c. were all but in shadow and figure. He anointed to be the only King, Priest and Prophet of his Church. Hence he is called the Messiah; and Christ; both which do signify anointed, Esay 61.1, &c. Luk. 4.17, &c. Psal. 2.6. and 45.7. Act. 4.27. and Chap. 10.38. By this priesthood, he shut up and sealed up the Levitical priesthood, Heb. 8,9,10. Chapters. He the author and meritor of all the former blessings and benefits. He, the substance also of all that was formerly shadowed out, and prefigured.

**Verse 25.** *{Know therefore and understand}* Thus the Angel sharpens Daniel's attention again, and again, Mat. 24.15. We have more need to quicken ours, Dan. 12.10. and the rather also, because besides the weightiness of the matter, the difficulty and obscurity is so great, through the little light we have in Scripture, of the passages of these times; and the heathen historians, being so various and uncertain, and disagreeing among themselves, in the periods of times, and in the names, number and persons of the Kings, reigning within the compass of these years.

*{that from the going forth of the commandment, &c.}* There are diversities of opinions among the learned, concerning, both the beginning and the end of these seventy weeks: Yet not so great but that they make a joint evidence, and convincing demonstration against the Jews, that the Messiah is already come; and that many ages, long since past. For the beginning of them; there are four commandments, edicts, or proclamations, mentioned in Ezra, and Nehemiah: to one of which, these words, and the beginning of the seventy weeks, or 490 years, must of necessity be referred. The first, is that of Cyrus, 2 Chron. 36.22,23. Ezra 1.1. and 5.13. and 6.3. The second, is of Darius, Ezra 4.24. and 6.1. The third, is of Artaxerxes to Ezra, in the seventh year of his reign, Ezra 7.1,7,11,12,13,21. And the fourth, is of Artaxerxes again, to Nehemiah, in the twenty year of his reign, Neh. 1.1. and 2.1. Much variety, as we said, is in the opinions of the learned, to which of these the Angel has reference in these words; and from which of these the 70. weeks must take their commencement and beginning. Some, and among many others, the ancient Hebrews, do understand them to relate to that first edict of Cyrus; which was indeed the primary proclamation, and most famous; and that alone which was foretold, and prophesied of by name, so many ages before, Esay 44.28. and 45.13. and may seem strange, if it should be passed over, by the Angel, in silence, and not meant by him in these words. And the words here, both for the restoring and returning of the people, and for the building of the Temple and city, will well agree with the words of the edict. For that he gave order for the building of the city also, is not to be doubted, out of those said places, Esay 44.28. and 45.13. and also out of Ezra 4.12,13,15,16,19,21,23. And thereupon they reckon, that from, the expiration of the 70. years captivity in Babylon, and from the time of this prayer of Daniel and the words of the Angel, 70 weeks of years; that is, 490 years, should immediately follow, to the coming and death of the Messiah: and the destruction of Jerusalem should be soon after. And so they make Daniel's book, to contain six hundred years, viz. seventy in Babylon, 490. thence to Christ's death; and forty thence, to Jerusalem's destruction. And to this they labor, to make the chronology of the times to agree: disclaiming, hereupon, the computation of years, which is found in the heathen Greek histories of these times. And were it not for that difficulty, surely, this were an opinion to be much embraced. Others not finding the chronology of times, to agree with the former opinion, do fasten upon the second edict, in the second year of Darius: and here some do understand Darius, the son of Hystaspis; and reckon sixteen years from the first of Cyrus, to the sixth of this Darius, when the temple was finished. Ezra 6.15. And these strongly maintain this opinion, to agree best with the true chronology of the times: that from the second of Darius Hystaspis, to the death of the Messiah, are 490 years. Others, understand Darius Nothus, the base son of Artaxerxes Longimanus; and leaving out an 100, or an 150 years, from the first of Cyrus, to the second of this Darius; thence they make up the reckoning of the 490 years or seventy weeks, from the second of Darius Nothus, to the second of Vespasian, when the city and Temple were destroyed; thus, viz. from the second of Darius Nothus, to the end of the Persian monarchy, 92. Alexander the great in his monarchy, six years: who died in the first year of the 114 Olympiad; and in the 430 year since the building of Rome: Thence to our Savior's Passion, in the 18 of Tiberius, in the first year of the 103 Olympiad, 356 years, or 89 Olympiads. Thence to the destruction of Jerusalem by Titus, in the second of Vespasian, and in the 822 years since the building of Rome, 36 years. In all, 490 years, or seventy sevens, or weeks of years: ending them thus, not in the death of Christ, but in the destruction of Jerusalem, as we said. But in this second edict, of the second year of Darius, either Hystaspis, or Nothus, there is no mention made, either of the restoring and returning of the people, or of the building of Jerusalem, which are the words of the Angel's epoch here; but only of the building of the temple; and that only by removing the stop and hindrance; and so promoting only, and furthering the building of it. Others, understand these words of, and begin these weeks in the seventh year of Artaxerxes Longimanus, the father of Darius Nothus, and end them in the death of the Messiah. Others, of Mnemon, or Memon the son of Darius Nothus, and end them in the destruction of Jerusalem: But either of these is the least probable; because, then only Ezra was sent with his company; and the temple was built before; and the wall of Jerusalem after. And yet men not unlearned, embrace this opinion. The last opinion, conceives the Angel's words to be meant of the last edict, in the twentieth year of Artaxerxes, either Longimanus, or else Mnemon, or Memon; when, indeed, Nehemiah was sent to restore and to build Jerusalem; the walls and city, Nehem. 1.3. and 2.3,5,8,17. and Chap. 3. and Chap. 7.4. And to this also, as well as to the first edict of Cyrus, the next words of the Angel do seem well to relate and agree. And they, who are of this opinion, do labor also to make the chronology of times, to correspond hereunto. The cause of all this diversity, is in the silence of the Scripture; for that ends in Ezra his time: and in the uncertainty of human histories, the Greeks especially, which do very much vary, as we have said, among themselves, in setting down the parcels, and particularities of these times, here summed up, in so few words, by the Angel. And in these uncertainties, we cannot be very certain; but leave it to the judgment of the judicious reader: intimating only the inclination of our opinion, towards the first of all: as the fairest and safest to rest upon; specially, if the period of it were extended beyond the death of the Messiah, to the destruction of Jerusalem and the Temple; however, human histories will not clear up the chronology of times to be answerable thereunto. And, in likelihood, it stood best with the wisdom of God, all things considered; That, as many other prophecies, so this famous one, of the coming and death of the Messiah, and of the final destruction of the city and Temple, should have as much doubt and difficulty in it, for the precise point of time, as all this comes to.

*{to restore and to build Jerusalem}* These words note down the epoch, or period, from whence these seventy weeks must take their rise and beginning: for which see the last foregoing Annotations. This, and all that follows in this verse, are things which must be brought to pass and accomplished, within the compass of that space of time.

*{restore}* Some refer this to the Temple, and the restoring of it. Some to the city only; and the building of it again; as the same word is used again, in the latter end of this verse. But others, more rightly, to the people of the Jews, and their returning back; as the conjugation of the verb, and the distinct repetition of the particle or preposition *ל* do declare.

*{build Jerusalem}* The building of it was commanded, and begun in Cyrus his time; as was showed in the last Annotations. We must not here make two distinct times, to be intended by the Angel; one for the restoring and return of the people, and another time for the building of Jerusalem; as some do.

*{unto the Messiah}* Unto him; and to all that is there said of him, in his own person, and in his instruments, executing his most severe judgment upon the Jewish people, city and Temple; that shall be the period of the seventy weeks.

*{Messiah}* Messiah, here only, twice a proper name. Hence famous, Joh. 1.41. and 4.25. The eternal Word is, by the Angel, named Messiah; that is, in Greek, *Christ*, or *Anointed*, or written *in abstracto:* *Anointed*, in regard of both natures, as to the office of reconciliation and redemption. But anointed in the human nature only, as to the gifts of the Holy Ghost, collated upon it, infused into it.

*{the Prince}* Duke, leader, commander. So Esay 55.4. Mic. 5.2. Mat. 2.6. Act. 3.15. and 5.31. Heb. 2.10. and 12.2. We must follow his rule and conduct.

*{shall be}* The Angel now divides the 70 weeks into three parts; First, into seven weeks. Secondly, into sixty-two weeks: and thirdly, into one week; according to the sequence of times, and affairs therein. And he joins proper stories to them, at least, to the first and last part.

*{seven weeks}* Of years, which are forty-nine years. Which divers reckon, from divers beginnings; according to the various opinions thereof, formerly mentioned. Some begin them with Cyrus, his decree, and end them, when the walls of Jerusalem were finished by Nehemiah; and make this interval of time to be forty-nine years. And hereunto, they refer also those forty-six years, Joh. 2.20. beginning that reckoning from the third of Cyrus, when Daniel mourned for the hindrance of the building, Chap. 10.1,2. Others, begin them with the second of Darius Nothus, who reigned 19 years; and end them with the 32 of his successor Artaxerxes, Mnemon, when, say they, Nehemiah having finished the buildings, and his deputyship, returns to Artaxerxes again, Neh. 5.14. and Chap. 13.6. The truth is, the Scripture pleasing not to express, the full years of the reigns of those Persian Kings, Cyrus, Ahasuerus, Artaxerxes, Darius; nor yet the series of their succession: and most of these names, being so common to many of the Persian kings, in profane stories, leaves us in uncertainties, for the assignation and application of these years, to the precise years of those Kings.

*{and threescore and two weeks}* These 62 sevens, which make 434 years from the restauration of Jerusalem, are parceled alone, only, that the last seven or week of years may be handled alone, for our Lord's story. And these do begin when the former seven weeks are ended: and are to be joined with the former seven weeks, because they both jointly go before the time of the last week. Yet some render the former words, and these, thus, *shall be sevens of weeks, even 62 weeks*: And so make the latter words expository of the former. And understand these 62 weeks to be included only in the 70 weeks; But to have neither the same beginning, nor ending, with the 70 weeks; but to begin after them, and end before them: to begin in the seventh of Artaxerxes Mnemon, and to end in the death of the Messiah.

*{the street}* Some with some seeming strain to the words, do refer this to the first seven weeks of years only; and make them the troublous times. Others, to the following 62 weeks only; and make them the troublous times. But considering the placing of the words, with least strain, and in true sense, they may best in their latitude relate to them both; so including the seven weeks, as not excluding altogether the 62. And indeed both were troublous times.

*{wall}* Or, *breach,* or, *ditch.* Streets and walls comprehend the whole city.

*{even in troublous times}* Heb. *in strait of times.* Not for shortness of times; but for strait and sore troubles in them. See Ezra, and Nehemiah, and Ezek. Chap. 38. and 39. and Dan. 11. and the books of Maccabees, for the troubles of all these times. Such very often are the times, and state of the Church.

**Verse 26.** *{and after threescore and two weeks}* That is, in the seventh and last week, after the year 483. from the going forth of the commandment, Vers. 25.

*{shall Messiah be cut off}* Some understand these words, that in the last seven, and in the last half of that seven, Christ shall come and preach, and suffer death; for in three years and a half he finished his public office: The testament was ratified by the death of Christ, Heb. 9.17. Therefore they will have this last week to end in Christ's death; and in the 28 Jubilee from the eighth year of Joshua. Others, observing the Hebrew distinction *Zaeph Caton* set down before these words, *and after the 62 weeks*; do rather and better render and understand them thus, *After the 62 weeks; and after the Messiah being cut off*; And so they make the Messiah to be cut off before this last week, or seven years; and refer the last week to the destruction of Jerusalem. The next verse will better clear these things.

*{Messiah}* The same in the former verse. The Jews do seek but foolishly to wrest it to Hircanus the high Priest cut off by Herod, or to any other such person of the priestly line. For, besides other reasons, the things here spoken of Messiah, are so great as can agree to none other but to Christ our Savior.

*{cut off}* By violent death from the land of the living; as Esay 53.8. and in Ezekiah's case, Esay 38.10,11. See this Act. 2.23. and 3.13. and 4.15. and 13.28,29. and in the history of the Gospels, Luk. 24.26. This is set down here, as the cause of the destruction following.

*{but not for himself}* Or, *and shall have nothing.* Being brought to nothing by his death, without life, strength, or any dignity in appearance, Psal. 22.6,7. Act. 3.13,14. Esay 53.2,3. Phil. 2.3. as he had in his lifetime no esteem or regard, Act. 4.11. Joh. 10.21. Others thus; *there being nothing in him;* Namely, no cause or fault, Esay 53.4,9. as Job 16.17. See 1 Pet. 2.22. 1 Joh. 3.5. Or, *there being nothing therein for him;* that is to say, all being done for the good and redemption of mankind, 2 Cor. 5.11. Or, *they being none of his;* that is, there being nothing then in Jerusalem, which he will have and own, but cast off and reject. The Trent Bible does greatly corrupt the text, and swerve from it.

*{and the people, &c.}* For that heinous sin of cutting off Christ, this horrible judgment shall follow, and fall upon them.

*{the people of the Prince that shall come, shall destroy}* That is, the Roman legions under Titus shall destroy city, and Sanctuary. Or, the Gentiles, now strangers, Eph. 2.12. but, by the preaching of the Gospel, should become the people of the Prince to come, the Messiah, when Israel was rejected, they shall destroy, &c. Some translate thus, *the city and Sanctuary shall he destroy, the governor's people to come*; that is, Christ the King, to whom the Father has given all judgment, shall destroy this Prince's reputed people the Jews, that should come, and reject him, and also their Sanctuary: and this in the next generation after their cutting of him off. Of this Christ foretold them, Mat. 24.2,15——22. and ver. 34. and Chap. 23.36,37,38. and of the proper token of it, Luk. 21.20——25.

*{and the end thereof shall be with a flood}* A deluge of destruction, as Esay 8.7,8. an irresistible torrent shall finally sweep away city and Sanctuary, people and state, Matt. 24.21. Luk. 21.22.

*{desolations are determined}* They are inevitable by God's decree. See Esay 10.22,23. Rom. 9.27,28,29.

**Verse 27.** *{And he shall confirm, &c.}* The Messiah shall corroborate, ratify and establish. And this by his preaching and Passion; as Prophet, Priest, and King of his Church.

*{the Covenant}* Of God, and his Gospel; the New Covenant, Jer. 31.31——35. Heb. 8.10——12. Rom. 5.15.

*{with many}* Rom. 5.15,19. Mat. 26.28. with the elect, not Jews only, but Gentiles also.

*{many for one week}* Or, *many one week;* that is, that one week remaining, which is the 70 week: where the first half of it only is to be understood; as appears, by comparing this with the other half of it, in the words immediately following.

*{and in the midst of the week}* The former half part of that week being past: in the latter half of it, allotted, say some, for the public function of the Messiah, in the end of it.

*{he shall cause the sacrifice and the oblation to cease}* The Messiah, the Prince by the sacrifice of himself, say some, once offered, shall put an end to the daily sacrifice, and all other Levitical sacrifices, which were but shadows and types of him, and of his sacrifice which was to come. The shadows therefore were to vanish and cease, when the body itself was come, Heb. 10.1,9,14. Others, and not amiss, make this whole verse to be a continuation of that in the latter part of the former verse: and refer it all to the seventieth, or the last week of years, beginning 483; and being 29 years after the Messiah was cut off: and understand it thus, That the Messiah, by his Apostles and ministers, preaching the Gospel, should that one week confirm the covenant with many; in manner aforesaid confirm them in it. As also in token thereof he further did confirm his love to those of his that were in Jerusalem, by bringing them out of it to Pella before the siege, by a divine revelation and oracle; as Eusebius relates in his Ecclesiastical History, book 3. Chap. 5. So saving them, as Lot out of Sodom, as the woman, Apoc. 12.14. and as Apoc. 18.4. from that deluge and inundation of wrath that fell upon the city; and so showing his faithfulness, and confirming the truth of his *covenant* of love with them. And that in the midst of that week; that is, much thereabout, in the year 487 or 488. he shall cause the sacrifice, and the oblation to cease; that is, he should so do, by Vespasian and the Roman legions and armies besieging Jerusalem, Nero being yet alive; and by Titus after continuing the siege, by the straightness whereof they should cause the means and requisites necessary for sacrifice and oblation to cease and fail, and soon after burn the Temple; so taking away the place itself, wherein only it was lawful for them to sacrifice.

*{And for the overspreading of abominations}* Or, *with the abominable armies,* or, *wing of abominations:* wing being taken for armies; as Esay 8.8. meaning the armies of the heathen Romans, abominable to the Jews, in themselves, and in their doings against them; as it after follows.

*{He shall make it desolate}* The Messiah, for their most heinous sin of murdering and cutting them off, by the Roman legions that make all desolate, lay all waste, city and Temple, state and nation, by their army, which is therefore called, Chap. 12.11. *the abomination that makes desolate.* Compare Luk. 21.20. with Matth. 24.15. and Mark 13.14. See also, Matthew 22.7. The accomplishment of all this we read in Josephus, Egesippus, and others.

*{even until the consummation}* Utter consumption, and final destruction of that place, and people of the Jews, Mat. 24.2. Luk. 21.6. See the like, Ezek. 7.2,3, &c.

*{and that determined}* Ordained before, and decreed by God; for their sin, chiefly in rejecting and cutting off the Messiah, the Prince, as was said, Mat. 27.25. See the like phrase, Esay 10.23. and 28.22.

*{shall be poured upon the desolate}* Shall flow in upon them as a flood, shall rush in, and break in upon them as a breach of the sea, as a deluge and inundation, that overflows all, sweeps all away, carries all before it. See the like, Esay 8.7,8. Jer. 46.7,8. and 48.2. See Luk. 21.24. Hence the Jews should have learned, not so much to rest upon, and to acquiesce in that temporal deliverance out of the Babylonish captivity, and in the reedification of the Temple and city; as by faith to look after that spiritual deliverance, by the Messiah, that heavenly Canaan, that spiritual Temple, that holy city, *the new Jerusalem which is above, the mother of us all*.

## CHAPTER 10

**Verse 1.** *{In, &c.}* Daniel mourning for the sad estate of the Jews in Judea, has a vision vouchsafed to him, which is the last, and is contained in this, and the two remaining Chapters. Wherein he is first relieved and strengthened, once, and again, against the great fright and fainting, wherein he was cast by the greatness of the vision. And after he is informed of the affairs of the empires of the *Persians* and *Grecians,* chiefly as they stood in relation to the state and condition of the people of the Jews, the Church of God, under them; and more fully he is told of the passages and transactions of state between the Seleucidae and Lagidae, the two main kingdoms of the Grecian empire divided. And most of all of the actions of *Antiochus Epiphanes,* one of the Seleucidae; of his subtlety, impiety, tyranny, and bloody persecuting the religion and people of the Jews. And that Christ in the end will not be wanting to the deliverance of his people, but save and free them, at the precise time there set down. In this Chapter are chiefly set down things precedent to the prophecy, in the next, the body of the prophecy itself. And in the last, some things annexed thereunto.

*{the third}* Heb. *three.* See *Annotations* on Chap. 1.1.

*{year}* And in the beginning of this year; as appears, vers. 4. And this was the third year of his monarchy, after the winning of Babylon: For he had reigned over the Persians many years before this: and in this year he was slain by *Tomyris* the Queen of the *Scythians.*

*{of Cyrus}* Heb. *Coresh;* signifying in the Persian tongue a Lord; given to him after, for honor's sake. But his first name was *spaco,* that is, a dog or bitch, in the language of the Medes; so called, because a bitch gave him suck when he was an infant exposed to death; as Herodotus and Justin do relate.

*{King of Persia}* Now monarch, as was said before.

*{a thing}* Heb. *a word;* usually put for thing.

*{was revealed}* From God by a vision.

*{unto Daniel}* So famous, even in foreign nations, for wisdom, worth, and piety for visions and for skill in dreams, revealing secrets, and foretelling things to come; whereof many were already come to pass: so miraculously saved in the Lions' den; so esteemed, advanced, and employed by the monarchs of Babylon, and Persia.

*{Belteshazzar}* See Chap. 1.7. the *Annotations* there.

*{and the thing was true}* See Apoc. 19.9. Luk. 1.20. Jer. 44.28. No false vision of a lying prophet.

*{but the time appointed was long}* Heb. *great in length;* Namely, in many ages after it should receive its final accomplishment, ver. 14. so long, say some, as till the end of the world: out of that, Chap. 12.2. But rather, till the time of *Antiochus Epiphanes,* which was yet for many generations to come hereafter. Which shows, Daniel's God to be the true God, foretelling things to come, Esay 41.22,23. and the duty of God's people, patiently to wait, and expect God's appointed time.

*{as he understood}* Namely, by the Angel's showing, ver. 11,14,20,21. and 11.2. and 12.8,9.

*{the thing, &c.}* Contained in Chap. 11. and 12.

**Verse 2.** *{In those days}* In the very beginning of that third year; as appears, ver. 4. most likely beginning the third day of the first month.

*{was mourning}* Because the people were so backward to return out of their captivity, when they were licensed and authorized thereunto; yet so very many of them remained still behind, mindless and careless of God's religion, worship, and promises: but chiefly because the building of the Temple, begun by the Jews that were returned at the command of Cyrus, Ezra 1. and 3. Chapters, was now inhibited, and ceased, by the means of the Samaritans, and others, who hired counselors against them, and so prevailed with Cambyses the son, in the absence of Cyrus the father, in the Scythian war. As they did also after this time, in the days of Ahasuerus and Artaxerxes, until the second year of Darius, Ezr. 8.4. This hindrance, in this year of Darius, much affected and afflicted holy Daniel; as for the Temple's sake, so for the God of the Temple, whose truth and power, in performing his promise, might by this interruption seem to be questioned and impeached. The use of mourning and tears, in like cases, is very frequent in Scripture, Esay 38.5. and 22.14. Psal. 6.5.126.5,6. Jerem. 9.1. and 13.17. and 14.17. Mat. 5.4.

*{three full weeks}* Heb. *weeks of days;* which distinguishes them from the weeks of years, Chap. 9.24, &c. with this time fell in their feast of Passover. Which Daniel could not observe, as being out of the land of Judea, and Jerusalem, Deut. 16.2,5,6,7. But the Jews there might, having built the altar, Ezr. 3.2——7.

**Verse 3.** *{I eat no pleasant bread}* Heb. *bread of desires,* as portion of desires, Jer. 12.10. and land of desire, Jerem. 3.19. Mal. 3.12. Zech. 7.14. Here are some expositions of the manner of his mourning: besides prayers and tears, which no doubt were not wanting; here he shows his abstinence in meats and drink, and other comforts and refreshments of the body: thereby to help forward the afflicting of his soul in this godly sorrow for the calamity of the Church, and the people of God; and by mortifying of his flesh, to quicken his spirit the more in making his addresses to God. These, and like signs of sorrow, are used in Scripture in like cases. So 2 Sam. 12,16,17,20. Of his choosing of pulse, rather than to eat of the daily provision of the King's meat, and to drink of the wine which Nebuchadnezzar drank, see Chap. 1.8. and the *Annotations* there. But when he was at his own finding, observing the Levitical ordinances, he abstained not from the lawful use of pleasant bread, and flesh, and wine; as appears here, except in cases of fasting and mourning.

*{flesh, nor wine}* His kind of fasting here consisted not in a choice of certain kind of meats; but of the condition and quality of them. He chose the coarsest sorts of meats and drinks; such as might serve to sustain nature only, though in much hardship and penance, and chastening himself, vers. 12. He abstained from wine as well as flesh. His diet was coarse, and scant, during so long a time.

*{anoint myself}* Anointing was much in use among the Jews, and others, to cheer up the countenance, and rejoice the heart, Psal. 104.15. Prov. 27.9. So used by Ruth, Chap. 3.3. by David, 2 Sam. 12.20. by those, Amos 6.6. Whence is that, Psal. 23.5. Eccl. 9.8. And the practice of those two women that anointed our Savior's feet; the one, Luk. 7.38,46. the other, Joh. 11.2. and 12.3. And therefore in time of mourning and sorrow, the use of it is forbidden, and laid aside; as 2 Sam. 14.2. And so Daniel here forbears it: as no doubt all things else which might any way delight and cheer up the body in this time of his mourning. Those words of our Savior, Mar. 8.17. are spoken only in opposition to hypocrisy in fasting, against such as in private fastings did disfigure their faces, that they might appear unto men to fast.

*{till three whole, &c.}* This was not then a sudden passion, and soon over: but thus struck him to the heart, and stuck by him; to see the Church and people of God in this distress; the Temple and worship of God thus stopped and hindered. See Neh. 1.3,4. and Chap. 2.3. Rom. 12.15. Psal. 137.4,5,6. and this time of fasting is like that, 1 Sam. 31.13.

**Verse 4.** *{And in the four and twentieth day}* Presently upon the ending of these three weeks mourning.

*{of the first month}* Called *Nisan*, in the Chaldee tongue, Esth. 3.7. and *Abib* in the Hebrew, Exod. 22,23. with Chap. 13.4. Deut. 16.1. which signifies the stalk with the ear; because in that month which answered to part of our March and April, barley was in the ear in those countries, Exod. 9.31. Lev. 23.10,15. Deut. 16.9.

*{I was}* In vision; or, it may be, in person, in this time of the Persian monarchy: and this the rather, considering a number of men were with him, vers. 7.

*{river}* By rivers' sides many times God showed visions to his Prophets. See *Annotations* on Chap. 8.2.

*{Hiddekel}* Gen. 2.14. the third river, or great channel or head of the great Euphrates, which goes toward the East of Assyria; or Eastward from the garden of *Eden,* to Assyria: bounding *Eden* most Northerly; and above Seleucia running into Tigris, whence it has its name. And Tigris so called from the name of an arrow, in the language of the Medes, by reason of the exceeding sharp swiftness of the stream: and Hiddekel in Hebrew, of a like notation. In English, *sharp-swift.* The Persians' chief glory was in those quarters. And likewise afterwards the Seleucidae there seated themselves: and Seleucus Nicator built upon Tigris, the famous city Seleucia.

**Verse 5.** *{Then I lift up mine eyes}* Walking forth likely by the riverside, with some company, vers. 7. to refresh himself after his long mourning and chastening of himself before his God: and not without the use of meditation and contemplation there; He then lift up his eyes, and looked.

*{a certain man}* Heb. *one man.* One in shape of a man: an Angel, say some: but more likely, Christ; by comparing Chap. 12.6,7. and Rev. 1.13,14,15. and 10.5. that *Palmoni,* Chap. 8.13——16. See Ezek. 1.26. and 40.3.

*{clothed in linen}* The word *בד* *bad,* signifies precious linen; such as Kings, Priests, and Princes used to wear; white and shining. See Exod. 28.39. Lev. 16.4. 1 Sam. 22.18. 2 Sam. 6.14. Gen. 41.42. Esth. 8.15. Thus also Angels appeared, Ezek. 9.2. and Mar. 16.5. Luk. 24.4. in long, white, shining garments, Act. 1.10. Rev. 15.6. Sometimes in Scripture our Savior appears clothed in red; as Esay 63.1,2,3. and Rev. 19.13. signifying thereby, that he treads *the winepress of the fierceness and wrath of God* for our sins; and has his garments victoriously sprinkled and stained with the blood of our spiritual enemies. He thus clothed in red, that we might be *clothed in white robes*, Rev. 7.9,12. and Chap. 19.13,14. This linen was so thin, that his body appeared through it, vers. 6.

*{whose loins, &c.}* Rev. 1.13. See Rev. 15.6.

*{gold of uphaz}* Where was the most precious gold, Jer. 10.9. more famous then than that of Havilah or Parvaim, Gen. 2.11,12. 2 Chron. 3.6. This Uphaz seems to be the same with Ophir, or *aurea Chersonesus,* as most think; whither Solomon's navy went; and Jehoshaphat's ships were bound for gold, 2 Chr. 8.18. 1 King. 9.28. and 22.48. and whereof we read, Job. 28.16. Psal. 45.9. Esay 13.9. 1 Chr. 29.4. This precious golden girdle may represent, the precious, the perfect, the willing and ready obedience of Christ to do his Father's will and service, in the redeeming of mankind, Esay 11.5. and 42.1. and 49.3,4. and Chap. 53. Phil. 2.7. Joh. 4.34. See 1 King. 18.46. 2 King. 4.29.

**Verse 6.** *{the beryl}* A precious stone of a sky color, or sea color. See Ezek. 1.16. and 10.9. and the *Annotations* there. The Trent Bible translates here, *a chrysolite.*

*{of lightning}* Glittering, and dazzling the eyes of a speedy presence, Mat. 24.27. and such was the Angel's countenance, Mat. 28.3. See Mar. 17.2.

*{and his eyes, &c.}* Rev. 1.12. and 19.12.

*{his arms, &c.}* Rev. 1.15. See Ezek. 1.7.

*{and the voice, &c.}* See Ezek. 1.24. and 43.2. Apoc. 1.10,15. Act. 2.2. By all these former resemblances, may be shadowed out several divine perfections, in Christ our Savior.

**Verse 7.** *{alone saw}* Act. 9.7. Not that his companions' eyes were struck with such blindness, as the Sodomites, Gen. 19.11. or the Syrian army, 2 King. 6.18. But that this vision was not vouchsafed to them, nor their eyes enlightened to see it; no more than the eyes of Elisha's servant were, till upon the Prophet's prayer, the Lord opened his eyes, to see the mountain *full of horses and chariots of fire round about Elisha,* 2 King. 6.17.

*{fled to hide themselves}* The wicked flee sometimes when none pursues, Prov. 28.1. But here likely, hearing suddenly that huge voice and noise, ver. 6. as Saul's company heard a voice, though not the words of him that spoke to Saul; but saw no man, but the light only, Act. 9.7. and 22.9. This struck them here into a panic fear; as 2 King. 7.7. through their inward guilt and frailty, misdeeming danger; that they sought to hide themselves, as Adam in paradise, in caves or weeds, or any such place, as might be by the riverside. This sight and flight in them served in part, for the confirmation of the truth of this vision; that this was not a thing feigned by Daniel, or formed only in his imagination; but in part witnessed by these men also.

**Verse 8.** *{this great}* This exceeding great; so much the original imports.

*{no strength}* Ver. 16. his spirits were dispersed and spent, his nerves and sinews enfeebled, the joints of his loins loosed, the blood running to the heart, to maintain and strengthen it, left all the other parts cold and liveless, somewhat like that, Chap. 5.6.

*{my comeliness}* Vigor, was changed, corrupted, gone, he was so appalled in color, and countenance, that he looked liker a dead carcass, than a living man. Such was his consternation and astonishment at the exceeding greatness of the vision, that he was fainting and swooning, and scarce life remained in him. See the like, Chap. 7.15,28. and 8.17,27. Ezek. 1.28. Hab. 3.16. Act. 9.3,4. Rev. 1.17. Esay 6.5.

**Verse 9.** *{then was I in a deep sleep}* Then I fell into a deep sleep Chap. 8.18. like that of Adam, Gen. 2.21. or of Jonas, Chap. 1.5. or that, 1 Sam. 26.12. All this was to fit him the more, by this deep humiliation, to a religious attention and docility, for the receiving of these wonderful mysteries and prophecies in this vision to be revealed to him.

**Verse 10.** *{an hand touched me}* Ver. 16,18. Chap. 8.18. and 9.21. Apoc. 1.17. See Esay 6.6,7. This seems to be the hand of the Angel Gabriel, Chap. 8.16. and 9.21. like the appearance of a man, ver. 16,18. touched him, with the hand of a man; not the hand of him, ver. 6. Hereby may be signified and symbolized the power of the Holy Ghost. See Ezek. 1.3.

*{set me upon my knees}* Heb. *caused me to move my self upon my knees;* and so set me up in part; being fallen before all along upon the ground upon his face. Thus striving it seems to rise, yet though faintness could not at the first get upright.

**Verse 11.** *{a man greatly beloved}* Ver. 19. Chap. 9.23.

*{that I speak}* I Gabriel.

*{stand upright}* Heb. *stand upon thy standing:* or, *in thy standing.* So Neh. 8.7. a second degree of his raising, and corroboration. He must stand up to hear God's Word by the Angel. See Jud. 3.20.

*{for unto thee am I now sent}* Thus then was an Angel, sent to tell and teach him things to come.

*{I stood trembling}* Not fully recovered; but as a faint man newly rising out of his sickbed, weak in his knees. Thus God when he pleases, does by degrees dispense his favors, and supports.

**Verse 12.** *{Fear not}* A third degree. He now comes from his body to his mind. Bids him put away that terror and trouble of mind wherein he was, that he might quietly and comfortably hear and understand what he had to deliver unto him. So again, ver. 19. So Mat. 28.5,10. Luk. 2.10. and 24.35. and in many other places.

*{the first day}* Ver. 2. the first of the three weeks. Here is God's readiness to hear prayer, Esay 65.24. and 58.9. Psal. 32.5. See Chap. 9.20.

*{set thine heart}* Heb. *give thy heart:* apply, and employ it, not formally, or perfunctorily.

*{to understand}* Consider, and weigh the present calamity of your people; with a desire to know what their future state shall be.

*{and to chasten}* Ver. 2,3. See Lev. 16.29.

*{before thy God}* In his sight; without all hypocrisy: in deep humiliation, with true faith, and serious repentance. To all which the Angel here gives testimony.

*{thy words were heard}* The words of your prayers. And those were not so much to know what should befall his people in the latter days: of that he had understood, Chap. 7. and 8. neither would he for that have been so boldly inquisitive, or so much have afflicted himself, ver. 2,3. But to ease and relieve them of their present pressure and calamity, which now in this third year of Cyrus was befallen them. And here we see the gracious effect and fruit of prayer, Psal. 65.2.

*{and I am come for thy words}* Sent on this ambassage to you, upon the gracious hearing of your prayers.

**Verse 13.** *{But the Prince, &c.}* By *Prince,* here; Romish interpreters do generally understand an *Angel.* And do teach, that to kingdoms and provinces, as also to every man when he is born, there are appointed by God, particular good Angels for their continual guardians: and that Satan on the other side, does appoint to them particular evil angels to watch and work for their hurt; and that these are in opposition, and fight one against another. Bold assertions upon weak grounds. And here the chief of these expositors understand by the *Prince of the Kingdom of Persia,* a good *Angel,* which they call the *Tutelar Angel,* and guardian of that kingdom. But then how he and Gabriel should withstand and fight against one another, they puzzle themselves, and are at a loss. Others of them understand an evil angel, that did here withstand Gabriel, and after fight against him, vers. 20. But leaving these groundless fancies, and unsafe supposals. The text fairly and directly leads us to the Prince of the kingdom of Persia, who was Cambyses, the son of Cyrus, as heathen histories do record; who in his father's absence, as was said, stopped the execution of his father's decree, and forbade the building of the Temple; being drawn thereto by those mentioned, Ezra 4.1——6. and likely not without the Devil's hand and help in it.

*{withstood me}* Or, *stood before me;* as it were, juridically. I being appointed to observe him, and his doings against your people, and to provide for, and withstand the impediments which he casts in the way, and the oppositions made by him.

*{one and twenty days}* Those three weeks of your mourning and chastening yourself, ver. 2,3. and this hindered me from coming to you till now, though the words of your prayers were heard and granted from the first of those days.

*{but lo Michael, one of the chief Princes}* Or, *the first of the chief Princes;* their Lord, called your Prince, vers. 21. and the great Prince, which stands for the children of your people, Chap. 12.1. *Michael the Archangel,* Jude ver. 9. *Michael and his Angels:* Rev. 12.7. This Michael here, some take to be a chief created angel. But most, for the *Angel of the Covenant,* Christ our Savior; that *Archangel,* by whose voice and proclamation the dead shall be raised, 1 Thess. 4.16. And *Angels* here are called *Princes.* See Rom. 8.38. Eph. 1.21. and 3.20. Col. 1.16.

*{came to help me}* Cambyses so strongly withstood, that I stood in need of this help, which is above the help of Angels, against him, to repress his further cruel counsels, and mischievous purposes, against the Jews; and to inform and enable me in this service. This does minister sound comfort to the Church in all afflictions.

*{remained there}* 21 days.

*{with the Kings of Persia}* Cambyses, and his peers, and counselors that set him on against the Jews.

**Verse 14.** *{Now I am come}* At the end of the 21 days.

*{in the later days}* Heb. *in the sequel, or posterity of dayes.* So Chap. 2.28. Gen. 49.1. Act. 2.17. Not at the end of the world, or in the days of the Gospel; But in the declination, and towards the end of their temporal state; a while before the coming of the Messiah; under whom began another age; and a spiritual state of the Church. And this is more than Daniel seems to have prayed for. God herein deals with him as he did with Ahaz, Esay 7.14.

*{for yet}* Besides those things which were shown you in vision, Chap. 7. and 8.

*{the Vision}* And prophecy; as Chap. 8.17. Esay 1.1.

*{for many days}* See Chap. 12.4,6——12. The days of Antiochus Epiphanes are most insisted upon, Chap. 11. which were many ages after this.

**Verse 15.** *{I set my face, &c.}* Ver. 8,9. as not able to endure the brightness of his glory; as our eye is not able to endure the brightness of the sun. After the former sweet consolations and corroborations, we may marvel at this continued dejection: But rather guess at, and conjecture, than certainly define any fresh causes or occasions of it. New ones we can hardly imagine, did intervene. But we must rest still in the former; as his own words do import, ver. 16,17.

*{and I became dumb}* Either of set purpose, silent; or else not able to speak: and indeed we read not that he spoke anything since the beginning of this vision. And the words he first uses, seem to tend this way, ver. 17. And sometimes astonishment, fear, shame, sorrow, yea, admiration and joy, when they are on a sudden in an excessive degree, do for a while take away the use and faculty of speech, till a man is able to recollect himself.

**Verse 16.** *{one like, &c.}* Ver. 10,18. This seems to be the same Angel Gabriel; the same that spoke to him, and was sent to him, Chap. 8.16. and 9.21. Though some understand this of the Son of God, in the appearance of man: as in that shape he often showed himself to the Patriarchs and Prophets, before his incarnation.

*{touched my lips}* That were dumb. Ver. 15. By this visible sign strengthening him, and enabling him for speech. See Jer. 1.9. Esay 6.7. Exod. 4.11,12. Psal. 51.15. Mat. 8.3.

*{then I opened, &c.}* The effect following the former sign. God's signs and Sacraments are not without their efficacy and effect in the right use of their administration and participation.

*{that stood before me}* This same Angel Gabriel.

*{O my Lord}* Thus he styles the Angel; in all humility, out of the sense of his own frailty, and the Angel's glory.

*{by the Vision}* Thus he excuses himself and his weakness; from the gloriousness of the vision and apparition.

*{my sorrows are turned upon me}* Rush and break in upon me, as the pangs of a woman in travail.

*{no strength}* See ver. 8. So again, ver. 17.

**Verse 17.** *{the servant of this my Lord}* Or, *this servant of my Lord;* that is, I your servant, so weak and worthless; and in the state which I am in. See Rev. 19.10. and 22.9.

*{talk}* The vision thus working on me, much less can I be able to talk.

*{with this my Lord;}* The glorious Angel. And much less with yonder my Lord, the Lord of Angels, him that appeared vers. 5,6. to whom the doubling of the pronoun demonstrative in the original, in the opinion of some, makes a reference.

*{for as for me}* Heb. *and I.* The nominative put absolutely.

*{straightway}* Since you did begin to enter, speak and talk with me.

*{breath}* Scarce any breath, or life; notwithstanding that touching in the former verse: an hyperbole.

**Verse 18.** *{again}* This third time touched. See ver. 16.

**Verse 19.** *{And said}* See vers. 11,12.

*{Peace be unto thee}* The loving and usual form of salutation and comprecation, Luk. 10.5. and 24.36. Joh. 3. ver. 14. *Peace* comprehending all blessings, all felicity. He should not then need to fear death, or any danger.

*{be strong}* Redoubled, for greater assurance, and efficacy. So Josh. 1.6,7,9,18. and 10.25. Thus the Angel, as it were, strives and wrestles to overcome Daniel's infirmity.

*{I was strengthened}* The power and effect of God's Word by the Angel. God by degrees lifts up his; and ministers grace and strength.

*{Let my Lord speak}* When God does fit and furnish; then man is willing and ready. So Esay 6.8.

**Verse 20.** *{Knowest thou &c.?}* By this interrogation, he would quicken Daniel's desire after it. See ver. 12,14.

*{and now}* He summarily propounds here, what he particularly declares at large in the Chapters following.

*{will I return}* To the King of Persia, from whom I came, ver. 13. this ambassage being performed.

*{to fight}* To resist Cambyses, and his plots and proceedings against the Jews; to ruin him, and that whole empire, by degrees.

*{and when I am gone forth}* Thence, from Persia; to call and consume the Prince of Graecia.

*{Lo,}* A note of attention, and admiration, to that which follows.

*{the Prince of Grecia shall come}* A wonder indeed, that Alexander the Great with so small an army, should come against that last mighty Persian monarch Darius Codomannus, and gain his empire. The Papists that understand here by this *Prince of Grecia,* an *Angel,* either good, or bad, may please themselves in their own dreams; and fight among themselves about their own fancies.

*{Grecia}* Heb. *Javan.* The style of Grecia; because they were of the posterity of Javan, the fourth son of Japhet, Gen. 10.2. Whence also they were called *Iones*. See Dan. 8.21. Joel 3.6.

**Verse 21.** *{But I will shew thee}* Before I return, ver. 20. This may have principal reference to the Seleucidae, and Lagidae, which followed after Alexander the Great, and upon whom the main body of the vision and prophecy following is spent, Chap. 11. and 12.

*{in the Scripture of truth}* In the decree of God; in his determinate counsel, in his mind and providence: which is likened hereto an authentic writing or record, for the certainty of its truth, to be in its time accomplished: figurative terms. See Psal. 139.16. and 149.9. Mal. 3.16.

*{none that holdeth with me}* Or, *strengtheneth himself with me.* Namely, to help me against them, the enemies of the Church. He speaks of no human help; as the case then stood.

*{but Michael}* Vers. 13. Christ alone stands for his Church against all the world; that neither men nor devils shall prevail against it, Psal. 2. Mat. 16.18. This is the Church's comfort and security.

*{your Prince}* *Prince of the Jews,* Chap. 9.25,26. The King, and head of his Church, and Savior of his body, Eph. 1.22,23. See Josh. 5.14. Ezek. 34.24. Act. 5.31. Apoc. 1.5. and 19.16.

## CHAPTER 11

**Verse 1.** *{Also I}* These are the words, not of Daniel, but of the Angel Gabriel; who here continues his speech, and performs his promise, Chap. 10.14,21. and gives a more particular exposition of the matters of the visions, Chap. 2. and 7. and 8. So that in those visions the same nations are figured thrice over (only Babel, or the Chaldean empire left out in the last, as then at an end) and are expounded thrice; the latter exposition still the clearer: and here last of all, with the greatest clearness of all, are foretold by the Angel, the particular Kings of Persia, and of Grecia more specially, the sundry several passages of their divided kings, and their affairs one against another; as they stood in a relation to the Jews, God's people.

*{in the first year of Darius the Mede}* Chap. 5.31. and 6.28. and 9.1. the first year of the Persian empire.

*{even I stood to confirm, &c.}* In two things chiefly, viz. first to break down the Chaldean empire, so that by the Angel's assistance, the golden head was broken to pieces, Chap. 2. by the petition of the watcher, the ax was laid to the root of the tree, Chap. 4. and the Lion plucked, Chap. 7. and Belshazzar slain, Chap. 5.

Secondly, to confirm, and strengthen Darius and Cyrus (for they reigned together) to issue forth that edict and proclamation, for the return of the Jews out of the Babylonish captivity, into their own land. For, no doubt, but so great a matter, at that time, did admit of great debate. Whether, or no, to dismiss so many captives, to lose the benefit they reaped by them, to incur the danger might follow thereupon; We see how loath the King of Egypt was to let them go out of his land, Exod. Chap. 5, &c. But the Angel here confirms and strengthens Darius in this good resolution to let them go in the midst of all his hesitations and fluctuations of mind, and against all politic suggestions, and oppositions, made by any to the contrary. And thus the Angel shows the great care he had of Daniel's people in their great affairs with these monarchs, Mat. 18.10. Psal. 34.7. and 91.11. Heb. 1.14.

**Verse 2.** *{And now}* Being not only employed under God, in the management and administration of the Persian monarchy, so as may make for the use and good of the Church, but also being sent to you, as aforesaid, Chap. 10. ver. 11.14.

*{will I shew thee the truth}* Chap. 10.21. the propriety of the visions of the image, Ch. 2. and of the beasts Chap. 7. and 8. which then we may not expound, but according to the Angel's commentary: which stands in matters so famous, that men, out of the Church, will bring us stories most exactly agreeing with the Angel's words, That our enemies being judges, this might seem rather a history of things past, than a prophecy of such things to come so many ages after. The histories, now extant that help so much to the clear understanding of this Chapter, are *Herodotus, Polybius: Zonaras, Pausanias, Xenophon, Josephus, Livius, Florus, Appian, Plutarch, Justin, Ptolomaeus, Physcon,* and others. See Esay 41. vers. 22,23.

*{yet three Kings in Persia}* Yet besides this Darius, that was; and this Cyrus, that now is, Chap. 10.1. So is the particle yet used, Gen. 40.12. Jonas 3.4. Some reckon them thus, *Cambyses, Smerdis, Magus,* and *Darius Hystaspis.* Others take, in *Cyrus;* and leave out *Smerdis Magus,* being but a counterfeit, and therefore slain within one year.

*{and the fourth shall be far richer, &c.}* This is *Xerxes,* famously so known, for his riches in heathen stories, Justin. lib. 2. which riches *Cyrus* had got, Esay 45.3. and his Father *Darius* by levying of *tributes and taxes,* Esth. 10.1. whence he was called the *Huckster,* or *Hoarder of the kingdom:* like that exactor of *tributes,* in the 20. verse of this Chapter. Besides his own *impositions* laid upon so many *provinces.*

*{and by his strength, through his riches, he shall stir up, &c.}* This expedition of *Xerxes,* against Grecia, is very famous in heathen histories, in *Herodotus, Justin, Diodorus Siculus, Paulus Orosius, Plutarch,* and others; his armies huge, by land and sea; his pride monstrous; his flight shameful: whence he was called, first, *the terror of Greece;* and after, *the scorn of Greece.* Some imagine, as if this were the best monarch of Persia, and so but four in all: But it is nothing so. There were these after him, *Artaxerxes Longimanus, Darius Nothus, Artaxerxes Mnemon, Darius Ochus, Darius Codomannus.* But these are passed over by the Angel, as not making so much to his present purpose, nor meddling much with the affairs of the Jews, after the Temple and city of Jerusalem were once built.

**Verse 3.** *{And a mighty King}* This was above 100 years after that expedition of Xerxes; and this man was that belly of brass, Chap. 2.32. the body of the Leopard, Chap. 7.6. the notable horn of the he-goat, Chap. 8.5. Alexander the Great; here is an abridgment of his story.

*{that shall rule with great dominion}* Conquered the world in 6 years; was sorry there was not another world to conquer, Chap. 2.39.

*{and do according to his will}* Whatsoever he wished, and attempted; even beyond all human hope and expectation: and upon such successes, would needs be held and reputed a god. See Amos on Chap. 3.1.

**Verse 4.** *{And when he shall stand up}* Monarch 6 or 7 years after he had withstood and subdued the Persians, and their monarchy.

*{his kingdom shall be broken}* Chap. 2.44. and 8.8,21,22. See the *Annotations* there. Of great Alexander's death at Babylon, in the 33 year of his age, 12 of his kingdom, and 6 or 7 of his monarchy; and of the manner of his death; with some small variety therein, heathen histories do plentifully record.

*{and shall be divided}* First his 12 chieftains divided his empire among themselves, as administrators, and governors of it, each of his share.

*{toward the four winds}* Chap. 7.6. four notable ones, towards the four winds of heaven, Chap. 8.8. the rest were soon after reduced to these four kingdoms; of *Antigonus,* in the East; *Cassander* in the West; *Seleucus* in the North; and *Ptolemy* in the South. Some place *Antigonus* in the North, and *Seleucus* in the East.

*{and not to his posterity}* Great *Alexander's* posterity had them not; *Philippus Aridaeus, Alexander's* brother, was soon killed; *Alexander's* two sons, viz. *Alexander* by *Roxane,* and *Hercules* by *Barsine,* the eldest daughter of *Darius Codomannus,* were both poisoned by *Cassander,* leaving no issue; and *Olympias Alexander's* mother, killed by him. So his posterity was rooted out.

*{nor according to his dominion}* None of these four shall be anywise able to be compared in greatness and strength to *Alexander's* power and empire. They were far inferior, Chap. 8.22.

*{plucked up}* Rooted up, root and branch.

*{besides those}* Of great *Alexander's* posterity: None of them should remain to have any part of it.

**Verse 5.** *{And the King of the south}* Of the four kingdoms, that of the East, and that of the West, are hereafter no more mentioned, being the two lesser kingdoms though not reduced to the other two, and no way concerning or meddling with the people of the Jews, God's, and Daniel's people. But the other two, the kings of the North and of the South, are much mentioned, and their armies, victories, and exploits, one against another. Because the Jews and their land lay betwixt them two, and suffered very much of the one, and of the other, being challenged, conquered, and possessed, sometimes by the one, and sometimes by the other; and so torn and rent by this fourth beast which had teeth of iron, and nails of brass, devouring and breaking in pieces, and stamping the residue with his feet, Chap. 7.19. And indeed these of the North were, of all, the most cruel enemies, that very Gog and Magog, spoken of, Ezek. Chap. 38. and 39. The races of these Kings, were called, the Seleucidae in the North, from Seleucus Nicanor, or Nicanus the Conqueror, the first of them; and the Lagidae, in the South, from Ptolemy the son of Lagus, the first of that race: both of them being great Alexander's captains. The particulars concerning both races, which follow in this prophetical Chapter, though dark to the vulgar sort, not versed in the human, or heathen histories of these times, in which only the accomplishment of those prophecies is to be found, the sacred Scripture of the Old Testament ending before these times began; yet they are so generally agreed upon by all learned interpreters, old and new, of the Roman, and of the Reformed Church, as may yield thereby somewhat the better satisfaction to the common reader in this behalf. For his clearer light, we shall set down here in the beginning, the catalogue and succession of these Kings, thus; The Kings of the South, first Ptolomeus the son of Lagus, 2 Ptolomeus Philadelphus, 3 Ptolomeus Evergetes, 4 Ptolomeus Philopator, 5 Ptolomeus Epiphanes, 6 Ptolomeus Philometor. The Kings of the North, 1 Seleucus Nicanor, 2 Antiochus Soter, 3 Antiochus Theos, 4 Seleucus Callinicus, 5 Seleucus Ceraunus, 6 Antiochus Magnus, 7 Seleucus Philopator, or Soter, 8 Antiochus Epiphanes. Thus far this prophecy of the Angel to Daniel does go. And Hierome, that learned Father, is much to be regarded and followed, in his exposition of this prophecy, who had the help of divers historians, who write of these times: the names only of which authors, are left to us at this day. See the Preface of this Commentaries upon Daniel.

*{King of the South}* Ptolomeus the son of Lagus.

*{South}* Egypt, lying South from Jewry, named ver. 8,42,43.

*{shall be strong}* The Angel regards his strength, principally, how it touches the Jews: And so it fell out; For Ptolemy soon after he settled Egypt, came into Judea, and took Jerusalem on a sabbath day, pretending friendship, and not hostility, and carried very many captives into Egypt; as Josephus records in his Antiquities, b. 12. c. 1. He was the fifth horn, c. 7.7,20,24.

*{and one of his Princes}* Not of Ptolemy's Princes, though some learned men would have it so; but of great Alexander's: not Ptolemy Philadelphus, or Philopator; but Seleucus Nicanor, the first of the Seleucidae; and not his grandchild Antiochus Theos, as some would. The whole tenor and sequence of the speech will have it meant of a several kingdom. And the Angel speaking to Daniel, knew that he would help himself by the matter, in all doubtful terms. And he was to speak somewhat darkly for the Jews' safety. And this Seleucus Nicanor is the second horn.

*{strong above him}* This Seleucus was, at last, much stronger than the King of the South, even touching Judea. For though Ptolemy won it from Laomedon; and after recovered it from Antigonus; yet in the last conquest over Antigonus, it was agreed upon, that Seleucus should hold Syria and Judea; as his house pleaded in Polyb. book 5.

*{a great dominion}* Seleucus Nicanor; was the greatest, at last of all Alexander's successors. Some reckon that he had seventy-two kingdoms under him.

**Verse 6.** *{And the end of years}* About 70 after Alexander's death.

*{they}* The Seleucidae and Lagidae; Antiochus Theos, the son of Antiochus Soter, and grandchild of Seleucus Nicanor; and Ptolemeus Philadelphus, the son of Ptolomeus Lagides. This Ptolomeus Philadelphus was the third horn; potent king; a great lover of learning; the founder of that wonderful library, wherein were 200000 books: and among them the seventy translation, which he procured to be made.

*{shall join themselves together}* By marriage: Antiochus Theos, taking to wife Bernice, or Berenice, the daughter of Ptolomeus Philadelphus: so prophesied, Chap. 2.43.

*{to make an agreement}* Heb. *rights.* To keep the peace, and to end all differences, by which they were ready to go to war. This peace between the two kingdoms, was the main end of the marriage.

*{but she shall not retain the power of the arm}* Berenice, shall not be able to make the kingdoms cleave one to another, Chap. 2.43. But that shortly they shall come to open war.

*{neither shall he stand, nor his arm}* The King of the North, Antiochus Theos, who is the fourth horn, shall not stand true to himself, and his wife Berenice; but shall fall off, and lose his life soon after.

*{but she shall be given up}* Berenice shall lose her life.

*{and they that brought her}* Her friends and followers, and companions, that accompanied her out of Egypt, to her husband, and were her consorts, and attendants there in a strange land; they shall partake of her lot, and be wrapped in the same condition.

*{and he that begat her}* Or, *he whom shee brought forth.* Indeed both her father Ptolomeus Philadelphus died shortly; and also her son, by Antiochus Theos lost his life likewise.

*{and he that strengthened her in these times}* Every one that was her stay and support; some states about her. The histories do declare, that all these things came to pass, thus; Antiochus Theos, to embrace this marriage with Berenice, for the peace of the kingdoms, puts away his lawful wife Laodice, by whom he had two sons, Seleucus Callinicus, and another Antiochus. After some time, Antiochus Theos takes again to his first wife, Laodice; she seeing war preparing against him for it; and fearing the inconstancy of her husband, in time to come, uses then the means to poison her husband Antiochus Theos; to set up her son Callinicus to be King; and to slay Berenice and her son, and her followers and aiders.

**Verse 7.** *{But out of a branch of her roots}* Out of the stock and roots, that Berenice sprang, that is, out of the same *parents.*

*{shall one stand up in his estate}* Even her own brother, Ptolemy Evergetes, King after the decease of his Father Ptolomeus Philadelphus, being the fifth horn; He shall stand up in his estate, to revenge his sister's death, against Seleucus Callinicus.

*{of the King of the North}* Seleucus Callinicus.

*{and shall prevail}* Evergetes against Callinicus. The Trent text has here, *abutetur eis;* very abusively.

**Verse 8.** *{captives into Egypt}* Evergetes shall spoil Syria, Cilicia, almost all Asia, and the whole kingdom of Seleucus Callinicus; and shall carry the whole spoil of it into Egypt.

*{Egypt}* The name of Egypt, in a story of Alexander's successors, must needs cut off all controversy, touching the king of the North, and Magog; that none but the Seleucidae can be the men. And in a word, all the several passages of this Chapter, which do follow, are so clear in themselves, touching Antiochus Epiphanes, the Seleucidae and Lagidae; that a wonder it is, that any should dream of bringing in the Romans, and the division of it, into the Greek and Latin empire; or the Turk, or Antichrist to be meant, or minded here in these stories, which are the last and clearest expositions of the image, his legs and feet; and of the beasts, as was said on ver. 1,2.

*{their gods}* As many times heathen conquerors used to do. See 1 Sam. 5.1. 2 Chron. 28.23. Esay 46.1. among which were those which Cambyses, and others, had formerly carried away out of Egypt. Hieron says, out of authors then extant, in number, all were 2500. And that hence the Egyptians, being a nation given to idolatry, gave him the name of Evergetes, that is, gracious Lord; because of this bringing after, a long time, their gods back again.

*{with their Princes}* For hostages, and pledges of their loyalty and obedience. The Hebrew word, unvoweled, is either, *idols*, or else *high states*. That makes translators differ. And the allusion may serve to show, that idolatrous Princes, and their idols, are of much like esteem in God's account.

*{precious vessels}* Heb. *vessels of desire.* See Chap. 9.23.

*{of silver and gold}* Hierom says, 40000 talents of silver. See Dan. 1. ver. 2.

*{continue more years}* Either being secured against Callinicus; or more powerful than Callinicus. Some say, that Seleucus Callinicus was sooner killed, than Evergetes died.

**Verse 9.** *{so the King of the South}* Ptolomeus Evergetes.

*{shall come into his kingdom}* Shall return into his own kingdom of Egypt; as is expounded in the next words. Or, as others, shall come into the kingdom of Seleucus Ceraunus, into Syria, and shall cause himself to be crowned there, which, as King, he shall hold for many years.

*{and shall return into his own land}* Quietly and securely, without any let or fear of Seleucus Callinicus. A like phrase is used, Deut. 31.2. 1 Sam. 18.16. *Justin.* Book 27. records, that Evergetes returned into Egypt, upon occasion of sedition raised there; and that otherwise he had fully ousted Callinicus out of all Syria, if not his whole kingdom.

**Verse 10.** *{But his sons}* The sons of Seleucus Callinicus, namely, Seleucus Ceraunus, and Antiochus, surnamed afterwards the great.

*{shall be stirred up}* Or, *shall move war:* against Evergetes, to recover Syria, and what was taken by him from their father. In which expedition, as histories do record, they prevailed not, but had the worse, and Seleucus Ceraunus was slain or poisoned, in the second or third year of his reign. About which time Ptolomeus Evergetes died also; as Polybius writes, in his second and fourth books; and Apian Alexandrinus.

*{and one shall certainly come and overflow}* Ceraunus being dead, Antiochus Magnus succeeds; which makes the Angel, so soon change from the plural number, to the singular. This Antiochus, the sixth horn, with a sudden force, like unto an overflowing stream, shall overrun Syria and Judea, with great power, and frequent incursion, shall again get a great part of his kingdom. See Esay 8.8. the like in Sennacherib.

*{and pass through}* Even unto Raphiah, on the bounds of Egypt.

*{then shall he return}* Into his regained possessions. Or, the year following, say some, with his fresh and recruited army.

*{and be stirred up}* To war against Ptolomeus Philopator, the son of Evergetes, the seventh horn; called, by some, Eupator; but most generally Philopator, that is, a lover of his father; as some say, by the contrary, because he killed his parents.
